</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pv_escape</dt>
<dd> <p> Escapes at most the first <code>&quot;count&quot;</code> chars of <code>&quot;pv&quot;</code> and puts the results into <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code> such that the size of the escaped string will not exceed <code>&quot;max&quot;</code> chars and will not contain any incomplete escape sequences. The number of bytes escaped will be returned in the <code>&quot;STRLEN *escaped&quot;</code> parameter if it is not null. When the <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code> parameter is null no escaping actually occurs, but the number of bytes that would be escaped were it not null will be calculated. </p>
<p>If flags contains <code>&quot;PERL_PV_ESCAPE_QUOTE&quot;</code> then any double quotes in the string will also be escaped. </p>
<p>Normally the SV will be cleared before the escaped string is prepared, but when <code>&quot;PERL_PV_ESCAPE_NOCLEAR&quot;</code> is set this will not occur. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;PERL_PV_ESCAPE_UNI&quot;</code> is set then the input string is treated as UTF-8 if <code>&quot;PERL_PV_ESCAPE_UNI_DETECT&quot;</code> is set then the input string is scanned using <code>&quot;is_utf8_string()&quot;</code> to determine if it is UTF-8. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;PERL_PV_ESCAPE_ALL&quot;</code> is set then all input chars will be output using <code>&quot;\x01F1&quot;</code> style escapes, otherwise if <code>&quot;PERL_PV_ESCAPE_NONASCII&quot;</code> is set, only non-ASCII chars will be escaped using this style; otherwise, only chars above 255 will be so escaped; other non printable chars will use octal or common escaped patterns like <code>&quot;\n&quot;</code>. Otherwise, if <code>&quot;PERL_PV_ESCAPE_NOBACKSLASH&quot;</code> then all chars below 255 will be treated as printable and will be output as literals. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;PERL_PV_ESCAPE_FIRSTCHAR&quot;</code> is set then only the first char of the string will be escaped, regardless of max. If the output is to be in hex, then it will be returned as a plain hex sequence. Thus the output will either be a single char, an octal escape sequence, a special escape like <code>&quot;\n&quot;</code> or a hex value. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;PERL_PV_ESCAPE_RE&quot;</code> is set then the escape char used will be a <code>&quot;%&quot;</code> and not a <code>&quot;\\&quot;</code>. This is because regexes very often contain backslashed sequences, whereas <code>&quot;%&quot;</code> is not a particularly common character in patterns. </p>
<p>Returns a pointer to the escaped text as held by <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        char* pv_escape(SV *dsv, char const * const str,
                          const STRLEN count, const STRLEN max,
                          STRLEN * const escaped,
                          const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pv_pretty</dt>
<dd> <p> Converts a string into something presentable, handling escaping via <code>&quot;pv_escape()&quot;</code> and supporting quoting and ellipses. </p>
<p>If the <code>&quot;PERL_PV_PRETTY_QUOTE&quot;</code> flag is set then the result will be double quoted with any double quotes in the string escaped. Otherwise if the <code>&quot;PERL_PV_PRETTY_LTGT&quot;</code> flag is set then the result be wrapped in angle brackets. </p>
<p>If the <code>&quot;PERL_PV_PRETTY_ELLIPSES&quot;</code> flag is set and not all characters in string were output then an ellipsis <code>&quot;...&quot;</code> will be appended to the string. Note that this happens AFTER it has been quoted. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;start_color&quot;</code> is non-null then it will be inserted after the opening quote (if there is one) but before the escaped text. If <code>&quot;end_color&quot;</code> is non-null then it will be inserted after the escaped text but before any quotes or ellipses. </p>
<p>Returns a pointer to the prettified text as held by <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        char* pv_pretty(SV *dsv, char const * const str,
                          const STRLEN count, const STRLEN max,
                          char const * const start_color,
                          char const * const end_color,
                          const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Embedding Functions</h2>
<dl>
<dt>cv_clone</dt>
<dd> <p> Clone a CV, making a lexical closure. <code>&quot;proto&quot;</code> supplies the prototype of the function: its code, pad structure, and other attributes. The prototype is combined with a capture of outer lexicals to which the code refers, which are taken from the currently-executing instance of the immediately surrounding code. </p>
<pre><code>        CV * cv_clone(CV *proto)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>cv_name</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns an SV containing the name of the CV, mainly for use in error reporting. The CV may actually be a GV instead, in which case the returned SV holds the GV&#8216;s name. Anything other than a GV or CV is treated as a string already holding the sub name, but this could change in the future. </p>
<p>An SV may be passed as a second argument. If so, the name will be assigned to it and it will be returned. Otherwise the returned SV will be a new mortal. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;CV_NAME_NOTQUAL&quot;</code> bit set, then the package name will not be included. If the first argument is neither a CV nor a GV, this flag is ignored (subject to change). </p>
<pre><code>        SV * cv_name(CV *cv, SV *sv, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>cv_undef</dt>
<dd> <p> Clear out all the active components of a CV. This can happen either by an explicit <code>&quot;undef &amp;foo&quot;</code>, or by the reference count going to zero. In the former case, we keep the <code>&quot;CvOUTSIDE&quot;</code> pointer, so that any anonymous children can still follow the full lexical scope chain. </p>
<pre><code>        void cv_undef(CV* cv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>find_rundefsv</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the global variable <code>$_</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV * find_rundefsv()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>find_rundefsvoffset</dt>
<dd> <p> DEPRECATED! It is planned to remove this function from a future release of Perl. Do not use it for new code; remove it from existing code. </p>
<p>Until the lexical <code>$_</code> feature was removed, this function would find the position of the lexical <code>$_</code> in the pad of the currently-executing function and return the offset in the current pad, or <code>&quot;NOT_IN_PAD&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Now it always returns <code>&quot;NOT_IN_PAD&quot;</code>. </p>
<p> NOTE: the perl_ form of this function is deprecated. </p>
<pre><code>        PADOFFSET find_rundefsvoffset()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>intro_my</dt>
<dd> <p> ``Introduce&#8217;&#8216; <code>&quot;my&quot;</code> variables to visible status. This is called during parsing at the end of each statement to make lexical variables visible to subsequent statements. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 intro_my()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>load_module</dt>
<dd> <p> Loads the module whose name is pointed to by the string part of <code>&quot;name&quot;</code>. Note that the actual module name, not its filename, should be given. Eg, ``Foo::Bar&#8217;&#8216; instead of ``Foo/Bar.pm&#8217;&#8216;. ver, if specified and not NULL, provides version semantics similar to <code>&quot;use Foo::Bar VERSION&quot;</code>. The optional trailing arguments can be used to specify arguments to the module&#8217;s <code>&quot;import()&quot;</code> method, similar to <code>&quot;use Foo::Bar VERSION LIST&quot;</code>; their precise handling depends on the flags. The flags argument is a bitwise-ORed collection of any of <code>&quot;PERL_LOADMOD_DENY&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;PERL_LOADMOD_NOIMPORT&quot;</code>, or <code>&quot;PERL_LOADMOD_IMPORT_OPS&quot;</code> (or 0 for no flags). </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;PERL_LOADMOD_NOIMPORT&quot;</code> is set, the module is loaded as if with an empty import list, as in <code>&quot;use Foo::Bar ()&quot;</code>; this is the only circumstance in which the trailing optional arguments may be omitted entirely. Otherwise, if <code>&quot;PERL_LOADMOD_IMPORT_OPS&quot;</code> is set, the trailing arguments must consist of exactly one <code>&quot;OP*&quot;</code>, containing the op tree that produces the relevant import arguments. Otherwise, the trailing arguments must all be <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code> values that will be used as import arguments; and the list must be terminated with <code>&quot;(SV*) NULL&quot;</code>. If neither <code>&quot;PERL_LOADMOD_NOIMPORT&quot;</code> nor <code>&quot;PERL_LOADMOD_IMPORT_OPS&quot;</code> is set, the trailing <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> pointer is needed even if no import arguments are desired. The reference count for each specified <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code> argument is decremented. In addition, the <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> argument is modified. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;PERL_LOADMOD_DENY&quot;</code> is set, the module is loaded as if with <code>&quot;no&quot;</code> rather than <code>&quot;use&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void load_module(U32 flags, SV* name, SV* ver, ...)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newPADNAMELIST</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Creates a new pad name list. <code>&quot;max&quot;</code> is the highest index for which space is allocated. </p>
<pre><code>        PADNAMELIST * newPADNAMELIST(size_t max)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newPADNAMEouter</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Constructs and returns a new pad name. Only use this function for names that refer to outer lexicals. (See also ``newPADNAMEpvn&#8217;&#8216;.) <code>&quot;outer&quot;</code> is the outer pad name that this one mirrors. The returned pad name has the <code>&quot;PADNAMEt_OUTER&quot;</code> flag already set. </p>
<pre><code>        PADNAME * newPADNAMEouter(PADNAME *outer)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newPADNAMEpvn</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Constructs and returns a new pad name. <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> must be a UTF-8 string. Do not use this for pad names that point to outer lexicals. See <code>&quot;newPADNAMEouter&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        PADNAME * newPADNAMEpvn(const char *s, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>nothreadhook</dt>
<dd> <p> Stub that provides thread hook for perl_destruct when there are no threads. </p>
<pre><code>        int nothreadhook()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_add_anon</dt>
<dd> <p> Allocates a place in the currently-compiling pad (via ``pad_alloc&#8217;&#8216;) for an anonymous function that is lexically scoped inside the currently-compiling function. The function <code>&quot;func&quot;</code> is linked into the pad, and its <code>&quot;CvOUTSIDE&quot;</code> link to the outer scope is weakened to avoid a reference loop. </p>
<p>One reference count is stolen, so you may need to do <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc(func)&quot;</code>. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;optype&quot;</code> should be an opcode indicating the type of operation that the pad entry is to support. This doesn&#8217;t affect operational semantics, but is used for debugging. </p>
<pre><code>        PADOFFSET pad_add_anon(CV *func, I32 optype)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_add_name_pv</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``pad_add_name_pvn&#8217;&#8216;, but takes a nul-terminated string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        PADOFFSET pad_add_name_pv(const char *name, U32 flags,
                                  HV *typestash, HV *ourstash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_add_name_pvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Allocates a place in the currently-compiling pad for a named lexical variable. Stores the name and other metadata in the name part of the pad, and makes preparations to manage the variable&#8217;s lexical scoping. Returns the offset of the allocated pad slot. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;namepv&quot;</code>/<code>&quot;namelen&quot;</code> specify the variable&#8217;s name, including leading sigil. If <code>&quot;typestash&quot;</code> is non-null, the name is for a typed lexical, and this identifies the type. If <code>&quot;ourstash&quot;</code> is non-null, it&#8217;s a lexical reference to a package variable, and this identifies the package. The following flags can be OR&#8216;ed together: </p>
<pre><code> padadd_OUR redundantly specifies if it's a package var
 padadd_STATE variable will retain value persistently
 padadd_NO_DUP_CHECK skip check for lexical shadowing
        PADOFFSET pad_add_name_pvn(const char *namepv,
                                   STRLEN namelen, U32 flags,
                                   HV *typestash, HV *ourstash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_add_name_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``pad_add_name_pvn&#8217;&#8216;, but takes the name string in the form of an SV instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        PADOFFSET pad_add_name_sv(SV *name, U32 flags,
                                  HV *typestash, HV *ourstash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_alloc</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Allocates a place in the currently-compiling pad, returning the offset of the allocated pad slot. No name is initially attached to the pad slot. <code>&quot;tmptype&quot;</code> is a set of flags indicating the kind of pad entry required, which will be set in the value SV for the allocated pad entry: </p>
<pre><code>    SVs_PADMY named lexical variable (&quot;my&quot;, &quot;our&quot;, &quot;state&quot;)
    SVs_PADTMP unnamed temporary store
    SVf_READONLY constant shared between recursion levels
</code></pre>
<p> <code>&quot;SVf_READONLY&quot;</code> has been supported here only since perl 5.20. To work with earlier versions as well, use <code>&quot;SVf_READONLY|SVs_PADTMP&quot;</code>. <code>&quot;SVf_READONLY&quot;</code> does not cause the SV in the pad slot to be marked read-only, but simply tells <code>&quot;pad_alloc&quot;</code> that it <i>will</i> be made read-only (by the caller), or at least should be treated as such. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;optype&quot;</code> should be an opcode indicating the type of operation that the pad entry is to support. This doesn&#8217;t affect operational semantics, but is used for debugging. </p>
<pre><code>        PADOFFSET pad_alloc(I32 optype, U32 tmptype)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_findmy_pv</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``pad_findmy_pvn&#8217;&#8216;, but takes a nul-terminated string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        PADOFFSET pad_findmy_pv(const char *name, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_findmy_pvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Given the name of a lexical variable, find its position in the currently-compiling pad. <code>&quot;namepv&quot;</code>/<code>&quot;namelen&quot;</code> specify the variable&#8217;s name, including leading sigil. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is reserved and must be zero. If it is not in the current pad but appears in the pad of any lexically enclosing scope, then a pseudo-entry for it is added in the current pad. Returns the offset in the current pad, or <code>&quot;NOT_IN_PAD&quot;</code> if no such lexical is in scope. </p>
<pre><code>        PADOFFSET pad_findmy_pvn(const char *namepv,
                                 STRLEN namelen, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_findmy_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``pad_findmy_pvn&#8217;&#8216;, but takes the name string in the form of an SV instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        PADOFFSET pad_findmy_sv(SV *name, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>padnamelist_fetch</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Fetches the pad name from the given index. </p>
<pre><code>        PADNAME * padnamelist_fetch(PADNAMELIST *pnl,
                                    SSize_t key)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>padnamelist_store</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Stores the pad name (which may be null) at the given index, freeing any existing pad name in that slot. </p>
<pre><code>        PADNAME ** padnamelist_store(PADNAMELIST *pnl,
                                     SSize_t key, PADNAME *val)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_setsv</dt>
<dd> <p> Set the value at offset <code>&quot;po&quot;</code> in the current (compiling or executing) pad. Use the macro <code>&quot;PAD_SETSV()&quot;</code> rather than calling this function directly. </p>
<pre><code>        void pad_setsv(PADOFFSET po, SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> Get the value at offset <code>&quot;po&quot;</code> in the current (compiling or executing) pad. Use macro <code>&quot;PAD_SV&quot;</code> instead of calling this function directly. </p>
<pre><code>        SV * pad_sv(PADOFFSET po)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_tidy</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Tidy up a pad at the end of compilation of the code to which it belongs. Jobs performed here are: remove most stuff from the pads of anonsub prototypes; give it a <code>@_</code>; mark temporaries as such. <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> indicates the kind of subroutine: </p>
<pre><code>    padtidy_SUB ordinary subroutine
    padtidy_SUBCLONE prototype for lexical closure
    padtidy_FORMAT format
        void pad_tidy(padtidy_type type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>perl_alloc</dt>
<dd> <p> Allocates a new Perl interpreter. See perlembed. </p>
<pre><code>        PerlInterpreter* perl_alloc()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>perl_construct</dt>
<dd> <p> Initializes a new Perl interpreter. See perlembed. </p>
<pre><code>        void perl_construct(PerlInterpreter *my_perl)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>perl_destruct</dt>
<dd> <p> Shuts down a Perl interpreter. See perlembed for a tutorial. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;my_perl&quot;</code> points to the Perl interpreter. It must have been previously created through the use of ``perl_alloc&#8217;&#8216; and ``perl_construct&#8217;&#8216;. It may have been initialised through ``perl_parse&#8217;&#8216;, and may have been used through ``perl_run&#8217;&#8216; and other means. This function should be called for any Perl interpreter that has been constructed with ``perl_construct&#8217;&#8216;, even if subsequent operations on it failed, for example if ``perl_parse&#8217;&#8216; returned a non-zero value. </p>
<p>If the interpreter&#8217;s <code>&quot;PL_exit_flags&quot;</code> word has the <code>&quot;PERL_EXIT_DESTRUCT_END&quot;</code> flag set, then this function will execute code in <code>&quot;END&quot;</code> blocks before performing the rest of destruction. If it is desired to make any use of the interpreter between ``perl_parse&#8217;&#8216; and ``perl_destruct&#8217;&#8216; other than just calling ``perl_run&#8217;&#8216;, then this flag should be set early on. This matters if ``perl_run&#8217;&#8216; will not be called, or if anything else will be done in addition to calling ``perl_run&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>Returns a value be a suitable value to pass to the C library function <code>&quot;exit&quot;</code> (or to return from <code>&quot;main&quot;</code>), to serve as an exit code indicating the nature of the way the interpreter terminated. This takes into account any failure of ``perl_parse&#8217;&#8216; and any early exit from ``perl_run&#8217;&#8216;. The exit code is of the type required by the host operating system, so because of differing exit code conventions it is not portable to interpret specific numeric values as having specific meanings. </p>
<pre><code>        int perl_destruct(PerlInterpreter *my_perl)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>perl_free</dt>
<dd> <p> Releases a Perl interpreter. See perlembed. </p>
<pre><code>        void perl_free(PerlInterpreter *my_perl)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>perl_parse</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells a Perl interpreter to parse a Perl script. This performs most of the initialisation of a Perl interpreter. See perlembed for a tutorial. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;my_perl&quot;</code> points to the Perl interpreter that is to parse the script. It must have been previously created through the use of ``perl_alloc&#8217;&#8216; and ``perl_construct&#8217;&#8216;. <code>&quot;xsinit&quot;</code> points to a callback function that will be called to set up the ability for this Perl interpreter to load XS extensions, or may be null to perform no such setup. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;argc&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;argv&quot;</code> supply a set of command-line arguments to the Perl interpreter, as would normally be passed to the <code>&quot;main&quot;</code> function of a C program. <code>&quot;argv[argc]&quot;</code> must be null. These arguments are where the script to parse is specified, either by naming a script file or by providing a script in a <code>&quot;-e&quot;</code> option. If <code>$0</code> will be written to in the Perl interpreter, then the argument strings must be in writable memory, and so mustn&#8217;t just be string constants. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;env&quot;</code> specifies a set of environment variables that will be used by this Perl interpreter. If non-null, it must point to a null-terminated array of environment strings. If null, the Perl interpreter will use the environment supplied by the <code>&quot;environ&quot;</code> global variable. </p>
<p>This function initialises the interpreter, and parses and compiles the script specified by the command-line arguments. This includes executing code in <code>&quot;BEGIN&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;UNITCHECK&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;CHECK&quot;</code> blocks. It does not execute <code>&quot;INIT&quot;</code> blocks or the main program. </p>
<p>Returns an integer of slightly tricky interpretation. The correct use of the return value is as a truth value indicating whether there was a failure in initialisation. If zero is returned, this indicates that initialisation was successful, and it is safe to proceed to call ``perl_run&#8217;&#8216; and make other use of it. If a non-zero value is returned, this indicates some problem that means the interpreter wants to terminate. The interpreter should not be just abandoned upon such failure; the caller should proceed to shut the interpreter down cleanly with ``perl_destruct&#8217;&#8216; and free it with ``perl_free&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>For historical reasons, the non-zero return value also attempts to be a suitable value to pass to the C library function <code>&quot;exit&quot;</code> (or to return from <code>&quot;main&quot;</code>), to serve as an exit code indicating the nature of the way initialisation terminated. However, this isn&#8217;t portable, due to differing exit code conventions. A historical bug is preserved for the time being: if the Perl built-in <code>&quot;exit&quot;</code> is called during this function&#8217;s execution, with a type of exit entailing a zero exit code under the host operating system&#8217;s conventions, then this function returns zero rather than a non-zero value. This bug, [perl #2754], leads to <code>&quot;perl_run&quot;</code> being called (and therefore <code>&quot;INIT&quot;</code> blocks and the main program running) despite a call to <code>&quot;exit&quot;</code>. It has been preserved because a popular module-installing module has come to rely on it and needs time to be fixed. This issue is [perl #132577], and the original bug is due to be fixed in Perl 5.30. </p>
<pre><code>        int perl_parse(PerlInterpreter *my_perl,
                           XSINIT_t xsinit, int argc,
                           char **argv, char **env)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>perl_run</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells a Perl interpreter to run its main program. See perlembed for a tutorial. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;my_perl&quot;</code> points to the Perl interpreter. It must have been previously created through the use of ``perl_alloc&#8217;&#8216; and ``perl_construct&#8217;&#8216;, and initialised through ``perl_parse&#8217;&#8216;. This function should not be called if ``perl_parse&#8217;&#8216; returned a non-zero value, indicating a failure in initialisation or compilation. </p>
<p>This function executes code in <code>&quot;INIT&quot;</code> blocks, and then executes the main program. The code to be executed is that established by the prior call to ``perl_parse&#8217;&#8216;. If the interpreter&#8217;s <code>&quot;PL_exit_flags&quot;</code> word does not have the <code>&quot;PERL_EXIT_DESTRUCT_END&quot;</code> flag set, then this function will also execute code in <code>&quot;END&quot;</code> blocks. If it is desired to make any further use of the interpreter after calling this function, then <code>&quot;END&quot;</code> blocks should be postponed to ``perl_destruct&#8217;&#8216; time by setting that flag. </p>
<p>Returns an integer of slightly tricky interpretation. The correct use of the return value is as a truth value indicating whether the program terminated non-locally. If zero is returned, this indicates that the program ran to completion, and it is safe to make other use of the interpreter (provided that the <code>&quot;PERL_EXIT_DESTRUCT_END&quot;</code> flag was set as described above). If a non-zero value is returned, this indicates that the interpreter wants to terminate early. The interpreter should not be just abandoned because of this desire to terminate; the caller should proceed to shut the interpreter down cleanly with ``perl_destruct&#8217;&#8216; and free it with ``perl_free&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>For historical reasons, the non-zero return value also attempts to be a suitable value to pass to the C library function <code>&quot;exit&quot;</code> (or to return from <code>&quot;main&quot;</code>), to serve as an exit code indicating the nature of the way the program terminated. However, this isn&#8217;t portable, due to differing exit code conventions. An attempt is made to return an exit code of the type required by the host operating system, but because it is constrained to be non-zero, it is not necessarily possible to indicate every type of exit. It is only reliable on Unix, where a zero exit code can be augmented with a set bit that will be ignored. In any case, this function is not the correct place to acquire an exit code: one should get that from ``perl_destruct&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        int perl_run(PerlInterpreter *my_perl)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>require_pv</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells Perl to <code>&quot;require&quot;</code> the file named by the string argument. It is analogous to the Perl code <code>&quot;eval &quot;require &#8216;$file&#8217;&quot;&quot;</code>. It&#8217;s even implemented that way; consider using load_module instead. </p>
<p> NOTE: the perl_ form of this function is deprecated. </p>
<pre><code>        void require_pv(const char* pv)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Exception Handling (simple) Macros</h2>
<dl>
<dt>dXCPT</dt>
<dd> <p> Set up necessary local variables for exception handling. See ``Exception Handling&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts. </p>
<pre><code>                dXCPT;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XCPT_CATCH</dt>
<dd> <p> Introduces a catch block. See ``Exception Handling&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>XCPT_RETHROW</dt>
<dd> <p> Rethrows a previously caught exception. See ``Exception Handling&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts. </p>
<pre><code>                XCPT_RETHROW;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XCPT_TRY_END</dt>
<dd> <p> Ends a try block. See ``Exception Handling&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>XCPT_TRY_START</dt>
<dd> <p> Starts a try block. See ``Exception Handling&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts. </p>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Functions in file pp_sort.c</h2>
<dl>
<dt>sortsv_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> In-place sort an array of SV pointers with the given comparison routine, with various SORTf_* flag options. </p>
<pre><code>        void sortsv_flags(SV** array, size_t num_elts,
                             SVCOMPARE_t cmp, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Functions in file scope.c</h2>
<dl>
<dt>save_gp</dt>
<dd> <p> Saves the current GP of gv on the save stack to be restored on scope exit. </p>
<p>If empty is true, replace the GP with a new GP. </p>
<p>If empty is false, mark gv with GVf_INTRO so the next reference assigned is localized, which is how <code>&quot; local *foo = $someref; &quot;</code> works. </p>
<pre><code>        void save_gp(GV* gv, I32 empty)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Functions in file vutil.c</h2>
<dl>
<dt>new_version</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a new version object based on the passed in SV: </p>
<pre><code>    SV *sv = new_version(SV *ver);
</code></pre>
<p>Does not alter the passed in ver SV. See ``upg_version&#8217;&#8216; if you want to upgrade the SV. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* new_version(SV *ver)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>prescan_version</dt>
<dd> <p> Validate that a given string can be parsed as a version object, but doesn&#8217;t actually perform the parsing. Can use either strict or lax validation rules. Can optionally set a number of hint variables to save the parsing code some time when tokenizing. </p>
<pre><code>        const char* prescan_version(const char *s, bool strict,
                                    const char** errstr,
                                    bool *sqv,
                                    int *ssaw_decimal,
                                    int *swidth, bool *salpha)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>scan_version</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a pointer to the next character after the parsed version string, as well as upgrading the passed in SV to an RV. </p>
<p>Function must be called with an already existing SV like </p>
<pre><code>    sv = newSV(0);
    s = scan_version(s, SV *sv, bool qv);
</code></pre>
<p>Performs some preprocessing to the string to ensure that it has the correct characteristics of a version. Flags the object if it contains an underscore (which denotes this is an alpha version). The boolean qv denotes that the version should be interpreted as if it had multiple decimals, even if it doesn&#8217;t. </p>
<pre><code>        const char* scan_version(const char *s, SV *rv, bool qv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>upg_version</dt>
<dd> <p> In-place upgrade of the supplied SV to a version object. </p>
<pre><code>    SV *sv = upg_version(SV *sv, bool qv);
</code></pre>
<p>Returns a pointer to the upgraded SV. Set the boolean qv if you want to force this SV to be interpreted as an ``extended&#8217;&#8216; version. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* upg_version(SV *ver, bool qv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>vcmp</dt>
<dd> <p> Version object aware cmp. Both operands must already have been converted into version objects. </p>
<pre><code>        int vcmp(SV *lhv, SV *rhv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>vnormal</dt>
<dd> <p> Accepts a version object and returns the normalized string representation. Call like: </p>
<pre><code>    sv = vnormal(rv);
</code></pre>
<p> NOTE: you can pass either the object directly or the SV contained within the RV. </p>
<p>The SV returned has a refcount of 1. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* vnormal(SV *vs)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>vnumify</dt>
<dd> <p> Accepts a version object and returns the normalized floating point representation. Call like: </p>
<pre><code>    sv = vnumify(rv);
</code></pre>
<p> NOTE: you can pass either the object directly or the SV contained within the RV. </p>
<p>The SV returned has a refcount of 1. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* vnumify(SV *vs)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>vstringify</dt>
<dd> <p> In order to maintain maximum compatibility with earlier versions of Perl, this function will return either the floating point notation or the multiple dotted notation, depending on whether the original version contained 1 or more dots, respectively. </p>
<p>The SV returned has a refcount of 1. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* vstringify(SV *vs)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>vverify</dt>
<dd> <p> Validates that the SV contains valid internal structure for a version object. It may be passed either the version object (RV) or the hash itself (HV). If the structure is valid, it returns the HV. If the structure is invalid, it returns NULL. </p>
<pre><code>    SV *hv = vverify(sv);
</code></pre>
<p>Note that it only confirms the bare minimum structure (so as not to get confused by derived classes which may contain additional hash entries): </p>
 </dd>
<dl>
<dd>  </dd>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</dt>
<dd> <p> The SV is an HV or a reference to an HV </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&bull;</dt>
<dd> <p> The hash contains a ``version&#8217;&#8216; key </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&bull;</dt>
<dd> <p> The ``version&#8217;&#8216; key has a reference to an AV as its value </p>
 </dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dd> <pre><code>        SV* vverify(SV *vs)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
</dl>
<h2>Gimme Values</h2>
<dl>
<dt>G_ARRAY</dt>
<dd> <p> Used to indicate list context. See <code>&quot;GIMME_V&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;GIMME&quot;</code> and perlcall. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>G_DISCARD</dt>
<dd> <p> Indicates that arguments returned from a callback should be discarded. See perlcall. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>G_EVAL</dt>
<dd> <p> Used to force a Perl <code>&quot;eval&quot;</code> wrapper around a callback. See perlcall. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>GIMME</dt>
<dd> <p> A backward-compatible version of <code>&quot;GIMME_V&quot;</code> which can only return <code>&quot;G_SCALAR&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;G_ARRAY&quot;</code>; in a void context, it returns <code>&quot;G_SCALAR&quot;</code>. Deprecated. Use <code>&quot;GIMME_V&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 GIMME
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>GIMME_V</dt>
<dd> <p> The XSUB-writer&#8217;s equivalent to Perl&#8217;s <code>&quot;wantarray&quot;</code>. Returns <code>&quot;G_VOID&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;G_SCALAR&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;G_ARRAY&quot;</code> for void, scalar or list context, respectively. See perlcall for a usage example. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 GIMME_V
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>G_NOARGS</dt>
<dd> <p> Indicates that no arguments are being sent to a callback. See perlcall. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>G_SCALAR</dt>
<dd> <p> Used to indicate scalar context. See <code>&quot;GIMME_V&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;GIMME&quot;</code>, and perlcall. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>G_VOID</dt>
<dd> <p> Used to indicate void context. See <code>&quot;GIMME_V&quot;</code> and perlcall. </p>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Global Variables</h2>
<p> These variables are global to an entire process. They are shared between all interpreters and all threads in a process. Any variables not documented here may be changed or removed without notice, so don&#8217;t use them! If you feel you really do need to use an unlisted variable, first send email to perl5-porters [at] perl.org &lt;mailto:perl5-porters [at] perl.org&gt;. It may be that someone there will point out a way to accomplish what you need without using an internal variable. But if not, you should get a go-ahead to document and then use the variable. </p>
<dl>
<dt>PL_check</dt>
<dd> <p> Array, indexed by opcode, of functions that will be called for the ``check&#8217;&#8216; phase of optree building during compilation of Perl code. For most (but not all) types of op, once the op has been initially built and populated with child ops it will be filtered through the check function referenced by the appropriate element of this array. The new op is passed in as the sole argument to the check function, and the check function returns the completed op. The check function may (as the name suggests) check the op for validity and signal errors. It may also initialise or modify parts of the ops, or perform more radical surgery such as adding or removing child ops, or even throw the op away and return a different op in its place. </p>
<p>This array of function pointers is a convenient place to hook into the compilation process. An XS module can put its own custom check function in place of any of the standard ones, to influence the compilation of a particular type of op. However, a custom check function must never fully replace a standard check function (or even a custom check function from another module). A module modifying checking must instead <code>wrap</code> the preexisting check function. A custom check function must be selective about when to apply its custom behaviour. In the usual case where it decides not to do anything special with an op, it must chain the preexisting op function. Check functions are thus linked in a chain, with the core&#8217;s base checker at the end. </p>
<p>For thread safety, modules should not write directly to this array. Instead, use the function ``wrap_op_checker&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_keyword_plugin</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Function pointer, pointing at a function used to handle extended keywords. The function should be declared as </p>
<pre><code>        int keyword_plugin_function(pTHX_
                char *keyword_ptr, STRLEN keyword_len,
                OP **op_ptr)
</code></pre>
<p>The function is called from the tokeniser, whenever a possible keyword is seen. <code>&quot;keyword_ptr&quot;</code> points at the word in the parser&#8217;s input buffer, and <code>&quot;keyword_len&quot;</code> gives its length; it is not null-terminated. The function is expected to examine the word, and possibly other state such as %^H, to decide whether it wants to handle it as an extended keyword. If it does not, the function should return <code>&quot;KEYWORD_PLUGIN_DECLINE&quot;</code>, and the normal parser process will continue. </p>
<p>If the function wants to handle the keyword, it first must parse anything following the keyword that is part of the syntax introduced by the keyword. See ``Lexer interface&#8217;&#8216; for details. </p>
<p>When a keyword is being handled, the plugin function must build a tree of <code>&quot;OP&quot;</code> structures, representing the code that was parsed. The root of the tree must be stored in <code>*op_ptr</code>. The function then returns a constant indicating the syntactic role of the construct that it has parsed: <code>&quot;KEYWORD_PLUGIN_STMT&quot;</code> if it is a complete statement, or <code>&quot;KEYWORD_PLUGIN_EXPR&quot;</code> if it is an expression. Note that a statement construct cannot be used inside an expression (except via <code>&quot;do BLOCK&quot;</code> and similar), and an expression is not a complete statement (it requires at least a terminating semicolon). </p>
<p>When a keyword is handled, the plugin function may also have (compile-time) side effects. It may modify <code>&quot;%^H&quot;</code>, define functions, and so on. Typically, if side effects are the main purpose of a handler, it does not wish to generate any ops to be included in the normal compilation. In this case it is still required to supply an op tree, but it suffices to generate a single null op. </p>
<p>That&#8217;s how the <code>*PL_keyword_plugin</code> function needs to behave overall. Conventionally, however, one does not completely replace the existing handler function. Instead, take a copy of <code>&quot;PL_keyword_plugin&quot;</code> before assigning your own function pointer to it. Your handler function should look for keywords that it is interested in and handle those. Where it is not interested, it should call the saved plugin function, passing on the arguments it received. Thus <code>&quot;PL_keyword_plugin&quot;</code> actually points at a chain of handler functions, all of which have an opportunity to handle keywords, and only the last function in the chain (built into the Perl core) will normally return <code>&quot;KEYWORD_PLUGIN_DECLINE&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>For thread safety, modules should not set this variable directly. Instead, use the function ``wrap_keyword_plugin&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>GV Functions</h2>
<p> A GV is a structure which corresponds to to a Perl typeglob, ie *foo. It is a structure that holds a pointer to a scalar, an array, a hash etc, corresponding to <code>$foo</code>, <code>@foo</code>, <code>%foo</code>. </p>
<p>GVs are usually found as values in stashes (symbol table hashes) where Perl stores its global variables. </p>
<dl>
<dt>GvAV</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the AV from the GV. </p>
<pre><code>        AV* GvAV(GV* gv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_const_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> If <code>&quot;gv&quot;</code> is a typeglob whose subroutine entry is a constant sub eligible for inlining, or <code>&quot;gv&quot;</code> is a placeholder reference that would be promoted to such a typeglob, then returns the value returned by the sub. Otherwise, returns <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* gv_const_sv(GV* gv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>GvCV</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the CV from the GV. </p>
<pre><code>        CV* GvCV(GV* gv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_fetchmeth</dt>
<dd> <p> Like ``gv_fetchmeth_pvn&#8217;&#8216;, but lacks a flags parameter. </p>
<pre><code>        GV* gv_fetchmeth(HV* stash, const char* name,
                             STRLEN len, I32 level)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_fetchmethod_autoload</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the glob which contains the subroutine to call to invoke the method on the <code>&quot;stash&quot;</code>. In fact in the presence of autoloading this may be the glob for ``AUTOLOAD&#8217;&#8216;. In this case the corresponding variable <code>$AUTOLOAD</code> is already setup. </p>
<p>The third parameter of <code>&quot;gv_fetchmethod_autoload&quot;</code> determines whether AUTOLOAD lookup is performed if the given method is not present: non-zero means yes, look for AUTOLOAD; zero means no, don&#8217;t look for AUTOLOAD. Calling <code>&quot;gv_fetchmethod&quot;</code> is equivalent to calling <code>&quot;gv_fetchmethod_autoload&quot;</code> with a non-zero <code>&quot;autoload&quot;</code> parameter. </p>
<p>These functions grant <code>&quot;SUPER&quot;</code> token as a prefix of the method name. Note that if you want to keep the returned glob for a long time, you need to check for it being ``AUTOLOAD&#8217;&#8216;, since at the later time the call may load a different subroutine due to <code>$AUTOLOAD</code> changing its value. Use the glob created as a side effect to do this. </p>
<p>These functions have the same side-effects as <code>&quot;gv_fetchmeth&quot;</code> with <code>&quot;level==0&quot;</code>. The warning against passing the GV returned by <code>&quot;gv_fetchmeth&quot;</code> to <code>&quot;call_sv&quot;</code> applies equally to these functions. </p>
<pre><code>        GV* gv_fetchmethod_autoload(HV* stash,
                                        const char* name,
                                        I32 autoload)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_fetchmeth_autoload</dt>
<dd> <p> This is the old form of ``gv_fetchmeth_pvn_autoload&#8217;&#8216;, which has no flags parameter. </p>
<pre><code>        GV* gv_fetchmeth_autoload(HV* stash,
                                      const char* name,
                                      STRLEN len, I32 level)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_fetchmeth_pv</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``gv_fetchmeth_pvn&#8217;&#8216;, but takes a nul-terminated string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        GV* gv_fetchmeth_pv(HV* stash, const char* name,
                                I32 level, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_fetchmeth_pvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the glob with the given <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> and a defined subroutine or <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>. The glob lives in the given <code>&quot;stash&quot;</code>, or in the stashes accessible via <code>@ISA</code> and <code>&quot;UNIVERSAL::&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>The argument <code>&quot;level&quot;</code> should be either 0 or -1. If <code>&quot;level==0&quot;</code>, as a side-effect creates a glob with the given <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> in the given <code>&quot;stash&quot;</code> which in the case of success contains an alias for the subroutine, and sets up caching info for this glob. </p>
<p>The only significant values for <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> are <code>&quot;GV_SUPER&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SVf_UTF8&quot;</code>. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;GV_SUPER&quot;</code> indicates that we want to look up the method in the superclasses of the <code>&quot;stash&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>The GV returned from <code>&quot;gv_fetchmeth&quot;</code> may be a method cache entry, which is not visible to Perl code. So when calling <code>&quot;call_sv&quot;</code>, you should not use the GV directly; instead, you should use the method&#8217;s CV, which can be obtained from the GV with the <code>&quot;GvCV&quot;</code> macro. </p>
<pre><code>        GV* gv_fetchmeth_pvn(HV* stash, const char* name,
                                 STRLEN len, I32 level,
                                 U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_fetchmeth_pvn_autoload</dt>
<dd> <p> Same as <code>&quot;gv_fetchmeth_pvn()&quot;</code>, but looks for autoloaded subroutines too. Returns a glob for the subroutine. </p>
<p>For an autoloaded subroutine without a GV, will create a GV even if <code>&quot;level &lt; 0&quot;</code>. For an autoloaded subroutine without a stub, <code>&quot;GvCV()&quot;</code> of the result may be zero. </p>
<p>Currently, the only significant value for <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is <code>&quot;SVf_UTF8&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        GV* gv_fetchmeth_pvn_autoload(HV* stash,
                                          const char* name,
                                          STRLEN len, I32 level,
                                          U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_fetchmeth_pv_autoload</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``gv_fetchmeth_pvn_autoload&#8217;&#8216;, but takes a nul-terminated string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        GV* gv_fetchmeth_pv_autoload(HV* stash,
                                         const char* name,
                                         I32 level, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_fetchmeth_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``gv_fetchmeth_pvn&#8217;&#8216;, but takes the name string in the form of an SV instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        GV* gv_fetchmeth_sv(HV* stash, SV* namesv,
                                I32 level, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_fetchmeth_sv_autoload</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``gv_fetchmeth_pvn_autoload&#8217;&#8216;, but takes the name string in the form of an SV instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        GV* gv_fetchmeth_sv_autoload(HV* stash, SV* namesv,
                                         I32 level, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>GvHV</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the HV from the GV. </p>
<pre><code>        HV* GvHV(GV* gv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_init</dt>
<dd> <p> The old form of <code>&quot;gv_init_pvn()&quot;</code>. It does not work with UTF-8 strings, as it has no flags parameter. If the <code>&quot;multi&quot;</code> parameter is set, the <code>&quot;GV_ADDMULTI&quot;</code> flag will be passed to <code>&quot;gv_init_pvn()&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void gv_init(GV* gv, HV* stash, const char* name,
                        STRLEN len, int multi)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_init_pv</dt>
<dd> <p> Same as <code>&quot;gv_init_pvn()&quot;</code>, but takes a nul-terminated string for the name instead of separate char * and length parameters. </p>
<pre><code>        void gv_init_pv(GV* gv, HV* stash, const char* name,
                           U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_init_pvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Converts a scalar into a typeglob. This is an incoercible typeglob; assigning a reference to it will assign to one of its slots, instead of overwriting it as happens with typeglobs created by <code>&quot;SvSetSV&quot;</code>. Converting any scalar that is <code>&quot;SvOK()&quot;</code> may produce unpredictable results and is reserved for perl&#8217;s internal use. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;gv&quot;</code> is the scalar to be converted. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;stash&quot;</code> is the parent stash/package, if any. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> give the name. The name must be unqualified; that is, it must not include the package name. If <code>&quot;gv&quot;</code> is a stash element, it is the caller&#8217;s responsibility to ensure that the name passed to this function matches the name of the element. If it does not match, perl&#8217;s internal bookkeeping will get out of sync. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> can be set to <code>&quot;SVf_UTF8&quot;</code> if <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> is a UTF-8 string, or the return value of SvUTF8(sv). It can also take the <code>&quot;GV_ADDMULTI&quot;</code> flag, which means to pretend that the GV has been seen before (i.e., suppress ``Used once&#8217;&#8216; warnings). </p>
<pre><code>        void gv_init_pvn(GV* gv, HV* stash, const char* name,
                            STRLEN len, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_init_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> Same as <code>&quot;gv_init_pvn()&quot;</code>, but takes an SV * for the name instead of separate char * and length parameters. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is currently unused. </p>
<pre><code>        void gv_init_sv(GV* gv, HV* stash, SV* namesv,
                           U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_stashpv</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a pointer to the stash for a specified package. Uses <code>&quot;strlen&quot;</code> to determine the length of <code>&quot;name&quot;</code>, then calls <code>&quot;gv_stashpvn()&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        HV* gv_stashpv(const char* name, I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_stashpvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a pointer to the stash for a specified package. The <code>&quot;namelen&quot;</code> parameter indicates the length of the <code>&quot;name&quot;</code>, in bytes. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is passed to <code>&quot;gv_fetchpvn_flags()&quot;</code>, so if set to <code>&quot;GV_ADD&quot;</code> then the package will be created if it does not already exist. If the package does not exist and <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is 0 (or any other setting that does not create packages) then <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> is returned. </p>
<p>Flags may be one of: </p>
<pre><code>    GV_ADD
    SVf_UTF8
    GV_NOADD_NOINIT
    GV_NOINIT
    GV_NOEXPAND
    GV_ADDMG
</code></pre>
<p>The most important of which are probably <code>&quot;GV_ADD&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SVf_UTF8&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Note, use of <code>&quot;gv_stashsv&quot;</code> instead of <code>&quot;gv_stashpvn&quot;</code> where possible is strongly recommended for performance reasons. </p>
<pre><code>        HV* gv_stashpvn(const char* name, U32 namelen,
                            I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_stashpvs</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;gv_stashpvn&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        HV* gv_stashpvs(&quot;literal string&quot; name, I32 create)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_stashsv</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a pointer to the stash for a specified package. See <code>&quot;gv_stashpvn&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Note this interface is strongly preferred over <code>&quot;gv_stashpvn&quot;</code> for performance reasons. </p>
<pre><code>        HV* gv_stashsv(SV* sv, I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>GvSV</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the SV from the GV. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* GvSV(GV* gv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>setdefout</dt>
<dd> <p> Sets <code>&quot;PL_defoutgv&quot;</code>, the default file handle for output, to the passed in typeglob. As <code>&quot;PL_defoutgv&quot;</code> ``owns&#8217;&#8216; a reference on its typeglob, the reference count of the passed in typeglob is increased by one, and the reference count of the typeglob that <code>&quot;PL_defoutgv&quot;</code> points to is decreased by one. </p>
<pre><code>        void setdefout(GV* gv)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Handy Values</h2>
<dl>
<dt>Nullav</dt>
<dd> <p> Null AV pointer. </p>
<p>(deprecated &#8211; use <code>&quot;(AV *)NULL&quot;</code> instead) </p>
 </dd>
<dt>Nullch</dt>
<dd> <p> Null character pointer. (No longer available when <code>&quot;PERL_CORE&quot;</code> is defined.) </p>
 </dd>
<dt>Nullcv</dt>
<dd> <p> Null CV pointer. </p>
<p>(deprecated &#8211; use <code>&quot;(CV *)NULL&quot;</code> instead) </p>
 </dd>
<dt>Nullhv</dt>
<dd> <p> Null HV pointer. </p>
<p>(deprecated &#8211; use <code>&quot;(HV *)NULL&quot;</code> instead) </p>
 </dd>
<dt>Nullsv</dt>
<dd> <p> Null SV pointer. (No longer available when <code>&quot;PERL_CORE&quot;</code> is defined.) </p>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Hash Manipulation Functions</h2>
<p> A HV structure represents a Perl hash. It consists mainly of an array of pointers, each of which points to a linked list of HE structures. The array is indexed by the hash function of the key, so each linked list represents all the hash entries with the same hash value. Each HE contains a pointer to the actual value, plus a pointer to a HEK structure which holds the key and hash value. </p>
<dl>
<dt>cop_fetch_label</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Returns the label attached to a cop. The flags pointer may be set to <code>&quot;SVf_UTF8&quot;</code> or 0. </p>
<pre><code>        const char * cop_fetch_label(COP *const cop,
                                     STRLEN *len, U32 *flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>cop_store_label</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Save a label into a <code>&quot;cop_hints_hash&quot;</code>. You need to set flags to <code>&quot;SVf_UTF8&quot;</code> for a UTF-8 label. </p>
<pre><code>        void cop_store_label(COP *const cop,
                                const char *label, STRLEN len,
                                U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>get_hv</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the HV of the specified Perl hash. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> are passed to <code>&quot;gv_fetchpv&quot;</code>. If <code>&quot;GV_ADD&quot;</code> is set and the Perl variable does not exist then it will be created. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is zero and the variable does not exist then <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> is returned. </p>
<p> NOTE: the perl_ form of this function is deprecated. </p>
<pre><code>        HV* get_hv(const char *name, I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HEf_SVKEY</dt>
<dd> <p> This flag, used in the length slot of hash entries and magic structures, specifies the structure contains an <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code> pointer where a <code>&quot;char*&quot;</code> pointer is to be expected. (For information only---not to be used). </p>
 </dd>
<dt>HeHASH</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the computed hash stored in the hash entry. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 HeHASH(HE* he)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HeKEY</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the actual pointer stored in the key slot of the hash entry. The pointer may be either <code>&quot;char*&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code>, depending on the value of <code>&quot;HeKLEN()&quot;</code>. Can be assigned to. The <code>&quot;HePV()&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;HeSVKEY()&quot;</code> macros are usually preferable for finding the value of a key. </p>
<pre><code>        void* HeKEY(HE* he)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HeKLEN</dt>
<dd> <p> If this is negative, and amounts to <code>&quot;HEf_SVKEY&quot;</code>, it indicates the entry holds an <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code> key. Otherwise, holds the actual length of the key. Can be assigned to. The <code>&quot;HePV()&quot;</code> macro is usually preferable for finding key lengths. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN HeKLEN(HE* he)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HePV</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the key slot of the hash entry as a <code>&quot;char*&quot;</code> value, doing any necessary dereferencing of possibly <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code> keys. The length of the string is placed in <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> (this is a macro, so do <i>not</i> use <code>&amp;len</code>). If you do not care about what the length of the key is, you may use the global variable <code>&quot;PL_na&quot;</code>, though this is rather less efficient than using a local variable. Remember though, that hash keys in perl are free to contain embedded nulls, so using <code>&quot;strlen()&quot;</code> or similar is not a good way to find the length of hash keys. This is very similar to the <code>&quot;SvPV()&quot;</code> macro described elsewhere in this document. See also <code>&quot;HeUTF8&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>If you are using <code>&quot;HePV&quot;</code> to get values to pass to <code>&quot;newSVpvn()&quot;</code> to create a new SV, you should consider using <code>&quot;newSVhek(HeKEY_hek(he))&quot;</code> as it is more efficient. </p>
<pre><code>        char* HePV(HE* he, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HeSVKEY</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the key as an <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code>, or <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> if the hash entry does not contain an <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code> key. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* HeSVKEY(HE* he)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HeSVKEY_force</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the key as an <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code>. Will create and return a temporary mortal <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code> if the hash entry contains only a <code>&quot;char*&quot;</code> key. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* HeSVKEY_force(HE* he)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HeSVKEY_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Sets the key to a given <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code>, taking care to set the appropriate flags to indicate the presence of an <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code> key, and returns the same <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* HeSVKEY_set(HE* he, SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HeUTF8</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns whether the <code>&quot;char *&quot;</code> value returned by <code>&quot;HePV&quot;</code> is encoded in UTF-8, doing any necessary dereferencing of possibly <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code> keys. The value returned will be 0 or non-0, not necessarily 1 (or even a value with any low bits set), so <code>do not</code> blindly assign this to a <code>&quot;bool&quot;</code> variable, as <code>&quot;bool&quot;</code> may be a typedef for <code>&quot;char&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 HeUTF8(HE* he)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HeVAL</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the value slot (type <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code>) stored in the hash entry. Can be assigned to. </p>
<pre><code>  SV *foo= HeVAL(hv);
  HeVAL(hv)= sv;
        SV* HeVAL(HE* he)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_assert</dt>
<dd> <p> Check that a hash is in an internally consistent state. </p>
<pre><code>        void hv_assert(HV *hv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_bucket_ratio</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>If the hash is tied dispatches through to the SCALAR tied method, otherwise if the hash contains no keys returns 0, otherwise returns a mortal sv containing a string specifying the number of used buckets, followed by a slash, followed by the number of available buckets. </p>
<p>This function is expensive, it must scan all of the buckets to determine which are used, and the count is NOT cached. In a large hash this could be a lot of buckets. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* hv_bucket_ratio(HV *hv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_clear</dt>
<dd> <p> Frees the all the elements of a hash, leaving it empty. The XS equivalent of <code>&quot;%hash = ()&quot;</code>. See also ``hv_undef&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>See ``av_clear&#8217;&#8216; for a note about the hash possibly being invalid on return. </p>
<pre><code>        void hv_clear(HV *hv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_clear_placeholders</dt>
<dd> <p> Clears any placeholders from a hash. If a restricted hash has any of its keys marked as readonly and the key is subsequently deleted, the key is not actually deleted but is marked by assigning it a value of <code>&amp;PL_sv_placeholder</code>. This tags it so it will be ignored by future operations such as iterating over the hash, but will still allow the hash to have a value reassigned to the key at some future point. This function clears any such placeholder keys from the hash. See <code>&quot;Hash::Util::lock_keys()&quot;</code> for an example of its use. </p>
<pre><code>        void hv_clear_placeholders(HV *hv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_copy_hints_hv</dt>
<dd> <p> A specialised version of ``newHVhv&#8217;&#8216; for copying <code>&quot;%^H&quot;</code>. <code>&quot;ohv&quot;</code> must be a pointer to a hash (which may have <code>&quot;%^H&quot;</code> magic, but should be generally non-magical), or <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> (interpreted as an empty hash). The content of <code>&quot;ohv&quot;</code> is copied to a new hash, which has the <code>&quot;%^H&quot;</code>-specific magic added to it. A pointer to the new hash is returned. </p>
<pre><code>        HV * hv_copy_hints_hv(HV *ohv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_delete</dt>
<dd> <p> Deletes a key/value pair in the hash. The value&#8217;s SV is removed from the hash, made mortal, and returned to the caller. The absolute value of <code>&quot;klen&quot;</code> is the length of the key. If <code>&quot;klen&quot;</code> is negative the key is assumed to be in UTF-8-encoded Unicode. The <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> value will normally be zero; if set to <code>&quot;G_DISCARD&quot;</code> then <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> will be returned. <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> will also be returned if the key is not found. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* hv_delete(HV *hv, const char *key, I32 klen,
                          I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_delete_ent</dt>
<dd> <p> Deletes a key/value pair in the hash. The value SV is removed from the hash, made mortal, and returned to the caller. The <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> value will normally be zero; if set to <code>&quot;G_DISCARD&quot;</code> then <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> will be returned. <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> will also be returned if the key is not found. <code>&quot;hash&quot;</code> can be a valid precomputed hash value, or 0 to ask for it to be computed. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* hv_delete_ent(HV *hv, SV *keysv, I32 flags,
                              U32 hash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HvENAME</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the effective name of a stash, or NULL if there is none. The effective name represents a location in the symbol table where this stash resides. It is updated automatically when packages are aliased or deleted. A stash that is no longer in the symbol table has no effective name. This name is preferable to <code>&quot;HvNAME&quot;</code> for use in MRO linearisations and isa caches. </p>
<pre><code>        char* HvENAME(HV* stash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HvENAMELEN</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the length of the stash&#8217;s effective name. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN HvENAMELEN(HV *stash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HvENAMEUTF8</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns true if the effective name is in UTF-8 encoding. </p>
<pre><code>        unsigned char HvENAMEUTF8(HV *stash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_exists</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the specified hash key exists. The absolute value of <code>&quot;klen&quot;</code> is the length of the key. If <code>&quot;klen&quot;</code> is negative the key is assumed to be in UTF-8-encoded Unicode. </p>
<pre><code>        bool hv_exists(HV *hv, const char *key, I32 klen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_exists_ent</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the specified hash key exists. <code>&quot;hash&quot;</code> can be a valid precomputed hash value, or 0 to ask for it to be computed. </p>
<pre><code>        bool hv_exists_ent(HV *hv, SV *keysv, U32 hash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_fetch</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the SV which corresponds to the specified key in the hash. The absolute value of <code>&quot;klen&quot;</code> is the length of the key. If <code>&quot;klen&quot;</code> is negative the key is assumed to be in UTF-8-encoded Unicode. If <code>&quot;lval&quot;</code> is set then the fetch will be part of a store. This means that if there is no value in the hash associated with the given key, then one is created and a pointer to it is returned. The <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code> it points to can be assigned to. But always check that the return value is non-null before dereferencing it to an <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>See ``Understanding the Magic of Tied Hashes and Arrays&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts for more information on how to use this function on tied hashes. </p>
<pre><code>        SV** hv_fetch(HV *hv, const char *key, I32 klen,
                         I32 lval)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_fetchs</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;hv_fetch&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        SV** hv_fetchs(HV* tb, &quot;literal string&quot; key,
                          I32 lval)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_fetch_ent</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the hash entry which corresponds to the specified key in the hash. <code>&quot;hash&quot;</code> must be a valid precomputed hash number for the given <code>&quot;key&quot;</code>, or 0 if you want the function to compute it. IF <code>&quot;lval&quot;</code> is set then the fetch will be part of a store. Make sure the return value is non-null before accessing it. The return value when <code>&quot;hv&quot;</code> is a tied hash is a pointer to a static location, so be sure to make a copy of the structure if you need to store it somewhere. </p>
<p>See ``Understanding the Magic of Tied Hashes and Arrays&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts for more information on how to use this function on tied hashes. </p>
<pre><code>        HE* hv_fetch_ent(HV *hv, SV *keysv, I32 lval,
                             U32 hash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_fill</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the number of hash buckets that happen to be in use. </p>
<p>This function is wrapped by the macro <code>&quot;HvFILL&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>As of perl 5.25 this function is used only for debugging purposes, and the number of used hash buckets is not in any way cached, thus this function can be costly to execute as it must iterate over all the buckets in the hash. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN hv_fill(HV *const hv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_iterinit</dt>
<dd> <p> Prepares a starting point to traverse a hash table. Returns the number of keys in the hash, including placeholders (i.e. the same as <code>&quot;HvTOTALKEYS(hv)&quot;</code>). The return value is currently only meaningful for hashes without tie magic. </p>
<p> NOTE: Before version 5.004_65, <code>&quot;hv_iterinit&quot;</code> used to return the number of hash buckets that happen to be in use. If you still need that esoteric value, you can get it through the macro <code>&quot;HvFILL(hv)&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 hv_iterinit(HV *hv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_iterkey</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the key from the current position of the hash iterator. See <code>&quot;hv_iterinit&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        char* hv_iterkey(HE* entry, I32* retlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_iterkeysv</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the key as an <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code> from the current position of the hash iterator. The return value will always be a mortal copy of the key. Also see <code>&quot;hv_iterinit&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* hv_iterkeysv(HE* entry)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_iternext</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns entries from a hash iterator. See <code>&quot;hv_iterinit&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>You may call <code>&quot;hv_delete&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;hv_delete_ent&quot;</code> on the hash entry that the iterator currently points to, without losing your place or invalidating your iterator. Note that in this case the current entry is deleted from the hash with your iterator holding the last reference to it. Your iterator is flagged to free the entry on the next call to <code>&quot;hv_iternext&quot;</code>, so you must not discard your iterator immediately else the entry will leak - call <code>&quot;hv_iternext&quot;</code> to trigger the resource deallocation. </p>
<pre><code>        HE* hv_iternext(HV *hv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_iternextsv</dt>
<dd> <p> Performs an <code>&quot;hv_iternext&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;hv_iterkey&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;hv_iterval&quot;</code> in one operation. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* hv_iternextsv(HV *hv, char **key, I32 *retlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_iternext_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Returns entries from a hash iterator. See <code>&quot;hv_iterinit&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;hv_iternext&quot;</code>. The <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> value will normally be zero; if <code>&quot;HV_ITERNEXT_WANTPLACEHOLDERS&quot;</code> is set the placeholders keys (for restricted hashes) will be returned in addition to normal keys. By default placeholders are automatically skipped over. Currently a placeholder is implemented with a value that is <code>&amp;PL_sv_placeholder</code>. Note that the implementation of placeholders and restricted hashes may change, and the implementation currently is insufficiently abstracted for any change to be tidy. </p>
<pre><code>        HE* hv_iternext_flags(HV *hv, I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_iterval</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the value from the current position of the hash iterator. See <code>&quot;hv_iterkey&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* hv_iterval(HV *hv, HE *entry)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_magic</dt>
<dd> <p> Adds magic to a hash. See <code>&quot;sv_magic&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void hv_magic(HV *hv, GV *gv, int how)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HvNAME</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the package name of a stash, or <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> if <code>&quot;stash&quot;</code> isn&#8217;t a stash. See <code>&quot;SvSTASH&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;CvSTASH&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        char* HvNAME(HV* stash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HvNAMELEN</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the length of the stash&#8217;s name. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN HvNAMELEN(HV *stash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>HvNAMEUTF8</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns true if the name is in UTF-8 encoding. </p>
<pre><code>        unsigned char HvNAMEUTF8(HV *stash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_scalar</dt>
<dd> <p> Evaluates the hash in scalar context and returns the result. </p>
<p>When the hash is tied dispatches through to the SCALAR method, otherwise returns a mortal SV containing the number of keys in the hash. </p>
<p>Note, prior to 5.25 this function returned what is now returned by the <code>hv_bucket_ratio()</code> function. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* hv_scalar(HV *hv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_store</dt>
<dd> <p> Stores an SV in a hash. The hash key is specified as <code>&quot;key&quot;</code> and the absolute value of <code>&quot;klen&quot;</code> is the length of the key. If <code>&quot;klen&quot;</code> is negative the key is assumed to be in UTF-8-encoded Unicode. The <code>&quot;hash&quot;</code> parameter is the precomputed hash value; if it is zero then Perl will compute it. </p>
<p>The return value will be <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> if the operation failed or if the value did not need to be actually stored within the hash (as in the case of tied hashes). Otherwise it can be dereferenced to get the original <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code>. Note that the caller is responsible for suitably incrementing the reference count of <code>&quot;val&quot;</code> before the call, and decrementing it if the function returned <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>. Effectively a successful <code>&quot;hv_store&quot;</code> takes ownership of one reference to <code>&quot;val&quot;</code>. This is usually what you want; a newly created SV has a reference count of one, so if all your code does is create SVs then store them in a hash, <code>&quot;hv_store&quot;</code> will own the only reference to the new SV, and your code doesn&#8217;t need to do anything further to tidy up. <code>&quot;hv_store&quot;</code> is not implemented as a call to <code>&quot;hv_store_ent&quot;</code>, and does not create a temporary SV for the key, so if your key data is not already in SV form then use <code>&quot;hv_store&quot;</code> in preference to <code>&quot;hv_store_ent&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>See ``Understanding the Magic of Tied Hashes and Arrays&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts for more information on how to use this function on tied hashes. </p>
<pre><code>        SV** hv_store(HV *hv, const char *key, I32 klen,
                         SV *val, U32 hash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_stores</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;hv_store&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair and omits the hash parameter. </p>
<pre><code>        SV** hv_stores(HV* tb, &quot;literal string&quot; key, SV* val)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_store_ent</dt>
<dd> <p> Stores <code>&quot;val&quot;</code> in a hash. The hash key is specified as <code>&quot;key&quot;</code>. The <code>&quot;hash&quot;</code> parameter is the precomputed hash value; if it is zero then Perl will compute it. The return value is the new hash entry so created. It will be <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> if the operation failed or if the value did not need to be actually stored within the hash (as in the case of tied hashes). Otherwise the contents of the return value can be accessed using the <code>&quot;He?&quot;</code> macros described here. Note that the caller is responsible for suitably incrementing the reference count of <code>&quot;val&quot;</code> before the call, and decrementing it if the function returned NULL. Effectively a successful <code>&quot;hv_store_ent&quot;</code> takes ownership of one reference to <code>&quot;val&quot;</code>. This is usually what you want; a newly created SV has a reference count of one, so if all your code does is create SVs then store them in a hash, <code>&quot;hv_store&quot;</code> will own the only reference to the new SV, and your code doesn&#8217;t need to do anything further to tidy up. Note that <code>&quot;hv_store_ent&quot;</code> only reads the <code>&quot;key&quot;</code>; unlike <code>&quot;val&quot;</code> it does not take ownership of it, so maintaining the correct reference count on <code>&quot;key&quot;</code> is entirely the caller&#8217;s responsibility. The reason it does not take ownership, is that <code>&quot;key&quot;</code> is not used after this function returns, and so can be freed immediately. <code>&quot;hv_store&quot;</code> is not implemented as a call to <code>&quot;hv_store_ent&quot;</code>, and does not create a temporary SV for the key, so if your key data is not already in SV form then use <code>&quot;hv_store&quot;</code> in preference to <code>&quot;hv_store_ent&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>See ``Understanding the Magic of Tied Hashes and Arrays&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts for more information on how to use this function on tied hashes. </p>
<pre><code>        HE* hv_store_ent(HV *hv, SV *key, SV *val, U32 hash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>hv_undef</dt>
<dd> <p> Undefines the hash. The XS equivalent of <code>&quot;undef(%hash)&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>As well as freeing all the elements of the hash (like <code>&quot;hv_clear()&quot;</code>), this also frees any auxiliary data and storage associated with the hash. </p>
<p>See ``av_clear&#8217;&#8216; for a note about the hash possibly being invalid on return. </p>
<pre><code>        void hv_undef(HV *hv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newHV</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new HV. The reference count is set to 1. </p>
<pre><code>        HV* newHV()
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Hook manipulation</h2>
<p> These functions provide convenient and thread-safe means of manipulating hook variables. </p>
<dl>
<dt>wrap_op_checker</dt>
<dd> <p> Puts a C function into the chain of check functions for a specified op type. This is the preferred way to manipulate the ``PL_check&#8217;&#8216; array. <code>&quot;opcode&quot;</code> specifies which type of op is to be affected. <code>&quot;new_checker&quot;</code> is a pointer to the C function that is to be added to that opcode&#8217;s check chain, and <code>&quot;old_checker_p&quot;</code> points to the storage location where a pointer to the next function in the chain will be stored. The value of <code>&quot;new_checker&quot;</code> is written into the ``PL_check&#8217;&#8216; array, while the value previously stored there is written to <code>*old_checker_p</code>. </p>
<p>``PL_check&#8217;&#8216; is global to an entire process, and a module wishing to hook op checking may find itself invoked more than once per process, typically in different threads. To handle that situation, this function is idempotent. The location <code>*old_checker_p</code> must initially (once per process) contain a null pointer. A C variable of static duration (declared at file scope, typically also marked <code>&quot;static&quot;</code> to give it internal linkage) will be implicitly initialised appropriately, if it does not have an explicit initialiser. This function will only actually modify the check chain if it finds <code>*old_checker_p</code> to be null. This function is also thread safe on the small scale. It uses appropriate locking to avoid race conditions in accessing ``PL_check&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>When this function is called, the function referenced by <code>&quot;new_checker&quot;</code> must be ready to be called, except for <code>*old_checker_p</code> being unfilled. In a threading situation, <code>&quot;new_checker&quot;</code> may be called immediately, even before this function has returned. <code>*old_checker_p</code> will always be appropriately set before <code>&quot;new_checker&quot;</code> is called. If <code>&quot;new_checker&quot;</code> decides not to do anything special with an op that it is given (which is the usual case for most uses of op check hooking), it must chain the check function referenced by <code>*old_checker_p</code>. </p>
<p>Taken all together, XS code to hook an op checker should typically look something like this: </p>
<pre><code>    static Perl_check_t nxck_frob;
    static OP *myck_frob(pTHX_ OP *op) {
        ...
        op = nxck_frob(aTHX_ op);
        ...
        return op;
    }
    BOOT:
        wrap_op_checker(OP_FROB, myck_frob, &amp;nxck_frob);
</code></pre>
<p>If you want to influence compilation of calls to a specific subroutine, then use ``cv_set_call_checker_flags&#8217;&#8216; rather than hooking checking of all <code>&quot;entersub&quot;</code> ops. </p>
<pre><code>        void wrap_op_checker(Optype opcode,
                                Perl_check_t new_checker,
                                Perl_check_t *old_checker_p)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Lexer interface</h2>
<p> This is the lower layer of the Perl parser, managing characters and tokens. </p>
<dl>
<dt>lex_bufutf8</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Indicates whether the octets in the lexer buffer (``PL_parser-&gt;linestr&#8217;&#8216;) should be interpreted as the UTF-8 encoding of Unicode characters. If not, they should be interpreted as Latin-1 characters. This is analogous to the <code>&quot;SvUTF8&quot;</code> flag for scalars. </p>
<p>In UTF-8 mode, it is not guaranteed that the lexer buffer actually contains valid UTF-8. Lexing code must be robust in the face of invalid encoding. </p>
<p>The actual <code>&quot;SvUTF8&quot;</code> flag of the ``PL_parser-&gt;linestr&#8217;&#8216; scalar is significant, but not the whole story regarding the input character encoding. Normally, when a file is being read, the scalar contains octets and its <code>&quot;SvUTF8&quot;</code> flag is off, but the octets should be interpreted as UTF-8 if the <code>&quot;use utf8&quot;</code> pragma is in effect. During a string eval, however, the scalar may have the <code>&quot;SvUTF8&quot;</code> flag on, and in this case its octets should be interpreted as UTF-8 unless the <code>&quot;use bytes&quot;</code> pragma is in effect. This logic may change in the future; use this function instead of implementing the logic yourself. </p>
