<pre><code>        bool lex_bufutf8()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_discard_to</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Discards the first part of the ``PL_parser-&gt;linestr&#8217;&#8216; buffer, up to <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code>. The remaining content of the buffer will be moved, and all pointers into the buffer updated appropriately. <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code> must not be later in the buffer than the position of ``PL_parser-&gt;bufptr&#8217;&#8216;: it is not permitted to discard text that has yet to be lexed. </p>
<p>Normally it is not necessarily to do this directly, because it suffices to use the implicit discarding behaviour of ``lex_next_chunk&#8217;&#8216; and things based on it. However, if a token stretches across multiple lines, and the lexing code has kept multiple lines of text in the buffer for that purpose, then after completion of the token it would be wise to explicitly discard the now-unneeded earlier lines, to avoid future multi-line tokens growing the buffer without bound. </p>
<pre><code>        void lex_discard_to(char *ptr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_grow_linestr</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Reallocates the lexer buffer (``PL_parser-&gt;linestr&#8217;&#8216;) to accommodate at least <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> octets (including terminating <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>). Returns a pointer to the reallocated buffer. This is necessary before making any direct modification of the buffer that would increase its length. ``lex_stuff_pvn&#8217;&#8216; provides a more convenient way to insert text into the buffer. </p>
<p>Do not use <code>&quot;SvGROW&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;sv_grow&quot;</code> directly on <code>&quot;PL_parser-&gt;linestr&quot;</code>; this function updates all of the lexer&#8217;s variables that point directly into the buffer. </p>
<pre><code>        char * lex_grow_linestr(STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_next_chunk</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Reads in the next chunk of text to be lexed, appending it to ``PL_parser-&gt;linestr&#8217;&#8216;. This should be called when lexing code has looked to the end of the current chunk and wants to know more. It is usual, but not necessary, for lexing to have consumed the entirety of the current chunk at this time. </p>
<p>If ``PL_parser-&gt;bufptr&#8217;&#8216; is pointing to the very end of the current chunk (i.e., the current chunk has been entirely consumed), normally the current chunk will be discarded at the same time that the new chunk is read in. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;LEX_KEEP_PREVIOUS&quot;</code> bit set, the current chunk will not be discarded. If the current chunk has not been entirely consumed, then it will not be discarded regardless of the flag. </p>
<p>Returns true if some new text was added to the buffer, or false if the buffer has reached the end of the input text. </p>
<pre><code>        bool lex_next_chunk(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_peek_unichar</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Looks ahead one (Unicode) character in the text currently being lexed. Returns the codepoint (unsigned integer value) of the next character, or -1 if lexing has reached the end of the input text. To consume the peeked character, use ``lex_read_unichar&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>If the next character is in (or extends into) the next chunk of input text, the next chunk will be read in. Normally the current chunk will be discarded at the same time, but if <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;LEX_KEEP_PREVIOUS&quot;</code> bit set, then the current chunk will not be discarded. </p>
<p>If the input is being interpreted as UTF-8 and a UTF-8 encoding error is encountered, an exception is generated. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 lex_peek_unichar(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_read_space</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Reads optional spaces, in Perl style, in the text currently being lexed. The spaces may include ordinary whitespace characters and Perl-style comments. <code>&quot;#line&quot;</code> directives are processed if encountered. ``PL_parser-&gt;bufptr&#8217;&#8216; is moved past the spaces, so that it points at a non-space character (or the end of the input text). </p>
<p>If spaces extend into the next chunk of input text, the next chunk will be read in. Normally the current chunk will be discarded at the same time, but if <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;LEX_KEEP_PREVIOUS&quot;</code> bit set, then the current chunk will not be discarded. </p>
<pre><code>        void lex_read_space(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_read_to</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Consume text in the lexer buffer, from ``PL_parser-&gt;bufptr&#8217;&#8216; up to <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code>. This advances ``PL_parser-&gt;bufptr&#8217;&#8216; to match <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code>, performing the correct bookkeeping whenever a newline character is passed. This is the normal way to consume lexed text. </p>
<p>Interpretation of the buffer&#8217;s octets can be abstracted out by using the slightly higher-level functions ``lex_peek_unichar&#8217;&#8216; and ``lex_read_unichar&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        void lex_read_to(char *ptr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_read_unichar</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Reads the next (Unicode) character in the text currently being lexed. Returns the codepoint (unsigned integer value) of the character read, and moves ``PL_parser-&gt;bufptr&#8217;&#8216; past the character, or returns -1 if lexing has reached the end of the input text. To non-destructively examine the next character, use ``lex_peek_unichar&#8217;&#8216; instead. </p>
<p>If the next character is in (or extends into) the next chunk of input text, the next chunk will be read in. Normally the current chunk will be discarded at the same time, but if <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;LEX_KEEP_PREVIOUS&quot;</code> bit set, then the current chunk will not be discarded. </p>
<p>If the input is being interpreted as UTF-8 and a UTF-8 encoding error is encountered, an exception is generated. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 lex_read_unichar(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_start</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Creates and initialises a new lexer/parser state object, supplying a context in which to lex and parse from a new source of Perl code. A pointer to the new state object is placed in ``PL_parser&#8217;&#8216;. An entry is made on the save stack so that upon unwinding, the new state object will be destroyed and the former value of ``PL_parser&#8217;&#8216; will be restored. Nothing else need be done to clean up the parsing context. </p>
<p>The code to be parsed comes from <code>&quot;line&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;rsfp&quot;</code>. <code>&quot;line&quot;</code>, if non-null, provides a string (in SV form) containing code to be parsed. A copy of the string is made, so subsequent modification of <code>&quot;line&quot;</code> does not affect parsing. <code>&quot;rsfp&quot;</code>, if non-null, provides an input stream from which code will be read to be parsed. If both are non-null, the code in <code>&quot;line&quot;</code> comes first and must consist of complete lines of input, and <code>&quot;rsfp&quot;</code> supplies the remainder of the source. </p>
<p>The <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter is reserved for future use. Currently it is only used by perl internally, so extensions should always pass zero. </p>
<pre><code>        void lex_start(SV *line, PerlIO *rsfp, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_stuff_pv</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Insert characters into the lexer buffer (``PL_parser-&gt;linestr&#8217;&#8216;), immediately after the current lexing point (``PL_parser-&gt;bufptr&#8217;&#8216;), reallocating the buffer if necessary. This means that lexing code that runs later will see the characters as if they had appeared in the input. It is not recommended to do this as part of normal parsing, and most uses of this facility run the risk of the inserted characters being interpreted in an unintended manner. </p>
<p>The string to be inserted is represented by octets starting at <code>&quot;pv&quot;</code> and continuing to the first nul. These octets are interpreted as either UTF-8 or Latin-1, according to whether the <code>&quot;LEX_STUFF_UTF8&quot;</code> flag is set in <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code>. The characters are recoded for the lexer buffer, according to how the buffer is currently being interpreted (``lex_bufutf8&#8217;&#8216;). If it is not convenient to nul-terminate a string to be inserted, the ``lex_stuff_pvn&#8217;&#8216; function is more appropriate. </p>
<pre><code>        void lex_stuff_pv(const char *pv, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_stuff_pvn</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Insert characters into the lexer buffer (``PL_parser-&gt;linestr&#8217;&#8216;), immediately after the current lexing point (``PL_parser-&gt;bufptr&#8217;&#8216;), reallocating the buffer if necessary. This means that lexing code that runs later will see the characters as if they had appeared in the input. It is not recommended to do this as part of normal parsing, and most uses of this facility run the risk of the inserted characters being interpreted in an unintended manner. </p>
<p>The string to be inserted is represented by <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> octets starting at <code>&quot;pv&quot;</code>. These octets are interpreted as either UTF-8 or Latin-1, according to whether the <code>&quot;LEX_STUFF_UTF8&quot;</code> flag is set in <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code>. The characters are recoded for the lexer buffer, according to how the buffer is currently being interpreted (``lex_bufutf8&#8217;&#8216;). If a string to be inserted is available as a Perl scalar, the ``lex_stuff_sv&#8217;&#8216; function is more convenient. </p>
<pre><code>        void lex_stuff_pvn(const char *pv, STRLEN len,
                              U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_stuff_pvs</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Like ``lex_stuff_pvn&#8217;&#8216;, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        void lex_stuff_pvs(&quot;literal string&quot; pv, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_stuff_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Insert characters into the lexer buffer (``PL_parser-&gt;linestr&#8217;&#8216;), immediately after the current lexing point (``PL_parser-&gt;bufptr&#8217;&#8216;), reallocating the buffer if necessary. This means that lexing code that runs later will see the characters as if they had appeared in the input. It is not recommended to do this as part of normal parsing, and most uses of this facility run the risk of the inserted characters being interpreted in an unintended manner. </p>
<p>The string to be inserted is the string value of <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>. The characters are recoded for the lexer buffer, according to how the buffer is currently being interpreted (``lex_bufutf8&#8217;&#8216;). If a string to be inserted is not already a Perl scalar, the ``lex_stuff_pvn&#8217;&#8216; function avoids the need to construct a scalar. </p>
<pre><code>        void lex_stuff_sv(SV *sv, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>lex_unstuff</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Discards text about to be lexed, from ``PL_parser-&gt;bufptr&#8217;&#8216; up to <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code>. Text following <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code> will be moved, and the buffer shortened. This hides the discarded text from any lexing code that runs later, as if the text had never appeared. </p>
<p>This is not the normal way to consume lexed text. For that, use ``lex_read_to&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        void lex_unstuff(char *ptr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>parse_arithexpr</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Parse a Perl arithmetic expression. This may contain operators of precedence down to the bit shift operators. The expression must be followed (and thus terminated) either by a comparison or lower-precedence operator or by something that would normally terminate an expression such as semicolon. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;PARSE_OPTIONAL&quot;</code> bit set, then the expression is optional, otherwise it is mandatory. It is up to the caller to ensure that the dynamic parser state (``PL_parser&#8217;&#8216; et al) is correctly set to reflect the source of the code to be parsed and the lexical context for the expression. </p>
<p>The op tree representing the expression is returned. If an optional expression is absent, a null pointer is returned, otherwise the pointer will be non-null. </p>
<p>If an error occurs in parsing or compilation, in most cases a valid op tree is returned anyway. The error is reflected in the parser state, normally resulting in a single exception at the top level of parsing which covers all the compilation errors that occurred. Some compilation errors, however, will throw an exception immediately. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * parse_arithexpr(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>parse_barestmt</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Parse a single unadorned Perl statement. This may be a normal imperative statement or a declaration that has compile-time effect. It does not include any label or other affixture. It is up to the caller to ensure that the dynamic parser state (``PL_parser&#8217;&#8216; et al) is correctly set to reflect the source of the code to be parsed and the lexical context for the statement. </p>
<p>The op tree representing the statement is returned. This may be a null pointer if the statement is null, for example if it was actually a subroutine definition (which has compile-time side effects). If not null, it will be ops directly implementing the statement, suitable to pass to ``newSTATEOP&#8217;&#8216;. It will not normally include a <code>&quot;nextstate&quot;</code> or equivalent op (except for those embedded in a scope contained entirely within the statement). </p>
<p>If an error occurs in parsing or compilation, in most cases a valid op tree (most likely null) is returned anyway. The error is reflected in the parser state, normally resulting in a single exception at the top level of parsing which covers all the compilation errors that occurred. Some compilation errors, however, will throw an exception immediately. </p>
<p>The <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter is reserved for future use, and must always be zero. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * parse_barestmt(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>parse_block</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Parse a single complete Perl code block. This consists of an opening brace, a sequence of statements, and a closing brace. The block constitutes a lexical scope, so <code>&quot;my&quot;</code> variables and various compile-time effects can be contained within it. It is up to the caller to ensure that the dynamic parser state (``PL_parser&#8217;&#8216; et al) is correctly set to reflect the source of the code to be parsed and the lexical context for the statement. </p>
<p>The op tree representing the code block is returned. This is always a real op, never a null pointer. It will normally be a <code>&quot;lineseq&quot;</code> list, including <code>&quot;nextstate&quot;</code> or equivalent ops. No ops to construct any kind of runtime scope are included by virtue of it being a block. </p>
<p>If an error occurs in parsing or compilation, in most cases a valid op tree (most likely null) is returned anyway. The error is reflected in the parser state, normally resulting in a single exception at the top level of parsing which covers all the compilation errors that occurred. Some compilation errors, however, will throw an exception immediately. </p>
<p>The <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter is reserved for future use, and must always be zero. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * parse_block(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>parse_fullexpr</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Parse a single complete Perl expression. This allows the full expression grammar, including the lowest-precedence operators such as <code>&quot;or&quot;</code>. The expression must be followed (and thus terminated) by a token that an expression would normally be terminated by: end-of-file, closing bracketing punctuation, semicolon, or one of the keywords that signals a postfix expression-statement modifier. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;PARSE_OPTIONAL&quot;</code> bit set, then the expression is optional, otherwise it is mandatory. It is up to the caller to ensure that the dynamic parser state (``PL_parser&#8217;&#8216; et al) is correctly set to reflect the source of the code to be parsed and the lexical context for the expression. </p>
<p>The op tree representing the expression is returned. If an optional expression is absent, a null pointer is returned, otherwise the pointer will be non-null. </p>
<p>If an error occurs in parsing or compilation, in most cases a valid op tree is returned anyway. The error is reflected in the parser state, normally resulting in a single exception at the top level of parsing which covers all the compilation errors that occurred. Some compilation errors, however, will throw an exception immediately. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * parse_fullexpr(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>parse_fullstmt</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Parse a single complete Perl statement. This may be a normal imperative statement or a declaration that has compile-time effect, and may include optional labels. It is up to the caller to ensure that the dynamic parser state (``PL_parser&#8217;&#8216; et al) is correctly set to reflect the source of the code to be parsed and the lexical context for the statement. </p>
<p>The op tree representing the statement is returned. This may be a null pointer if the statement is null, for example if it was actually a subroutine definition (which has compile-time side effects). If not null, it will be the result of a ``newSTATEOP&#8217;&#8216; call, normally including a <code>&quot;nextstate&quot;</code> or equivalent op. </p>
<p>If an error occurs in parsing or compilation, in most cases a valid op tree (most likely null) is returned anyway. The error is reflected in the parser state, normally resulting in a single exception at the top level of parsing which covers all the compilation errors that occurred. Some compilation errors, however, will throw an exception immediately. </p>
<p>The <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter is reserved for future use, and must always be zero. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * parse_fullstmt(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>parse_label</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Parse a single label, possibly optional, of the type that may prefix a Perl statement. It is up to the caller to ensure that the dynamic parser state (``PL_parser&#8217;&#8216; et al) is correctly set to reflect the source of the code to be parsed. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;PARSE_OPTIONAL&quot;</code> bit set, then the label is optional, otherwise it is mandatory. </p>
<p>The name of the label is returned in the form of a fresh scalar. If an optional label is absent, a null pointer is returned. </p>
<p>If an error occurs in parsing, which can only occur if the label is mandatory, a valid label is returned anyway. The error is reflected in the parser state, normally resulting in a single exception at the top level of parsing which covers all the compilation errors that occurred. </p>
<pre><code>        SV * parse_label(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>parse_listexpr</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Parse a Perl list expression. This may contain operators of precedence down to the comma operator. The expression must be followed (and thus terminated) either by a low-precedence logic operator such as <code>&quot;or&quot;</code> or by something that would normally terminate an expression such as semicolon. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;PARSE_OPTIONAL&quot;</code> bit set, then the expression is optional, otherwise it is mandatory. It is up to the caller to ensure that the dynamic parser state (``PL_parser&#8217;&#8216; et al) is correctly set to reflect the source of the code to be parsed and the lexical context for the expression. </p>
<p>The op tree representing the expression is returned. If an optional expression is absent, a null pointer is returned, otherwise the pointer will be non-null. </p>
<p>If an error occurs in parsing or compilation, in most cases a valid op tree is returned anyway. The error is reflected in the parser state, normally resulting in a single exception at the top level of parsing which covers all the compilation errors that occurred. Some compilation errors, however, will throw an exception immediately. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * parse_listexpr(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>parse_stmtseq</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Parse a sequence of zero or more Perl statements. These may be normal imperative statements, including optional labels, or declarations that have compile-time effect, or any mixture thereof. The statement sequence ends when a closing brace or end-of-file is encountered in a place where a new statement could have validly started. It is up to the caller to ensure that the dynamic parser state (``PL_parser&#8217;&#8216; et al) is correctly set to reflect the source of the code to be parsed and the lexical context for the statements. </p>
<p>The op tree representing the statement sequence is returned. This may be a null pointer if the statements were all null, for example if there were no statements or if there were only subroutine definitions (which have compile-time side effects). If not null, it will be a <code>&quot;lineseq&quot;</code> list, normally including <code>&quot;nextstate&quot;</code> or equivalent ops. </p>
<p>If an error occurs in parsing or compilation, in most cases a valid op tree is returned anyway. The error is reflected in the parser state, normally resulting in a single exception at the top level of parsing which covers all the compilation errors that occurred. Some compilation errors, however, will throw an exception immediately. </p>
<p>The <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter is reserved for future use, and must always be zero. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * parse_stmtseq(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>parse_termexpr</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Parse a Perl term expression. This may contain operators of precedence down to the assignment operators. The expression must be followed (and thus terminated) either by a comma or lower-precedence operator or by something that would normally terminate an expression such as semicolon. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;PARSE_OPTIONAL&quot;</code> bit set, then the expression is optional, otherwise it is mandatory. It is up to the caller to ensure that the dynamic parser state (``PL_parser&#8217;&#8216; et al) is correctly set to reflect the source of the code to be parsed and the lexical context for the expression. </p>
<p>The op tree representing the expression is returned. If an optional expression is absent, a null pointer is returned, otherwise the pointer will be non-null. </p>
<p>If an error occurs in parsing or compilation, in most cases a valid op tree is returned anyway. The error is reflected in the parser state, normally resulting in a single exception at the top level of parsing which covers all the compilation errors that occurred. Some compilation errors, however, will throw an exception immediately. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * parse_termexpr(U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_parser</dt>
<dd> <p> Pointer to a structure encapsulating the state of the parsing operation currently in progress. The pointer can be locally changed to perform a nested parse without interfering with the state of an outer parse. Individual members of <code>&quot;PL_parser&quot;</code> have their own documentation. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_parser-&gt;bufend</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Direct pointer to the end of the chunk of text currently being lexed, the end of the lexer buffer. This is equal to <code>&quot;SvPVX(PL_parser-&gt;linestr) + SvCUR(PL_parser-&gt;linestr)&quot;</code>. A <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> character (zero octet) is always located at the end of the buffer, and does not count as part of the buffer&#8217;s contents. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_parser-&gt;bufptr</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Points to the current position of lexing inside the lexer buffer. Characters around this point may be freely examined, within the range delimited by <code>&quot;SvPVX(&quot;PL_parser-&gt;linestr&quot;)&quot;</code> and ``PL_parser-&gt;bufend&#8217;&#8216;. The octets of the buffer may be intended to be interpreted as either UTF-8 or Latin-1, as indicated by ``lex_bufutf8&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>Lexing code (whether in the Perl core or not) moves this pointer past the characters that it consumes. It is also expected to perform some bookkeeping whenever a newline character is consumed. This movement can be more conveniently performed by the function ``lex_read_to&#8217;&#8216;, which handles newlines appropriately. </p>
<p>Interpretation of the buffer&#8217;s octets can be abstracted out by using the slightly higher-level functions ``lex_peek_unichar&#8217;&#8216; and ``lex_read_unichar&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_parser-&gt;linestart</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Points to the start of the current line inside the lexer buffer. This is useful for indicating at which column an error occurred, and not much else. This must be updated by any lexing code that consumes a newline; the function ``lex_read_to&#8217;&#8216; handles this detail. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_parser-&gt;linestr</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Buffer scalar containing the chunk currently under consideration of the text currently being lexed. This is always a plain string scalar (for which <code>&quot;SvPOK&quot;</code> is true). It is not intended to be used as a scalar by normal scalar means; instead refer to the buffer directly by the pointer variables described below. </p>
<p>The lexer maintains various <code>&quot;char*&quot;</code> pointers to things in the <code>&quot;PL_parser-&gt;linestr&quot;</code> buffer. If <code>&quot;PL_parser-&gt;linestr&quot;</code> is ever reallocated, all of these pointers must be updated. Don&#8217;t attempt to do this manually, but rather use ``lex_grow_linestr&#8217;&#8216; if you need to reallocate the buffer. </p>
<p>The content of the text chunk in the buffer is commonly exactly one complete line of input, up to and including a newline terminator, but there are situations where it is otherwise. The octets of the buffer may be intended to be interpreted as either UTF-8 or Latin-1. The function ``lex_bufutf8&#8217;&#8216; tells you which. Do not use the <code>&quot;SvUTF8&quot;</code> flag on this scalar, which may disagree with it. </p>
<p>For direct examination of the buffer, the variable ``PL_parser-&gt;bufend&#8217;&#8216; points to the end of the buffer. The current lexing position is pointed to by ``PL_parser-&gt;bufptr&#8217;&#8216;. Direct use of these pointers is usually preferable to examination of the scalar through normal scalar means. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>wrap_keyword_plugin</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Puts a C function into the chain of keyword plugins. This is the preferred way to manipulate the ``PL_keyword_plugin&#8217;&#8216; variable. <code>&quot;new_plugin&quot;</code> is a pointer to the C function that is to be added to the keyword plugin chain, and <code>&quot;old_plugin_p&quot;</code> points to the storage location where a pointer to the next function in the chain will be stored. The value of <code>&quot;new_plugin&quot;</code> is written into the ``PL_keyword_plugin&#8217;&#8216; variable, while the value previously stored there is written to <code>*old_plugin_p</code>. </p>
<p>``PL_keyword_plugin&#8217;&#8216; is global to an entire process, and a module wishing to hook keyword parsing may find itself invoked more than once per process, typically in different threads. To handle that situation, this function is idempotent. The location <code>*old_plugin_p</code> must initially (once per process) contain a null pointer. A C variable of static duration (declared at file scope, typically also marked <code>&quot;static&quot;</code> to give it internal linkage) will be implicitly initialised appropriately, if it does not have an explicit initialiser. This function will only actually modify the plugin chain if it finds <code>*old_plugin_p</code> to be null. This function is also thread safe on the small scale. It uses appropriate locking to avoid race conditions in accessing ``PL_keyword_plugin&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>When this function is called, the function referenced by <code>&quot;new_plugin&quot;</code> must be ready to be called, except for <code>*old_plugin_p</code> being unfilled. In a threading situation, <code>&quot;new_plugin&quot;</code> may be called immediately, even before this function has returned. <code>*old_plugin_p</code> will always be appropriately set before <code>&quot;new_plugin&quot;</code> is called. If <code>&quot;new_plugin&quot;</code> decides not to do anything special with the identifier that it is given (which is the usual case for most calls to a keyword plugin), it must chain the plugin function referenced by <code>*old_plugin_p</code>. </p>
<p>Taken all together, XS code to install a keyword plugin should typically look something like this: </p>
<pre><code>    static Perl_keyword_plugin_t next_keyword_plugin;
    static OP *my_keyword_plugin(pTHX_
        char *keyword_plugin, STRLEN keyword_len, OP **op_ptr)
    {
        if (memEQs(keyword_ptr, keyword_len,
                   &quot;my_new_keyword&quot;)) {
            ...
        } else {
            return next_keyword_plugin(aTHX_
                keyword_ptr, keyword_len, op_ptr);
        }
    }
    BOOT:
        wrap_keyword_plugin(my_keyword_plugin,
                            &amp;next_keyword_plugin);
</code></pre>
<p>Direct access to ``PL_keyword_plugin&#8217;&#8216; should be avoided. </p>
<pre><code>        void wrap_keyword_plugin(
                    Perl_keyword_plugin_t new_plugin,
                    Perl_keyword_plugin_t *old_plugin_p
                )
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Locale-related functions and macros</h2>
<dl>
<dt>DECLARATION_FOR_LC_NUMERIC_MANIPULATION</dt>
<dd> <p> This macro should be used as a statement. It declares a private variable (whose name begins with an underscore) that is needed by the other macros in this section. Failing to include this correctly should lead to a syntax error. For compatibility with C89 C compilers it should be placed in a block before any executable statements. </p>
<pre><code>        void DECLARATION_FOR_LC_NUMERIC_MANIPULATION
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>Perl_langinfo</dt>
<dd> <p> This is an (almost) drop-in replacement for the system <code><a href="/docs/linux/man/3-nl_langinfo/">nl_langinfo</a>(3)</code>, taking the same <code>&quot;item&quot;</code> parameter values, and returning the same information. But it is more thread-safe than regular <code>&quot;nl_langinfo()&quot;</code>, and hides the quirks of Perl&#8217;s locale handling from your code, and can be used on systems that lack a native <code>&quot;nl_langinfo&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Expanding on these: </p>
 </dd>
<dl>
<dd>  </dd>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</dt>
<dd> <p> The reason it isn&#8217;t quite a drop-in replacement is actually an advantage. The only difference is that it returns <code>&quot;const char *&quot;</code>, whereas plain <code>&quot;nl_langinfo()&quot;</code> returns <code>&quot;char *&quot;</code>, but you are (only by documentation) forbidden to write into the buffer. By declaring this <code>&quot;const&quot;</code>, the compiler enforces this restriction, so if it is violated, you know at compilation time, rather than getting segfaults at runtime. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&bull;</dt>
<dd> <p> It delivers the correct results for the <code>&quot;RADIXCHAR&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;THOUSEP&quot;</code> items, without you having to write extra code. The reason for the extra code would be because these are from the <code>&quot;LC_NUMERIC&quot;</code> locale category, which is normally kept set by Perl so that the radix is a dot, and the separator is the empty string, no matter what the underlying locale is supposed to be, and so to get the expected results, you have to temporarily toggle into the underlying locale, and later toggle back. (You could use plain <code>&quot;nl_langinfo&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;STORE_LC_NUMERIC_FORCE_TO_UNDERLYING&quot;</code> for this but then you wouldn&#8217;t get the other advantages of <code>&quot;Perl_langinfo()&quot;</code>; not keeping <code>&quot;LC_NUMERIC&quot;</code> in the C (or equivalent) locale would break a lot of CPAN, which is expecting the radix (decimal point) character to be a dot.) </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&bull;</dt>
<dd> <p> The system function it replaces can have its static return buffer trashed, not only by a subesequent call to that function, but by a <code>&quot;freelocale&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;setlocale&quot;</code>, or other locale change. The returned buffer of this function is not changed until the next call to it, so the buffer is never in a trashed state. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&bull;</dt>
<dd> <p> Its return buffer is per-thread, so it also is never overwritten by a call to this function from another thread; unlike the function it replaces. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&bull;</dt>
<dd> <p> But most importantly, it works on systems that don&#8217;t have <code>&quot;nl_langinfo&quot;</code>, such as Windows, hence makes your code more portable. Of the fifty-some possible items specified by the POSIX 2008 standard, &lt;<a rel="nofollow" href="http://pubs.opengroup.org/onlinepubs/9699919799/basedefs/langinfo.h.html">http://pubs.opengroup.org/onlinepubs/9699919799/basedefs/langinfo.h.html</a>&gt;, only one is completely unimplemented, though on non-Windows platforms, another significant one is also not implemented). It uses various techniques to recover the other items, including calling <code><a href="/docs/linux/man/3-localeconv/">localeconv</a>(3)</code>, and <code><a href="/docs/linux/man/3-strftime/">strftime</a>(3)</code>, both of which are specified in C89, so should be always be available. Later <code>&quot;strftime()&quot;</code> versions have additional capabilities; <code>&quot;&quot;</code> is returned for those not available on your system. </p>
<p>It is important to note that when called with an item that is recovered by using <code>&quot;localeconv&quot;</code>, the buffer from any previous explicit call to <code>&quot;localeconv&quot;</code> will be overwritten. This means you must save that buffer&#8217;s contents if you need to access them after a call to this function. (But note that you might not want to be using <code>&quot;localeconv()&quot;</code> directly anyway, because of issues like the ones listed in the second item of this list (above) for <code>&quot;RADIXCHAR&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;THOUSEP&quot;</code>. You can use the methods given in perlcall to call ``localeconv&#8217;&#8216; in POSIX and avoid all the issues, but then you have a hash to unpack). </p>
<p>The details for those items which may deviate from what this emulation returns and what a native <code>&quot;nl_langinfo()&quot;</code> would return are specified in I18N::Langinfo. </p>
 </dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dd> <p>When using <code>&quot;Perl_langinfo&quot;</code> on systems that don&#8217;t have a native <code>&quot;nl_langinfo()&quot;</code>, you must </p>
<pre><code> #include &quot;perl_langinfo.h&quot;
</code></pre>
<p>before the <code>&quot;perl.h&quot;</code> <code>&quot;#include&quot;</code>. You can replace your <code>&quot;langinfo.h&quot;</code> <code>&quot;#include&quot;</code> with this one. (Doing it this way keeps out the symbols that plain <code>&quot;langinfo.h&quot;</code> would try to import into the namespace for code that doesn&#8217;t need it.) </p>
<p>The original impetus for <code>&quot;Perl_langinfo()&quot;</code> was so that code that needs to find out the current currency symbol, floating point radix character, or digit grouping separator can use, on all systems, the simpler and more thread-friendly <code>&quot;nl_langinfo&quot;</code> API instead of <code><a href="/docs/linux/man/3-localeconv/">localeconv</a>(3)</code> which is a pain to make thread-friendly. For other fields returned by <code>&quot;localeconv&quot;</code>, it is better to use the methods given in perlcall to call <code>&quot;POSIX::localeconv()&quot;</code>, which is thread-friendly. </p>
<pre><code>        const char* Perl_langinfo(const nl_item item)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<dt>Perl_setlocale</dt>
<dd> <p> This is an (almost) drop-in replacement for the system <code><a href="/docs/linux/man/3-setlocale/">setlocale</a>(3)</code>, taking the same parameters, and returning the same information, except that it returns the correct underlying <code>&quot;LC_NUMERIC&quot;</code> locale. Regular <code>&quot;setlocale&quot;</code> will instead return <code>&quot;C&quot;</code> if the underlying locale has a non-dot decimal point character, or a non-empty thousands separator for displaying floating point numbers. This is because perl keeps that locale category such that it has a dot and empty separator, changing the locale briefly during the operations where the underlying one is required. <code>&quot;Perl_setlocale&quot;</code> knows about this, and compensates; regular <code>&quot;setlocale&quot;</code> doesn&#8217;t. </p>
<p>Another reason it isn&#8217;t completely a drop-in replacement is that it is declared to return <code>&quot;const char *&quot;</code>, whereas the system setlocale omits the <code>&quot;const&quot;</code> (presumably because its API was specified long ago, and can&#8217;t be updated; it is illegal to change the information <code>&quot;setlocale&quot;</code> returns; doing so leads to segfaults.) </p>
<p>Finally, <code>&quot;Perl_setlocale&quot;</code> works under all circumstances, whereas plain <code>&quot;setlocale&quot;</code> can be completely ineffective on some platforms under some configurations. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;Perl_setlocale&quot;</code> should not be used to change the locale except on systems where the predefined variable <code>&quot;${^SAFE_LOCALES}&quot;</code> is 1. On some such systems, the system <code>&quot;setlocale()&quot;</code> is ineffective, returning the wrong information, and failing to actually change the locale. <code>&quot;Perl_setlocale&quot;</code>, however works properly in all circumstances. </p>
<p>The return points to a per-thread static buffer, which is overwritten the next time <code>&quot;Perl_setlocale&quot;</code> is called from the same thread. </p>
<pre><code>        const char* Perl_setlocale(const int category,
                                   const char* locale)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>RESTORE_LC_NUMERIC</dt>
<dd> <p> This is used in conjunction with one of the macros ``STORE_LC_NUMERIC_SET_TO_NEEDED&#8217;&#8216; and ``STORE_LC_NUMERIC_FORCE_TO_UNDERLYING&#8217;&#8216; to properly restore the <code>&quot;LC_NUMERIC&quot;</code> state. </p>
<p>A call to ``DECLARATION_FOR_LC_NUMERIC_MANIPULATION&#8217;&#8216; must have been made to declare at compile time a private variable used by this macro and the two <code>&quot;STORE&quot;</code> ones. This macro should be called as a single statement, not an expression, but with an empty argument list, like this: </p>
<pre><code> {
    DECLARATION_FOR_LC_NUMERIC_MANIPULATION;
     ...
    RESTORE_LC_NUMERIC();
     ...
 }
        void RESTORE_LC_NUMERIC()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>STORE_LC_NUMERIC_FORCE_TO_UNDERLYING</dt>
<dd> <p> This is used by XS code that that is <code>&quot;LC_NUMERIC&quot;</code> locale-aware to force the locale for category <code>&quot;LC_NUMERIC&quot;</code> to be what perl thinks is the current underlying locale. (The perl interpreter could be wrong about what the underlying locale actually is if some C or XS code has called the C library function <code><a href="/docs/linux/man/3-setlocale/">setlocale</a></code>(3) behind its back; calling ``sync_locale&#8217;&#8216; before calling this macro will update perl&#8217;s records.) </p>
<p>A call to ``DECLARATION_FOR_LC_NUMERIC_MANIPULATION&#8217;&#8216; must have been made to declare at compile time a private variable used by this macro. This macro should be called as a single statement, not an expression, but with an empty argument list, like this: </p>
<pre><code> {
    DECLARATION_FOR_LC_NUMERIC_MANIPULATION;
     ...
    STORE_LC_NUMERIC_FORCE_TO_UNDERLYING();
     ...
    RESTORE_LC_NUMERIC();
     ...
 }
</code></pre>
<p>The private variable is used to save the current locale state, so that the requisite matching call to ``RESTORE_LC_NUMERIC&#8217;&#8216; can restore it. </p>
<p>On threaded perls not operating with thread-safe functionality, this macro uses a mutex to force a critical section. Therefore the matching RESTORE should be close by, and guaranteed to be called. </p>
<pre><code>        void STORE_LC_NUMERIC_FORCE_TO_UNDERLYING()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>STORE_LC_NUMERIC_SET_TO_NEEDED</dt>
<dd> <p> This is used to help wrap XS or C code that is <code>&quot;LC_NUMERIC&quot;</code> locale-aware. This locale category is generally kept set to a locale where the decimal radix character is a dot, and the separator between groups of digits is empty. This is because most XS code that reads floating point numbers is expecting them to have this syntax. </p>
<p>This macro makes sure the current <code>&quot;LC_NUMERIC&quot;</code> state is set properly, to be aware of locale if the call to the XS or C code from the Perl program is from within the scope of a <code>&quot;use locale&quot;</code>; or to ignore locale if the call is instead from outside such scope. </p>
<p>This macro is the start of wrapping the C or XS code; the wrap ending is done by calling the ``RESTORE_LC_NUMERIC&#8217;&#8216; macro after the operation. Otherwise the state can be changed that will adversely affect other XS code. </p>
<p>A call to ``DECLARATION_FOR_LC_NUMERIC_MANIPULATION&#8217;&#8216; must have been made to declare at compile time a private variable used by this macro. This macro should be called as a single statement, not an expression, but with an empty argument list, like this: </p>
<pre><code> {
    DECLARATION_FOR_LC_NUMERIC_MANIPULATION;
     ...
    STORE_LC_NUMERIC_SET_TO_NEEDED();
     ...
    RESTORE_LC_NUMERIC();
     ...
 }
</code></pre>
<p>On threaded perls not operating with thread-safe functionality, this macro uses a mutex to force a critical section. Therefore the matching RESTORE should be close by, and guaranteed to be called. </p>
<pre><code>        void STORE_LC_NUMERIC_SET_TO_NEEDED()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>switch_to_global_locale</dt>
<dd> <p> On systems without locale support, or on typical single-threaded builds, or on platforms that do not support per-thread locale operations, this function does nothing. On such systems that do have locale support, only a locale global to the whole program is available. </p>
<p>On multi-threaded builds on systems that do have per-thread locale operations, this function converts the thread it is running in to use the global locale. This is for code that has not yet or cannot be updated to handle multi-threaded locale operation. As long as only a single thread is so-converted, everything works fine, as all the other threads continue to ignore the global one, so only this thread looks at it. </p>
<p>However, on Windows systems this isn&#8217;t quite true prior to Visual Studio 15, at which point Microsoft fixed a bug. A race can occur if you use the following operations on earlier Windows platforms: </p>
 </dd>
<dl>
<dd>  </dd>
<dl>
<dt>POSIX::localeconv</dt>
<dd>  </dd>
<dt>I18N::Langinfo, items &quot;CRNCYSTR&quot; and &quot;THOUSEP&quot;</dt>
<dd>  </dd>
<dt>&quot;Perl_langinfo&quot; in perlapi, items &quot;CRNCYSTR&quot; and &quot;THOUSEP&quot;</dt>
<dd>  </dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dd> <p>The first item is not fixable (except by upgrading to a later Visual Studio release), but it would be possible to work around the latter two items by using the Windows API functions <code>&quot;GetNumberFormat&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;GetCurrencyFormat&quot;</code>; patches welcome. </p>
<p>Without this function call, threads that use the <code><a href="/docs/linux/man/3-setlocale/">setlocale</a>(3)</code> system function will not work properly, as all the locale-sensitive functions will look at the per-thread locale, and <code>&quot;setlocale&quot;</code> will have no effect on this thread. </p>
<p>Perl code should convert to either call <code>&quot;Perl_setlocale&quot;</code> (which is a drop-in for the system <code>&quot;setlocale&quot;</code>) or use the methods given in perlcall to call <code>&quot;POSIX::setlocale&quot;</code>. Either one will transparently properly handle all cases of single- vs multi-thread, POSIX 2008-supported or not. </p>
<p>Non-Perl libraries, such as <code>&quot;gtk&quot;</code>, that call the system <code>&quot;setlocale&quot;</code> can continue to work if this function is called before transferring control to the library. </p>
<p>Upon return from the code that needs to use the global locale, <code>&quot;sync_locale()&quot;</code> should be called to restore the safe multi-thread operation. </p>
<pre><code>        void switch_to_global_locale()
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<dt>sync_locale</dt>
<dd> <p> <code>&quot;Perl_setlocale&quot;</code> can be used at any time to query or change the locale (though changing the locale is antisocial and dangerous on multi-threaded systems that don&#8217;t have multi-thread safe locale operations. (See ``Multi-threaded operation&#8217;&#8216; in perllocale). Using the system <code><a href="/docs/linux/man/3-setlocale/">setlocale</a>(3)</code> should be avoided. Nevertheless, certain non-Perl libraries called from XS, such as <code>&quot;Gtk&quot;</code> do so, and this can&#8217;t be changed. When the locale is changed by XS code that didn&#8217;t use <code>&quot;Perl_setlocale&quot;</code>, Perl needs to be told that the locale has changed. Use this function to do so, before returning to Perl. </p>
<p>The return value is a boolean: TRUE if the global locale at the time of call was in effect; and FALSE if a per-thread locale was in effect. This can be used by the caller that needs to restore things as-they-were to decide whether or not to call <code>&quot;Perl_switch_to_global_locale&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sync_locale()
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Magical Functions</h2>
<dl>
<dt>mg_clear</dt>
<dd> <p> Clear something magical that the SV represents. See <code>&quot;sv_magic&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        int mg_clear(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mg_copy</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies the magic from one SV to another. See <code>&quot;sv_magic&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        int mg_copy(SV *sv, SV *nsv, const char *key,
                        I32 klen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mg_find</dt>
<dd> <p> Finds the magic pointer for <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> matching the SV. See <code>&quot;sv_magic&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        MAGIC* mg_find(const SV* sv, int type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mg_findext</dt>
<dd> <p> Finds the magic pointer of <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> with the given <code>&quot;vtbl&quot;</code> for the <code>&quot;SV&quot;</code>. See <code>&quot;sv_magicext&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        MAGIC* mg_findext(const SV* sv, int type,
                           const MGVTBL *vtbl)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mg_free</dt>
<dd> <p> Free any magic storage used by the SV. See <code>&quot;sv_magic&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        int mg_free(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mg_freeext</dt>
<dd> <p> Remove any magic of type <code>&quot;how&quot;</code> using virtual table <code>&quot;vtbl&quot;</code> from the SV <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>. See ``sv_magic&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;mg_freeext(sv, how, NULL)&quot;</code> is equivalent to <code>&quot;mg_free_type(sv, how)&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mg_freeext(SV* sv, int how, const MGVTBL *vtbl)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mg_free_type</dt>
<dd> <p> Remove any magic of type <code>&quot;how&quot;</code> from the SV <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>. See ``sv_magic&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        void mg_free_type(SV *sv, int how)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mg_get</dt>
<dd> <p> Do magic before a value is retrieved from the SV. The type of SV must be &gt;= <code>&quot;SVt_PVMG&quot;</code>. See <code>&quot;sv_magic&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        int mg_get(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mg_length</dt>
<dd> <p> DEPRECATED! It is planned to remove this function from a future release of Perl. Do not use it for new code; remove it from existing code. </p>
<p>Reports on the SV&#8216;s length in bytes, calling length magic if available, but does not set the UTF8 flag on <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>. It will fall back to &#8216;get&#8217; magic if there is no &#8216;length&#8217; magic, but with no indication as to whether it called &#8216;get&#8217; magic. It assumes <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is a <code>&quot;PVMG&quot;</code> or higher. Use <code>&quot;sv_len()&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 mg_length(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mg_magical</dt>
<dd> <p> Turns on the magical status of an SV. See <code>&quot;sv_magic&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mg_magical(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mg_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Do magic after a value is assigned to the SV. See <code>&quot;sv_magic&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        int mg_set(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvGETMAGIC</dt>
<dd> <p> Invokes <code>&quot;mg_get&quot;</code> on an SV if it has &#8216;get&#8217; magic. For example, this will call <code>&quot;FETCH&quot;</code> on a tied variable. This macro evaluates its argument more than once. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvGETMAGIC(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvLOCK</dt>
<dd> <p> Arranges for a mutual exclusion lock to be obtained on <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> if a suitable module has been loaded. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvLOCK(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvSETMAGIC</dt>
<dd> <p> Invokes <code>&quot;mg_set&quot;</code> on an SV if it has &#8216;set&#8217; magic. This is necessary after modifying a scalar, in case it is a magical variable like <code>$|</code> or a tied variable (it calls <code>&quot;STORE&quot;</code>). This macro evaluates its argument more than once. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvSETMAGIC(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvSetMagicSV</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvSetSV&quot;</code>, but does any set magic required afterwards. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvSetMagicSV(SV* dsv, SV* ssv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvSetMagicSV_nosteal</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvSetSV_nosteal&quot;</code>, but does any set magic required afterwards. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvSetMagicSV_nosteal(SV* dsv, SV* ssv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvSetSV</dt>
<dd> <p> Calls <code>&quot;sv_setsv&quot;</code> if <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code> is not the same as <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code>. May evaluate arguments more than once. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic on the destination SV. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvSetSV(SV* dsv, SV* ssv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvSetSV_nosteal</dt>
<dd> <p> Calls a non-destructive version of <code>&quot;sv_setsv&quot;</code> if <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code> is not the same as <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code>. May evaluate arguments more than once. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvSetSV_nosteal(SV* dsv, SV* ssv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvSHARE</dt>
<dd> <p> Arranges for <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to be shared between threads if a suitable module has been loaded. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvSHARE(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_string_from_errnum</dt>
<dd> <p> Generates the message string describing an OS error and returns it as an SV. <code>&quot;errnum&quot;</code> must be a value that <code>&quot;errno&quot;</code> could take, identifying the type of error. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;tgtsv&quot;</code> is non-null then the string will be written into that SV (overwriting existing content) and it will be returned. If <code>&quot;tgtsv&quot;</code> is a null pointer then the string will be written into a new mortal SV which will be returned. </p>
<p>The message will be taken from whatever locale would be used by <code>$!</code>, and will be encoded in the SV in whatever manner would be used by <code>$!</code>. The details of this process are subject to future change. Currently, the message is taken from the C locale by default (usually producing an English message), and from the currently selected locale when in the scope of the <code>&quot;use locale&quot;</code> pragma. A heuristic attempt is made to decode the message from the locale&#8217;s character encoding, but it will only be decoded as either UTF-8 or ISO-8859-1. It is always correctly decoded in a UTF-8 locale, usually in an ISO-8859-1 locale, and never in any other locale. </p>
<p>The SV is always returned containing an actual string, and with no other OK bits set. Unlike <code>$!</code>, a message is even yielded for <code>&quot;errnum&quot;</code> zero (meaning success), and if no useful message is available then a useless string (currently empty) is returned. </p>
<pre><code>        SV * sv_string_from_errnum(int errnum, SV *tgtsv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvUNLOCK</dt>
<dd> <p> Releases a mutual exclusion lock on <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> if a suitable module has been loaded. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvUNLOCK(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Memory Management</h2>
<dl>
<dt>Copy</dt>
<dd> <p> The XSUB-writer&#8217;s interface to the C <code>&quot;memcpy&quot;</code> function. The <code>&quot;src&quot;</code> is the source, <code>&quot;dest&quot;</code> is the destination, <code>&quot;nitems&quot;</code> is the number of items, and <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the type. May fail on overlapping copies. See also <code>&quot;Move&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void Copy(void* src, void* dest, int nitems, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>CopyD</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;Copy&quot;</code> but returns <code>&quot;dest&quot;</code>. Useful for encouraging compilers to tail-call optimise. </p>
<pre><code>        void * CopyD(void* src, void* dest, int nitems, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>Move</dt>
<dd> <p> The XSUB-writer&#8217;s interface to the C <code>&quot;memmove&quot;</code> function. The <code>&quot;src&quot;</code> is the source, <code>&quot;dest&quot;</code> is the destination, <code>&quot;nitems&quot;</code> is the number of items, and <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the type. Can do overlapping moves. See also <code>&quot;Copy&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void Move(void* src, void* dest, int nitems, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>MoveD</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;Move&quot;</code> but returns <code>&quot;dest&quot;</code>. Useful for encouraging compilers to tail-call optimise. </p>
<pre><code>        void * MoveD(void* src, void* dest, int nitems, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>Newx</dt>
<dd> <p> The XSUB-writer&#8217;s interface to the C <code>&quot;malloc&quot;</code> function. </p>
<p>Memory obtained by this should <code>ONLY</code> be freed with ``Safefree&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>In 5.9.3, <code>Newx()</code> and friends replace the older <code>New()</code> API, and drops the first parameter, <i>x</i>, a debug aid which allowed callers to identify themselves. This aid has been superseded by a new build option, PERL_MEM_LOG (see ``PERL_MEM_LOG&#8217;&#8216; in perlhacktips). The older API is still there for use in XS modules supporting older perls. </p>
<pre><code>        void Newx(void* ptr, int nitems, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>Newxc</dt>
<dd> <p> The XSUB-writer&#8217;s interface to the C <code>&quot;malloc&quot;</code> function, with cast. See also <code>&quot;Newx&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Memory obtained by this should <code>ONLY</code> be freed with ``Safefree&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        void Newxc(void* ptr, int nitems, type, cast)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>Newxz</dt>
<dd> <p> The XSUB-writer&#8217;s interface to the C <code>&quot;malloc&quot;</code> function. The allocated memory is zeroed with <code>&quot;memzero&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;Newx&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Memory obtained by this should <code>ONLY</code> be freed with ``Safefree&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        void Newxz(void* ptr, int nitems, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>Poison</dt>
<dd> <p> PoisonWith(0xEF) for catching access to freed memory. </p>
<pre><code>        void Poison(void* dest, int nitems, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PoisonFree</dt>
<dd> <p> PoisonWith(0xEF) for catching access to freed memory. </p>
<pre><code>        void PoisonFree(void* dest, int nitems, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PoisonNew</dt>
<dd> <p> PoisonWith(0xAB) for catching access to allocated but uninitialized memory. </p>
<pre><code>        void PoisonNew(void* dest, int nitems, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PoisonWith</dt>
<dd> <p> Fill up memory with a byte pattern (a byte repeated over and over again) that hopefully catches attempts to access uninitialized memory. </p>
<pre><code>        void PoisonWith(void* dest, int nitems, type,
                           U8 byte)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>Renew</dt>
<dd> <p> The XSUB-writer&#8217;s interface to the C <code>&quot;realloc&quot;</code> function. </p>
<p>Memory obtained by this should <code>ONLY</code> be freed with ``Safefree&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        void Renew(void* ptr, int nitems, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>Renewc</dt>
<dd> <p> The XSUB-writer&#8217;s interface to the C <code>&quot;realloc&quot;</code> function, with cast. </p>
<p>Memory obtained by this should <code>ONLY</code> be freed with ``Safefree&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        void Renewc(void* ptr, int nitems, type, cast)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>Safefree</dt>
<dd> <p> The XSUB-writer&#8217;s interface to the C <code>&quot;free&quot;</code> function. </p>
<p>This should <code>ONLY</code> be used on memory obtained using ``Newx&#8217;&#8216; and friends. </p>
<pre><code>        void Safefree(void* ptr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>savepv</dt>
<dd> <p> Perl&#8217;s version of <code>&quot;strdup()&quot;</code>. Returns a pointer to a newly allocated string which is a duplicate of <code>&quot;pv&quot;</code>. The size of the string is determined by <code>&quot;strlen()&quot;</code>, which means it may not contain embedded <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters and must have a trailing <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>. The memory allocated for the new string can be freed with the <code>&quot;Safefree()&quot;</code> function. </p>
<p>On some platforms, Windows for example, all allocated memory owned by a thread is deallocated when that thread ends. So if you need that not to happen, you need to use the shared memory functions, such as <code>&quot;savesharedpv&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        char* savepv(const char* pv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>savepvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Perl&#8217;s version of what <code>&quot;strndup()&quot;</code> would be if it existed. Returns a pointer to a newly allocated string which is a duplicate of the first <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> bytes from <code>&quot;pv&quot;</code>, plus a trailing <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> byte. The memory allocated for the new string can be freed with the <code>&quot;Safefree()&quot;</code> function. </p>
<p>On some platforms, Windows for example, all allocated memory owned by a thread is deallocated when that thread ends. So if you need that not to happen, you need to use the shared memory functions, such as <code>&quot;savesharedpvn&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        char* savepvn(const char* pv, I32 len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>savepvs</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;savepvn&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        char* savepvs(&quot;literal string&quot; s)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>savesharedpv</dt>
<dd> <p> A version of <code>&quot;savepv()&quot;</code> which allocates the duplicate string in memory which is shared between threads. </p>
<pre><code>        char* savesharedpv(const char* pv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>savesharedpvn</dt>
<dd> <p> A version of <code>&quot;savepvn()&quot;</code> which allocates the duplicate string in memory which is shared between threads. (With the specific difference that a <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> pointer is not acceptable) </p>
<pre><code>        char* savesharedpvn(const char *const pv,
                              const STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>savesharedpvs</dt>
<dd> <p> A version of <code>&quot;savepvs()&quot;</code> which allocates the duplicate string in memory which is shared between threads. </p>
<pre><code>        char* savesharedpvs(&quot;literal string&quot; s)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>savesharedsvpv</dt>
<dd> <p> A version of <code>&quot;savesharedpv()&quot;</code> which allocates the duplicate string in memory which is shared between threads. </p>
<pre><code>        char* savesharedsvpv(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>savesvpv</dt>
<dd> <p> A version of <code>&quot;savepv()&quot;</code>/<code>&quot;savepvn()&quot;</code> which gets the string to duplicate from the passed in SV using <code>&quot;SvPV()&quot;</code> </p>
<p>On some platforms, Windows for example, all allocated memory owned by a thread is deallocated when that thread ends. So if you need that not to happen, you need to use the shared memory functions, such as <code>&quot;savesharedsvpv&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        char* savesvpv(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>StructCopy</dt>
<dd> <p> This is an architecture-independent macro to copy one structure to another. </p>
<pre><code>        void StructCopy(type *src, type *dest, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>Zero</dt>
<dd> <p> The XSUB-writer&#8217;s interface to the C <code>&quot;memzero&quot;</code> function. The <code>&quot;dest&quot;</code> is the destination, <code>&quot;nitems&quot;</code> is the number of items, and <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the type. </p>
<pre><code>        void Zero(void* dest, int nitems, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ZeroD</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;Zero&quot;</code> but returns dest. Useful for encouraging compilers to tail-call optimise. </p>
<pre><code>        void * ZeroD(void* dest, int nitems, type)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Miscellaneous Functions</h2>
<dl>
<dt>dump_c_backtrace</dt>
<dd> <p> Dumps the C backtrace to the given <code>&quot;fp&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Returns true if a backtrace could be retrieved, false if not. </p>
<pre><code>        bool dump_c_backtrace(PerlIO* fp, int max_depth,
                                 int skip)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>fbm_compile</dt>
<dd> <p> Analyzes the string in order to make fast searches on it using <code>&quot;fbm_instr()&quot;</code> --- the Boyer-Moore algorithm. </p>
<pre><code>        void fbm_compile(SV* sv, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>fbm_instr</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the location of the SV in the string delimited by <code>&quot;big&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;bigend&quot;</code> (<code>&quot;bigend&quot;</code>) is the char following the last char). It returns <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> if the string can&#8217;t be found. The <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> does not have to be <code>&quot;fbm_compiled&quot;</code>, but the search will not be as fast then. </p>
<pre><code>        char* fbm_instr(unsigned char* big,
                          unsigned char* bigend, SV* littlestr,
                          U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>foldEQ</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns true if the leading <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> bytes of the strings <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;s2&quot;</code> are the same case-insensitively; false otherwise. Uppercase and lowercase ASCII range bytes match themselves and their opposite case counterparts. Non-cased and non-ASCII range bytes match only themselves. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 foldEQ(const char* a, const char* b, I32 len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>foldEQ_locale</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns true if the leading <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> bytes of the strings <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;s2&quot;</code> are the same case-insensitively in the current locale; false otherwise. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 foldEQ_locale(const char* a, const char* b,
                              I32 len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>form</dt>
<dd> <p> Takes a sprintf-style format pattern and conventional (non-SV) arguments and returns the formatted string. </p>
<pre><code>    (char *) Perl_form(pTHX_ const char* pat, ...)
</code></pre>
<p>can be used any place a string (char *) is required: </p>
<pre><code>    char * s = Perl_form(&quot;%d.%d&quot;,major,minor);
</code></pre>
<p>Uses a single private buffer so if you want to format several strings you must explicitly copy the earlier strings away (and free the copies when you are done). </p>
<pre><code>        char* form(const char* pat, ...)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>getcwd_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> Fill <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> with current working directory </p>
<pre><code>        int getcwd_sv(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>get_c_backtrace_dump</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a SV containing a dump of <code>&quot;depth&quot;</code> frames of the call stack, skipping the <code>&quot;skip&quot;</code> innermost ones. <code>&quot;depth&quot;</code> of 20 is usually enough. </p>
<p>The appended output looks like: </p>
<p>... 1 10e004812:0082 Perl_croak util.c:1716 /usr/bin/perl 2 10df8d6d2:1d72 perl_parse perl.c:3975 /usr/bin/perl ... </p>
<p>The fields are tab-separated. The first column is the depth (zero being the innermost non-skipped frame). In the hex:offset, the hex is where the program counter was in <code>&quot;S_parse_body&quot;</code>, and the :offset (might be missing) tells how much inside the <code>&quot;S_parse_body&quot;</code> the program counter was. </p>
<p>The <code>&quot;util.c:1716&quot;</code> is the source code file and line number. </p>
<p>The <i>/usr/bin/perl</i> is obvious (hopefully). </p>
<p>Unknowns are <code>&quot;-&quot;</code>. Unknowns can happen unfortunately quite easily: if the platform doesn&#8217;t support retrieving the information; if the binary is missing the debug information; if the optimizer has transformed the code by for example inlining. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* get_c_backtrace_dump(int max_depth, int skip)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ibcmp</dt>
<dd> <p> This is a synonym for <code>&quot;(! foldEQ())&quot;</code> </p>
<pre><code>        I32 ibcmp(const char* a, const char* b, I32 len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ibcmp_locale</dt>
<dd> <p> This is a synonym for <code>&quot;(! foldEQ_locale())&quot;</code> </p>
<pre><code>        I32 ibcmp_locale(const char* a, const char* b,
                             I32 len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_safe_syscall</dt>
<dd> <p> Test that the given <code>&quot;pv&quot;</code> doesn&#8217;t contain any internal <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters. If it does, set <code>&quot;errno&quot;</code> to <code>&quot;ENOENT&quot;</code>, optionally warn, and return FALSE. </p>
<p>Return TRUE if the name is safe. </p>
<p>Used by the <code>&quot;IS_SAFE_SYSCALL()&quot;</code> macro. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_safe_syscall(const char *pv, STRLEN len,
                                const char *what,
                                const char *op_name)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>memEQ</dt>
<dd> <p> Test two buffers (which may contain embedded <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters, to see if they are equal. The <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> parameter indicates the number of bytes to compare. Returns zero if equal, or non-zero if non-equal. </p>
<pre><code>        bool memEQ(char* s1, char* s2, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>memNE</dt>
<dd> <p> Test two buffers (which may contain embedded <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters, to see if they are not equal. The <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> parameter indicates the number of bytes to compare. Returns zero if non-equal, or non-zero if equal. </p>
<pre><code>        bool memNE(char* s1, char* s2, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mess</dt>
<dd> <p> Take a sprintf-style format pattern and argument list. These are used to generate a string message. If the message does not end with a newline, then it will be extended with some indication of the current location in the code, as described for ``mess_sv&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>Normally, the resulting message is returned in a new mortal SV. During global destruction a single SV may be shared between uses of this function. </p>
<pre><code>        SV * mess(const char *pat, ...)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mess_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> Expands a message, intended for the user, to include an indication of the current location in the code, if the message does not already appear to be complete. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;basemsg&quot;</code> is the initial message or object. If it is a reference, it will be used as-is and will be the result of this function. Otherwise it is used as a string, and if it already ends with a newline, it is taken to be complete, and the result of this function will be the same string. If the message does not end with a newline, then a segment such as <code>&quot;at foo.pl line 37&quot;</code> will be appended, and possibly other clauses indicating the current state of execution. The resulting message will end with a dot and a newline. </p>
<p>Normally, the resulting message is returned in a new mortal SV. During global destruction a single SV may be shared between uses of this function. If <code>&quot;consume&quot;</code> is true, then the function is permitted (but not required) to modify and return <code>&quot;basemsg&quot;</code> instead of allocating a new SV. </p>
<pre><code>        SV * mess_sv(SV *basemsg, bool consume)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>my_snprintf</dt>
<dd> <p> The C library <code>&quot;snprintf&quot;</code> functionality, if available and standards-compliant (uses <code>&quot;vsnprintf&quot;</code>, actually). However, if the <code>&quot;vsnprintf&quot;</code> is not available, will unfortunately use the unsafe <code>&quot;vsprintf&quot;</code> which can overrun the buffer (there is an overrun check, but that may be too late). Consider using <code>&quot;sv_vcatpvf&quot;</code> instead, or getting <code>&quot;vsnprintf&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        int my_snprintf(char *buffer, const Size_t len,
                            const char *format, ...)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>my_strlcat</dt>
<dd> <p> The C library <code>&quot;strlcat&quot;</code> if available, or a Perl implementation of it. This operates on C <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated strings. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;my_strlcat()&quot;</code> appends string <code>&quot;src&quot;</code> to the end of <code>&quot;dst&quot;</code>. It will append at most <code>&quot;size - strlen(dst) - 1&quot;</code> characters. It will then <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminate, unless <code>&quot;size&quot;</code> is 0 or the original <code>&quot;dst&quot;</code> string was longer than <code>&quot;size&quot;</code> (in practice this should not happen as it means that either <code>&quot;size&quot;</code> is incorrect or that <code>&quot;dst&quot;</code> is not a proper <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated string). </p>
<p>Note that <code>&quot;size&quot;</code> is the full size of the destination buffer and the result is guaranteed to be <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated if there is room. Note that room for the <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> should be included in <code>&quot;size&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>The return value is the total length that <code>&quot;dst&quot;</code> would have if <code>&quot;size&quot;</code> is sufficiently large. Thus it is the initial length of <code>&quot;dst&quot;</code> plus the length of <code>&quot;src&quot;</code>. If <code>&quot;size&quot;</code> is smaller than the return, the excess was not appended. </p>
<pre><code>        Size_t my_strlcat(char *dst, const char *src,
                           Size_t size)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>my_strlcpy</dt>
<dd> <p> The C library <code>&quot;strlcpy&quot;</code> if available, or a Perl implementation of it. This operates on C <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated strings. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;my_strlcpy()&quot;</code> copies up to <code>&quot;size - 1&quot;</code> characters from the string <code>&quot;src&quot;</code> to <code>&quot;dst&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminating the result if <code>&quot;size&quot;</code> is not 0. </p>
<p>The return value is the total length <code>&quot;src&quot;</code> would be if the copy completely succeeded. If it is larger than <code>&quot;size&quot;</code>, the excess was not copied. </p>
<pre><code>        Size_t my_strlcpy(char *dst, const char *src,
                           Size_t size)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>my_strnlen</dt>
<dd> <p> The C library <code>&quot;strnlen&quot;</code> if available, or a Perl implementation of it. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;my_strnlen()&quot;</code> computes the length of the string, up to <code>&quot;maxlen&quot;</code> characters. It will will never attempt to address more than <code>&quot;maxlen&quot;</code> characters, making it suitable for use with strings that are not guaranteed to be NUL-terminated. </p>
<pre><code>        Size_t my_strnlen(const char *str, Size_t maxlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>my_vsnprintf</dt>
<dd> <p> The C library <code>&quot;vsnprintf&quot;</code> if available and standards-compliant. However, if if the <code>&quot;vsnprintf&quot;</code> is not available, will unfortunately use the unsafe <code>&quot;vsprintf&quot;</code> which can overrun the buffer (there is an overrun check, but that may be too late). Consider using <code>&quot;sv_vcatpvf&quot;</code> instead, or getting <code>&quot;vsnprintf&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        int my_vsnprintf(char *buffer, const Size_t len,
                             const char *format, va_list ap)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ninstr</dt>
<dd> <p> Find the first (leftmost) occurrence of a sequence of bytes within another sequence. This is the Perl version of <code>&quot;strstr()&quot;</code>, extended to handle arbitrary sequences, potentially containing embedded <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters (<code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> is what the initial <code>&quot;n&quot;</code> in the function name stands for; some systems have an equivalent, <code>&quot;memmem()&quot;</code>, but with a somewhat different API). </p>
<p>Another way of thinking about this function is finding a needle in a haystack. <code>&quot;big&quot;</code> points to the first byte in the haystack. <code>&quot;big_end&quot;</code> points to one byte beyond the final byte in the haystack. <code>&quot;little&quot;</code> points to the first byte in the needle. <code>&quot;little_end&quot;</code> points to one byte beyond the final byte in the needle. All the parameters must be non-<code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>The function returns <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> if there is no occurrence of <code>&quot;little&quot;</code> within <code>&quot;big&quot;</code>. If <code>&quot;little&quot;</code> is the empty string, <code>&quot;big&quot;</code> is returned. </p>
<p>Because this function operates at the byte level, and because of the inherent characteristics of UTF-8 (or UTF-EBCDIC), it will work properly if both the needle and the haystack are strings with the same UTF-8ness, but not if the UTF-8ness differs. </p>
<pre><code>        char * ninstr(char * big, char * bigend, char * little,
                       char * little_end)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PERL_SYS_INIT</dt>
<dd> <p> Provides system-specific tune up of the C runtime environment necessary to run Perl interpreters. This should be called only once, before creating any Perl interpreters. </p>
<pre><code>        void PERL_SYS_INIT(int *argc, char*** argv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PERL_SYS_INIT3</dt>
<dd> <p> Provides system-specific tune up of the C runtime environment necessary to run Perl interpreters. This should be called only once, before creating any Perl interpreters. </p>
<pre><code>        void PERL_SYS_INIT3(int *argc, char*** argv,
                               char*** env)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PERL_SYS_TERM</dt>
<dd> <p> Provides system-specific clean up of the C runtime environment after running Perl interpreters. This should be called only once, after freeing any remaining Perl interpreters. </p>
<pre><code>        void PERL_SYS_TERM()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>quadmath_format_needed</dt>
<dd> <p> <code>&quot;quadmath_format_needed()&quot;</code> returns true if the <code>&quot;format&quot;</code> string seems to contain at least one non-Q-prefixed <code>&quot;%[efgaEFGA]&quot;</code> format specifier, or returns false otherwise. </p>
<p>The format specifier detection is not complete printf-syntax detection, but it should catch most common cases. </p>
<p>If true is returned, those arguments <code>should</code> in theory be processed with <code>&quot;quadmath_snprintf()&quot;</code>, but in case there is more than one such format specifier (see ``quadmath_format_single&#8217;&#8216;), and if there is anything else beyond that one (even just a single byte), they <code>cannot</code> be processed because <code>&quot;quadmath_snprintf()&quot;</code> is very strict, accepting only one format spec, and nothing else. In this case, the code should probably fail. </p>
<pre><code>        bool quadmath_format_needed(const char* format)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>quadmath_format_single</dt>
<dd> <p> <code>&quot;quadmath_snprintf()&quot;</code> is very strict about its <code>&quot;format&quot;</code> string and will fail, returning -1, if the format is invalid. It accepts exactly one format spec. </p>
