<p> <code>&quot;quadmath_format_single()&quot;</code> checks that the intended single spec looks sane: begins with <code>&quot;%&quot;</code>, has only one <code>&quot;%&quot;</code>, ends with <code>&quot;[efgaEFGA]&quot;</code>, and has <code>&quot;Q&quot;</code> before it. This is not a full ``printf syntax check&#8217;&#8216;, just the basics. </p>
<p>Returns the format if it is valid, NULL if not. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;quadmath_format_single()&quot;</code> can and will actually patch in the missing <code>&quot;Q&quot;</code>, if necessary. In this case it will return the modified copy of the format, <code>which the caller will need to free.</code> </p>
<p>See also ``quadmath_format_needed&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        const char* quadmath_format_single(const char* format)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>READ_XDIGIT</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the value of an ASCII-range hex digit and advances the string pointer. Behaviour is only well defined when isXDIGIT(*str) is true. </p>
<pre><code>        U8 READ_XDIGIT(char str*)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>rninstr</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;ninstr&quot;</code>, but instead finds the final (rightmost) occurrence of a sequence of bytes within another sequence, returning <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> if there is no such occurrence. </p>
<pre><code>        char * rninstr(char * big, char * bigend,
                        char * little, char * little_end)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>strEQ</dt>
<dd> <p> Test two <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated strings to see if they are equal. Returns true or false. </p>
<pre><code>        bool strEQ(char* s1, char* s2)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>strGE</dt>
<dd> <p> Test two <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated strings to see if the first, <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code>, is greater than or equal to the second, <code>&quot;s2&quot;</code>. Returns true or false. </p>
<pre><code>        bool strGE(char* s1, char* s2)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>strGT</dt>
<dd> <p> Test two <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated strings to see if the first, <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code>, is greater than the second, <code>&quot;s2&quot;</code>. Returns true or false. </p>
<pre><code>        bool strGT(char* s1, char* s2)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>strLE</dt>
<dd> <p> Test two <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated strings to see if the first, <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code>, is less than or equal to the second, <code>&quot;s2&quot;</code>. Returns true or false. </p>
<pre><code>        bool strLE(char* s1, char* s2)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>strLT</dt>
<dd> <p> Test two <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated strings to see if the first, <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code>, is less than the second, <code>&quot;s2&quot;</code>. Returns true or false. </p>
<pre><code>        bool strLT(char* s1, char* s2)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>strNE</dt>
<dd> <p> Test two <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated strings to see if they are different. Returns true or false. </p>
<pre><code>        bool strNE(char* s1, char* s2)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>strnEQ</dt>
<dd> <p> Test two <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated strings to see if they are equal. The <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> parameter indicates the number of bytes to compare. Returns true or false. (A wrapper for <code>&quot;strncmp&quot;</code>). </p>
<pre><code>        bool strnEQ(char* s1, char* s2, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>strnNE</dt>
<dd> <p> Test two <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated strings to see if they are different. The <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> parameter indicates the number of bytes to compare. Returns true or false. (A wrapper for <code>&quot;strncmp&quot;</code>). </p>
<pre><code>        bool strnNE(char* s1, char* s2, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_destroyable</dt>
<dd> <p> Dummy routine which reports that object can be destroyed when there is no sharing module present. It ignores its single SV argument, and returns &#8216;true&#8217;. Exists to avoid test for a <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> function pointer and because it could potentially warn under some level of strict-ness. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_destroyable(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_nosharing</dt>
<dd> <p> Dummy routine which ``shares&#8217;&#8216; an SV when there is no sharing module present. Or ``locks&#8217;&#8216; it. Or ``unlocks&#8217;&#8216; it. In other words, ignores its single SV argument. Exists to avoid test for a <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> function pointer and because it could potentially warn under some level of strict-ness. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_nosharing(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>vmess</dt>
<dd> <p> <code>&quot;pat&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;args&quot;</code> are a sprintf-style format pattern and encapsulated argument list, respectively. These are used to generate a string message. If the message does not end with a newline, then it will be extended with some indication of the current location in the code, as described for ``mess_sv&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>Normally, the resulting message is returned in a new mortal SV. During global destruction a single SV may be shared between uses of this function. </p>
<pre><code>        SV * vmess(const char *pat, va_list *args)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>MRO Functions</h2>
<p> These functions are related to the method resolution order of perl classes </p>
<dl>
<dt>mro_get_linear_isa</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the mro linearisation for the given stash. By default, this will be whatever <code>&quot;mro_get_linear_isa_dfs&quot;</code> returns unless some other MRO is in effect for the stash. The return value is a read-only AV*. </p>
<p>You are responsible for <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc()&quot;</code> on the return value if you plan to store it anywhere semi-permanently (otherwise it might be deleted out from under you the next time the cache is invalidated). </p>
<pre><code>        AV* mro_get_linear_isa(HV* stash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mro_method_changed_in</dt>
<dd> <p> Invalidates method caching on any child classes of the given stash, so that they might notice the changes in this one. </p>
<p>Ideally, all instances of <code>&quot;PL_sub_generation++&quot;</code> in perl source outside of <i>mro.c</i> should be replaced by calls to this. </p>
<p>Perl automatically handles most of the common ways a method might be redefined. However, there are a few ways you could change a method in a stash without the cache code noticing, in which case you need to call this method afterwards: </p>
<p>1) Directly manipulating the stash HV entries from XS code. </p>
<p>2) Assigning a reference to a readonly scalar constant into a stash entry in order to create a constant subroutine (like <i>constant.pm</i> does). </p>
<p>This same method is available from pure perl via, <code>&quot;mro::method_changed_in(classname)&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mro_method_changed_in(HV* stash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mro_register</dt>
<dd> <p> Registers a custom mro plugin. See perlmroapi for details. </p>
<pre><code>        void mro_register(const struct mro_alg *mro)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Multicall Functions</h2>
<dl>
<dt>dMULTICALL</dt>
<dd> <p> Declare local variables for a multicall. See ``LIGHTWEIGHT CALLBACKS&#8217;&#8216; in perlcall. </p>
<pre><code>                dMULTICALL;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>MULTICALL</dt>
<dd> <p> Make a lightweight callback. See ``LIGHTWEIGHT CALLBACKS&#8217;&#8216; in perlcall. </p>
<pre><code>                MULTICALL;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>POP_MULTICALL</dt>
<dd> <p> Closing bracket for a lightweight callback. See ``LIGHTWEIGHT CALLBACKS&#8217;&#8216; in perlcall. </p>
<pre><code>                POP_MULTICALL;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PUSH_MULTICALL</dt>
<dd> <p> Opening bracket for a lightweight callback. See ``LIGHTWEIGHT CALLBACKS&#8217;&#8216; in perlcall. </p>
<pre><code>                PUSH_MULTICALL;
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Numeric functions</h2>
<dl>
<dt>grok_bin</dt>
<dd> <p> converts a string representing a binary number to numeric form. </p>
<p>On entry <code>&quot;start&quot;</code> and <code>*len</code> give the string to scan, <code>*flags</code> gives conversion flags, and <code>&quot;result&quot;</code> should be <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> or a pointer to an NV. The scan stops at the end of the string, or the first invalid character. Unless <code>&quot;PERL_SCAN_SILENT_ILLDIGIT&quot;</code> is set in <code>*flags</code>, encountering an invalid character will also trigger a warning. On return <code>*len</code> is set to the length of the scanned string, and <code>*flags</code> gives output flags. </p>
<p>If the value is &lt;= <code>&quot;UV_MAX&quot;</code> it is returned as a UV, the output flags are clear, and nothing is written to <code>*result</code>. If the value is &gt; <code>&quot;UV_MAX&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;grok_bin&quot;</code> returns <code>&quot;UV_MAX&quot;</code>, sets <code>&quot;PERL_SCAN_GREATER_THAN_UV_MAX&quot;</code> in the output flags, and writes the value to <code>*result</code> (or the value is discarded if <code>&quot;result&quot;</code> is NULL). </p>
<p>The binary number may optionally be prefixed with <code>&quot;0b&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;b&quot;</code> unless <code>&quot;PERL_SCAN_DISALLOW_PREFIX&quot;</code> is set in <code>*flags</code> on entry. If <code>&quot;PERL_SCAN_ALLOW_UNDERSCORES&quot;</code> is set in <code>*flags</code> then the binary number may use <code>&quot;_&quot;</code> characters to separate digits. </p>
<pre><code>        UV grok_bin(const char* start, STRLEN* len_p,
                         I32* flags, NV *result)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>grok_hex</dt>
<dd> <p> converts a string representing a hex number to numeric form. </p>
<p>On entry <code>&quot;start&quot;</code> and <code>*len_p</code> give the string to scan, <code>*flags</code> gives conversion flags, and <code>&quot;result&quot;</code> should be <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> or a pointer to an NV. The scan stops at the end of the string, or the first invalid character. Unless <code>&quot;PERL_SCAN_SILENT_ILLDIGIT&quot;</code> is set in <code>*flags</code>, encountering an invalid character will also trigger a warning. On return <code>*len</code> is set to the length of the scanned string, and <code>*flags</code> gives output flags. </p>
<p>If the value is &lt;= <code>&quot;UV_MAX&quot;</code> it is returned as a UV, the output flags are clear, and nothing is written to <code>*result</code>. If the value is &gt; <code>&quot;UV_MAX&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;grok_hex&quot;</code> returns <code>&quot;UV_MAX&quot;</code>, sets <code>&quot;PERL_SCAN_GREATER_THAN_UV_MAX&quot;</code> in the output flags, and writes the value to <code>*result</code> (or the value is discarded if <code>&quot;result&quot;</code> is <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>). </p>
<p>The hex number may optionally be prefixed with <code>&quot;0x&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;x&quot;</code> unless <code>&quot;PERL_SCAN_DISALLOW_PREFIX&quot;</code> is set in <code>*flags</code> on entry. If <code>&quot;PERL_SCAN_ALLOW_UNDERSCORES&quot;</code> is set in <code>*flags</code> then the hex number may use <code>&quot;_&quot;</code> characters to separate digits. </p>
<pre><code>        UV grok_hex(const char* start, STRLEN* len_p,
                         I32* flags, NV *result)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>grok_infnan</dt>
<dd> <p> Helper for <code>&quot;grok_number()&quot;</code>, accepts various ways of spelling ``infinity&#8217;&#8216; or ``not a number&#8217;&#8216;, and returns one of the following flag combinations: </p>
<pre><code>  IS_NUMBER_INFINITY
  IS_NUMBER_NAN
  IS_NUMBER_INFINITY | IS_NUMBER_NEG
  IS_NUMBER_NAN | IS_NUMBER_NEG
  0
</code></pre>
<p>possibly |-ed with <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_TRAILING&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>If an infinity or a not-a-number is recognized, <code>*sp</code> will point to one byte past the end of the recognized string. If the recognition fails, zero is returned, and <code>*sp</code> will not move. </p>
<pre><code>        int grok_infnan(const char** sp, const char *send)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>grok_number</dt>
<dd> <p> Identical to <code>&quot;grok_number_flags()&quot;</code> with <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> set to zero. </p>
<pre><code>        int grok_number(const char *pv, STRLEN len,
                            UV *valuep)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>grok_number_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Recognise (or not) a number. The type of the number is returned (0 if unrecognised), otherwise it is a bit-ORed combination of <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_IN_UV&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_GREATER_THAN_UV_MAX&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_NOT_INT&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_NEG&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_INFINITY&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_NAN&quot;</code> (defined in perl.h). </p>
<p>If the value of the number can fit in a UV, it is returned in <code>*valuep</code>. <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_IN_UV&quot;</code> will be set to indicate that <code>*valuep</code> is valid, <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_IN_UV&quot;</code> will never be set unless <code>*valuep</code> is valid, but <code>*valuep</code> may have been assigned to during processing even though <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_IN_UV&quot;</code> is not set on return. If <code>&quot;valuep&quot;</code> is <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_IN_UV&quot;</code> will be set for the same cases as when <code>&quot;valuep&quot;</code> is non-<code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>, but no actual assignment (or SEGV) will occur. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_NOT_INT&quot;</code> will be set with <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_IN_UV&quot;</code> if trailing decimals were seen (in which case <code>*valuep</code> gives the true value truncated to an integer), and <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_NEG&quot;</code> if the number is negative (in which case <code>*valuep</code> holds the absolute value). <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_IN_UV&quot;</code> is not set if e notation was used or the number is larger than a UV. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> allows only <code>&quot;PERL_SCAN_TRAILING&quot;</code>, which allows for trailing non-numeric text on an otherwise successful <i>grok</i>, setting <code>&quot;IS_NUMBER_TRAILING&quot;</code> on the result. </p>
<pre><code>        int grok_number_flags(const char *pv, STRLEN len,
                                  UV *valuep, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>grok_numeric_radix</dt>
<dd> <p> Scan and skip for a numeric decimal separator (radix). </p>
<pre><code>        bool grok_numeric_radix(const char **sp,
                                   const char *send)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>grok_oct</dt>
<dd> <p> converts a string representing an octal number to numeric form. </p>
<p>On entry <code>&quot;start&quot;</code> and <code>*len</code> give the string to scan, <code>*flags</code> gives conversion flags, and <code>&quot;result&quot;</code> should be <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> or a pointer to an NV. The scan stops at the end of the string, or the first invalid character. Unless <code>&quot;PERL_SCAN_SILENT_ILLDIGIT&quot;</code> is set in <code>*flags</code>, encountering an 8 or 9 will also trigger a warning. On return <code>*len</code> is set to the length of the scanned string, and <code>*flags</code> gives output flags. </p>
<p>If the value is &lt;= <code>&quot;UV_MAX&quot;</code> it is returned as a UV, the output flags are clear, and nothing is written to <code>*result</code>. If the value is &gt; <code>&quot;UV_MAX&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;grok_oct&quot;</code> returns <code>&quot;UV_MAX&quot;</code>, sets <code>&quot;PERL_SCAN_GREATER_THAN_UV_MAX&quot;</code> in the output flags, and writes the value to <code>*result</code> (or the value is discarded if <code>&quot;result&quot;</code> is <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>). </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;PERL_SCAN_ALLOW_UNDERSCORES&quot;</code> is set in <code>*flags</code> then the octal number may use <code>&quot;_&quot;</code> characters to separate digits. </p>
<pre><code>        UV grok_oct(const char* start, STRLEN* len_p,
                         I32* flags, NV *result)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>isinfnan</dt>
<dd> <p> <code>&quot;Perl_isinfnan()&quot;</code> is utility function that returns true if the NV argument is either an infinity or a <code>&quot;NaN&quot;</code>, false otherwise. To test in more detail, use <code>&quot;Perl_isinf()&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;Perl_isnan()&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>This is also the logical inverse of <code>Perl_isfinite()</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool isinfnan(NV nv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>my_strtod</dt>
<dd> <p> This function is equivalent to the libc <code>strtod()</code> function, and is available even on platforms that lack plain <code>strtod()</code>. Its return value is the best available precision depending on platform capabilities and <i>Configure</i> options. </p>
<p>It properly handles the locale radix character, meaning it expects a dot except when called from within the scope of <code>&quot;use locale&quot;</code>, in which case the radix character should be that specified by the current locale. </p>
<p>The synonym <code>Strod()</code> may be used instead. </p>
<pre><code>        NV my_strtod(const char * const s, char ** e)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>Perl_signbit</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Return a non-zero integer if the sign bit on an NV is set, and 0 if it is not. </p>
<p>If <i>Configure</i> detects this system has a <code>&quot;signbit()&quot;</code> that will work with our NVs, then we just use it via the <code>&quot;#define&quot;</code> in <i>perl.h</i>. Otherwise, fall back on this implementation. The main use of this function is catching <code>&quot;-0.0&quot;</code>. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;Configure&quot;</code> notes: This function is called <code>&#8216;Perl_signbit&#8217;</code> instead of a plain <code>&#8216;signbit&#8217;</code> because it is easy to imagine a system having a <code>&quot;signbit()&quot;</code> function or macro that doesn&#8217;t happen to work with our particular choice of NVs. We shouldn&#8217;t just re-<code>&quot;#define&quot;</code> <code>&quot;signbit&quot;</code> as <code>&quot;Perl_signbit&quot;</code> and expect the standard system headers to be happy. Also, this is a no-context function (no <code>&quot;pTHX_&quot;</code>) because <code>&quot;Perl_signbit()&quot;</code> is usually re-<code>&quot;#defined&quot;</code> in <i>perl.h</i> as a simple macro call to the system&#8217;s <code>&quot;signbit()&quot;</code>. Users should just always call <code>&quot;Perl_signbit()&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        int Perl_signbit(NV f)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>scan_bin</dt>
<dd> <p> For backwards compatibility. Use <code>&quot;grok_bin&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        NV scan_bin(const char* start, STRLEN len,
                         STRLEN* retlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>scan_hex</dt>
<dd> <p> For backwards compatibility. Use <code>&quot;grok_hex&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        NV scan_hex(const char* start, STRLEN len,
                         STRLEN* retlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>scan_oct</dt>
<dd> <p> For backwards compatibility. Use <code>&quot;grok_oct&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        NV scan_oct(const char* start, STRLEN len,
                         STRLEN* retlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Obsolete backwards compatibility functions</h2>
<p> Some of these are also deprecated. You can exclude these from your compiled Perl by adding this option to Configure: <code>&quot;-Accflags=&#8216;-DNO_MATHOMS&#8217;&quot;</code> </p>
<dl>
<dt>custom_op_desc</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the description of a given custom op. This was once used by the <code>&quot;OP_DESC&quot;</code> macro, but is no longer: it has only been kept for compatibility, and should not be used. </p>
<pre><code>        const char * custom_op_desc(const OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>custom_op_name</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the name for a given custom op. This was once used by the <code>&quot;OP_NAME&quot;</code> macro, but is no longer: it has only been kept for compatibility, and should not be used. </p>
<pre><code>        const char * custom_op_name(const OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>gv_fetchmethod</dt>
<dd> <p> See ``gv_fetchmethod_autoload&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        GV* gv_fetchmethod(HV* stash, const char* name)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_char</dt>
<dd> <p> DEPRECATED! It is planned to remove this function from a future release of Perl. Do not use it for new code; remove it from existing code. </p>
<p>Tests if some arbitrary number of bytes begins in a valid UTF-8 character. Note that an INVARIANT (i.e. ASCII on non-EBCDIC machines) character is a valid UTF-8 character. The actual number of bytes in the UTF-8 character will be returned if it is valid, otherwise 0. </p>
<p>This function is deprecated due to the possibility that malformed input could cause reading beyond the end of the input buffer. Use ``isUTF8_CHAR&#8217;&#8216; instead. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN is_utf8_char(const U8 *s)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_char_buf</dt>
<dd> <p> This is identical to the macro ``isUTF8_CHAR&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN is_utf8_char_buf(const U8 *buf,
                                 const U8 *buf_end)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pack_cat</dt>
<dd> <p> The engine implementing <code>&quot;pack()&quot;</code> Perl function. Note: parameters <code>&quot;next_in_list&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> are not used. This call should not be used; use <code>&quot;packlist&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        void pack_cat(SV *cat, const char *pat,
                         const char *patend, SV **beglist,
                         SV **endlist, SV ***next_in_list,
                         U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_compname_type</dt>
<dd> <p> Looks up the type of the lexical variable at position <code>&quot;po&quot;</code> in the currently-compiling pad. If the variable is typed, the stash of the class to which it is typed is returned. If not, <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> is returned. </p>
<pre><code>        HV * pad_compname_type(PADOFFSET po)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2pvbyte_nolen</dt>
<dd> <p> Return a pointer to the byte-encoded representation of the SV. May cause the SV to be downgraded from UTF-8 as a side-effect. </p>
<p>Usually accessed via the <code>&quot;SvPVbyte_nolen&quot;</code> macro. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_2pvbyte_nolen(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2pvutf8_nolen</dt>
<dd> <p> Return a pointer to the UTF-8-encoded representation of the SV. May cause the SV to be upgraded to UTF-8 as a side-effect. </p>
<p>Usually accessed via the <code>&quot;SvPVutf8_nolen&quot;</code> macro. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_2pvutf8_nolen(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2pv_nolen</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_2pv()&quot;</code>, but doesn&#8217;t return the length too. You should usually use the macro wrapper <code>&quot;SvPV_nolen(sv)&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_2pv_nolen(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpvn_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_catpvn&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpvn_mg(SV *sv, const char *ptr,
                             STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catsv_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_catsv&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catsv_mg(SV *dsv, SV *ssv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_force_normal</dt>
<dd> <p> Undo various types of fakery on an SV: if the PV is a shared string, make a private copy; if we&#8217;re a ref, stop refing; if we&#8217;re a glob, downgrade to an <code>&quot;xpvmg&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;sv_force_normal_flags&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_force_normal(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_iv</dt>
<dd> <p> A private implementation of the <code>&quot;SvIVx&quot;</code> macro for compilers which can&#8217;t cope with complex macro expressions. Always use the macro instead. </p>
<pre><code>        IV sv_iv(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_nolocking</dt>
<dd> <p> Dummy routine which ``locks&#8217;&#8216; an SV when there is no locking module present. Exists to avoid test for a <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> function pointer and because it could potentially warn under some level of strict-ness. </p>
<p>``Superseded&#8217;&#8216; by <code>&quot;sv_nosharing()&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_nolocking(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_nounlocking</dt>
<dd> <p> Dummy routine which ``unlocks&#8217;&#8216; an SV when there is no locking module present. Exists to avoid test for a <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> function pointer and because it could potentially warn under some level of strict-ness. </p>
<p>``Superseded&#8217;&#8216; by <code>&quot;sv_nosharing()&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_nounlocking(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_nv</dt>
<dd> <p> A private implementation of the <code>&quot;SvNVx&quot;</code> macro for compilers which can&#8217;t cope with complex macro expressions. Always use the macro instead. </p>
<pre><code>        NV sv_nv(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pv</dt>
<dd> <p> Use the <code>&quot;SvPV_nolen&quot;</code> macro instead </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_pv(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pvbyte</dt>
<dd> <p> Use <code>&quot;SvPVbyte_nolen&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_pvbyte(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pvbyten</dt>
<dd> <p> A private implementation of the <code>&quot;SvPVbyte&quot;</code> macro for compilers which can&#8217;t cope with complex macro expressions. Always use the macro instead. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_pvbyten(SV *sv, STRLEN *lp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pvn</dt>
<dd> <p> A private implementation of the <code>&quot;SvPV&quot;</code> macro for compilers which can&#8217;t cope with complex macro expressions. Always use the macro instead. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_pvn(SV *sv, STRLEN *lp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pvutf8</dt>
<dd> <p> Use the <code>&quot;SvPVutf8_nolen&quot;</code> macro instead </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_pvutf8(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pvutf8n</dt>
<dd> <p> A private implementation of the <code>&quot;SvPVutf8&quot;</code> macro for compilers which can&#8217;t cope with complex macro expressions. Always use the macro instead. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_pvutf8n(SV *sv, STRLEN *lp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_taint</dt>
<dd> <p> Taint an SV. Use <code>&quot;SvTAINTED_on&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_taint(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_unref</dt>
<dd> <p> Unsets the RV status of the SV, and decrements the reference count of whatever was being referenced by the RV. This can almost be thought of as a reversal of <code>&quot;newSVrv&quot;</code>. This is <code>&quot;sv_unref_flags&quot;</code> with the <code>&quot;flag&quot;</code> being zero. See <code>&quot;SvROK_off&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_unref(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_usepvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells an SV to use <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code> to find its string value. Implemented by calling <code>&quot;sv_usepvn_flags&quot;</code> with <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> of 0, hence does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See <code>&quot;sv_usepvn_flags&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_usepvn(SV* sv, char* ptr, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_usepvn_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_usepvn&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_usepvn_mg(SV *sv, char *ptr, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_uv</dt>
<dd> <p> A private implementation of the <code>&quot;SvUVx&quot;</code> macro for compilers which can&#8217;t cope with complex macro expressions. Always use the macro instead. </p>
<pre><code>        UV sv_uv(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>unpack_str</dt>
<dd> <p> The engine implementing <code>&quot;unpack()&quot;</code> Perl function. Note: parameters <code>&quot;strbeg&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;new_s&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;ocnt&quot;</code> are not used. This call should not be used, use <code>&quot;unpackstring&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        SSize_t unpack_str(const char *pat, const char *patend,
                           const char *s, const char *strbeg,
                           const char *strend, char **new_s,
                           I32 ocnt, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8_to_uvuni</dt>
<dd> <p> DEPRECATED! It is planned to remove this function from a future release of Perl. Do not use it for new code; remove it from existing code. </p>
<p>Returns the Unicode code point of the first character in the string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> which is assumed to be in UTF-8 encoding; <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> will be set to the length, in bytes, of that character. </p>
<p>Some, but not all, UTF-8 malformations are detected, and in fact, some malformed input could cause reading beyond the end of the input buffer, which is one reason why this function is deprecated. The other is that only in extremely limited circumstances should the Unicode versus native code point be of any interest to you. See ``utf8_to_uvuni_buf&#8217;&#8216; for alternatives. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> points to one of the detected malformations, and UTF8 warnings are enabled, zero is returned and <code>*retlen</code> is set (if <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> doesn&#8217;t point to NULL) to -1. If those warnings are off, the computed value if well-defined (or the Unicode REPLACEMENT CHARACTER, if not) is silently returned, and <code>*retlen</code> is set (if <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> isn&#8217;t NULL) so that (<code>&quot;s&quot;</code> + <code>*retlen</code>) is the next possible position in <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> that could begin a non-malformed character. See ``utf8n_to_uvchr&#8217;&#8216; for details on when the REPLACEMENT CHARACTER is returned. </p>
<pre><code>        UV utf8_to_uvuni(const U8 *s, STRLEN *retlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Optree construction</h2>
<dl>
<dt>newASSIGNOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an assignment op. <code>&quot;left&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;right&quot;</code> supply the parameters of the assignment; they are consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;optype&quot;</code> is <code>&quot;OP_ANDASSIGN&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;OP_ORASSIGN&quot;</code>, or <code>&quot;OP_DORASSIGN&quot;</code>, then a suitable conditional optree is constructed. If <code>&quot;optype&quot;</code> is the opcode of a binary operator, such as <code>&quot;OP_BIT_OR&quot;</code>, then an op is constructed that performs the binary operation and assigns the result to the left argument. Either way, if <code>&quot;optype&quot;</code> is non-zero then <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has no effect. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;optype&quot;</code> is zero, then a plain scalar or list assignment is constructed. Which type of assignment it is is automatically determined. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>, except that <code>&quot;OPf_KIDS&quot;</code> will be set automatically, and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code>, except that the bit with value 1 or 2 is automatically set as required. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newASSIGNOP(I32 flags, OP *left, I32 optype,
                            OP *right)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newBINOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op of any binary type. <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>, except that <code>&quot;OPf_KIDS&quot;</code> will be set automatically, and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code>, except that the bit with value 1 or 2 is automatically set as required. <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> supply up to two ops to be the direct children of the binary op; they are consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newBINOP(I32 type, I32 flags, OP *first,
                         OP *last)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newCONDOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns a conditional-expression (<code>&quot;cond_expr&quot;</code>) op. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>, except that <code>&quot;OPf_KIDS&quot;</code> will be set automatically, and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code>, except that the bit with value 1 is automatically set. <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> supplies the expression selecting between the two branches, and <code>&quot;trueop&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;falseop&quot;</code> supply the branches; they are consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newCONDOP(I32 flags, OP *first, OP *trueop,
                          OP *falseop)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newDEFSVOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs and returns an op to access <code>$_</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newDEFSVOP()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newFOROP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op tree expressing a <code>&quot;foreach&quot;</code> loop (iteration through a list of values). This is a heavyweight loop, with structure that allows exiting the loop by <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> and suchlike. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> optionally supplies the variable that will be aliased to each item in turn; if null, it defaults to <code>$_</code>. <code>&quot;expr&quot;</code> supplies the list of values to iterate over. <code>&quot;block&quot;</code> supplies the main body of the loop, and <code>&quot;cont&quot;</code> optionally supplies a <code>&quot;continue&quot;</code> block that operates as a second half of the body. All of these optree inputs are consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code> for the <code>&quot;leaveloop&quot;</code> op and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code> for the <code>&quot;leaveloop&quot;</code> op, except that (in both cases) some bits will be set automatically. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newFOROP(I32 flags, OP *sv, OP *expr, OP *block,
                         OP *cont)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newGIVENOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op tree expressing a <code>&quot;given&quot;</code> block. <code>&quot;cond&quot;</code> supplies the expression to whose value <code>$_</code> will be locally aliased, and <code>&quot;block&quot;</code> supplies the body of the <code>&quot;given&quot;</code> construct; they are consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. <code>&quot;defsv_off&quot;</code> must be zero (it used to identity the pad slot of lexical <code>$_</code>). </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newGIVENOP(OP *cond, OP *block,
                           PADOFFSET defsv_off)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newGVOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op of any type that involves an embedded reference to a GV. <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>. <code>&quot;gv&quot;</code> identifies the GV that the op should reference; calling this function does not transfer ownership of any reference to it. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newGVOP(I32 type, I32 flags, GV *gv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newLISTOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op of any list type. <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>, except that <code>&quot;OPf_KIDS&quot;</code> will be set automatically if required. <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> supply up to two ops to be direct children of the list op; they are consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. </p>
<p>For most list operators, the check function expects all the kid ops to be present already, so calling <code>&quot;newLISTOP(OP_JOIN, ...)&quot;</code> (e.g.) is not appropriate. What you want to do in that case is create an op of type <code>&quot;OP_LIST&quot;</code>, append more children to it, and then call ``op_convert_list&#8217;&#8216;. See ``op_convert_list&#8217;&#8216; for more information. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newLISTOP(I32 type, I32 flags, OP *first,
                          OP *last)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newLOGOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns a logical (flow control) op. <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>, except that <code>&quot;OPf_KIDS&quot;</code> will be set automatically, and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code>, except that the bit with value 1 is automatically set. <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> supplies the expression controlling the flow, and <code>&quot;other&quot;</code> supplies the side (alternate) chain of ops; they are consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newLOGOP(I32 type, I32 flags, OP *first,
                         OP *other)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newLOOPEX</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns a loop-exiting op (such as <code>&quot;goto&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;last&quot;</code>). <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;label&quot;</code> supplies the parameter determining the target of the op; it is consumed by this function and becomes part of the constructed op tree. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newLOOPEX(I32 type, OP *label)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newLOOPOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op tree expressing a loop. This is only a loop in the control flow through the op tree; it does not have the heavyweight loop structure that allows exiting the loop by <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> and suchlike. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code> for the top-level op, except that some bits will be set automatically as required. <code>&quot;expr&quot;</code> supplies the expression controlling loop iteration, and <code>&quot;block&quot;</code> supplies the body of the loop; they are consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. <code>&quot;debuggable&quot;</code> is currently unused and should always be 1. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newLOOPOP(I32 flags, I32 debuggable, OP *expr,
                          OP *block)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newMETHOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op of method type with a method name evaluated at runtime. <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>, except that <code>&quot;OPf_KIDS&quot;</code> will be set automatically, and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code>, except that the bit with value 1 is automatically set. <code>&quot;dynamic_meth&quot;</code> supplies an op which evaluates method name; it is consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. Supported optypes: <code>&quot;OP_METHOD&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newMETHOP(I32 type, I32 flags, OP *first)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newMETHOP_named</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op of method type with a constant method name. <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>, and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code>. <code>&quot;const_meth&quot;</code> supplies a constant method name; it must be a shared COW string. Supported optypes: <code>&quot;OP_METHOD_NAMED&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newMETHOP_named(I32 type, I32 flags,
                                SV *const_meth)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newNULLLIST</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns a new <code>&quot;stub&quot;</code> op, which represents an empty list expression. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newNULLLIST()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op of any base type (any type that has no extra fields). <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>, and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newOP(I32 type, I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newPADOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op of any type that involves a reference to a pad element. <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>. A pad slot is automatically allocated, and is populated with <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>; this function takes ownership of one reference to it. </p>
<p>This function only exists if Perl has been compiled to use ithreads. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newPADOP(I32 type, I32 flags, SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newPMOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op of any pattern matching type. <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code> and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newPMOP(I32 type, I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newPVOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op of any type that involves an embedded C-level pointer (PV). <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>. <code>&quot;pv&quot;</code> supplies the C-level pointer. Depending on the op type, the memory referenced by <code>&quot;pv&quot;</code> may be freed when the op is destroyed. If the op is of a freeing type, <code>&quot;pv&quot;</code> must have been allocated using <code>&quot;PerlMemShared_malloc&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newPVOP(I32 type, I32 flags, char *pv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newRANGE</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs and returns a <code>&quot;range&quot;</code> op, with subordinate <code>&quot;flip&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;flop&quot;</code> ops. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code> for the <code>&quot;flip&quot;</code> op and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code> for both the <code>&quot;flip&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;range&quot;</code> ops, except that the bit with value 1 is automatically set. <code>&quot;left&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;right&quot;</code> supply the expressions controlling the endpoints of the range; they are consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newRANGE(I32 flags, OP *left, OP *right)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSLICEOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an <code>&quot;lslice&quot;</code> (list slice) op. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>, except that <code>&quot;OPf_KIDS&quot;</code> will be set automatically, and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code>, except that the bit with value 1 or 2 is automatically set as required. <code>&quot;listval&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;subscript&quot;</code> supply the parameters of the slice; they are consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newSLICEOP(I32 flags, OP *subscript,
                           OP *listval)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSTATEOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs a state op (COP). The state op is normally a <code>&quot;nextstate&quot;</code> op, but will be a <code>&quot;dbstate&quot;</code> op if debugging is enabled for currently-compiled code. The state op is populated from <code>&quot;PL_curcop&quot;</code> (or <code>&quot;PL_compiling&quot;</code>). If <code>&quot;label&quot;</code> is non-null, it supplies the name of a label to attach to the state op; this function takes ownership of the memory pointed at by <code>&quot;label&quot;</code>, and will free it. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code> for the state op. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;o&quot;</code> is null, the state op is returned. Otherwise the state op is combined with <code>&quot;o&quot;</code> into a <code>&quot;lineseq&quot;</code> list op, which is returned. <code>&quot;o&quot;</code> is consumed by this function and becomes part of the returned op tree. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newSTATEOP(I32 flags, char *label, OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op of any type that involves an embedded SV. <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>. <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> gives the SV to embed in the op; this function takes ownership of one reference to it. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newSVOP(I32 type, I32 flags, SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newUNOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op of any unary type. <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> is the opcode. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code>, except that <code>&quot;OPf_KIDS&quot;</code> will be set automatically if required, and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code>, except that the bit with value 1 is automatically set. <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> supplies an optional op to be the direct child of the unary op; it is consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newUNOP(I32 type, I32 flags, OP *first)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newUNOP_AUX</dt>
<dd> <p> Similar to <code>&quot;newUNOP&quot;</code>, but creates an <code>&quot;UNOP_AUX&quot;</code> struct instead, with <code>&quot;op_aux&quot;</code> initialised to <code>&quot;aux&quot;</code> </p>
<pre><code>        OP* newUNOP_AUX(I32 type, I32 flags, OP* first,
                            UNOP_AUX_item *aux)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newWHENOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op tree expressing a <code>&quot;when&quot;</code> block. <code>&quot;cond&quot;</code> supplies the test expression, and <code>&quot;block&quot;</code> supplies the block that will be executed if the test evaluates to true; they are consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. <code>&quot;cond&quot;</code> will be interpreted DWIMically, often as a comparison against <code>$_</code>, and may be null to generate a <code>&quot;default&quot;</code> block. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newWHENOP(OP *cond, OP *block)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newWHILEOP</dt>
<dd> <p> Constructs, checks, and returns an op tree expressing a <code>&quot;while&quot;</code> loop. This is a heavyweight loop, with structure that allows exiting the loop by <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> and suchlike. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;loop&quot;</code> is an optional preconstructed <code>&quot;enterloop&quot;</code> op to use in the loop; if it is null then a suitable op will be constructed automatically. <code>&quot;expr&quot;</code> supplies the loop&#8217;s controlling expression. <code>&quot;block&quot;</code> supplies the main body of the loop, and <code>&quot;cont&quot;</code> optionally supplies a <code>&quot;continue&quot;</code> block that operates as a second half of the body. All of these optree inputs are consumed by this function and become part of the constructed op tree. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> gives the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_flags&quot;</code> for the <code>&quot;leaveloop&quot;</code> op and, shifted up eight bits, the eight bits of <code>&quot;op_private&quot;</code> for the <code>&quot;leaveloop&quot;</code> op, except that (in both cases) some bits will be set automatically. <code>&quot;debuggable&quot;</code> is currently unused and should always be 1. <code>&quot;has_my&quot;</code> can be supplied as true to force the loop body to be enclosed in its own scope. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * newWHILEOP(I32 flags, I32 debuggable,
                           LOOP *loop, OP *expr, OP *block,
                           OP *cont, I32 has_my)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Optree Manipulation Functions</h2>
<dl>
<dt>alloccopstash</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Available only under threaded builds, this function allocates an entry in <code>&quot;PL_stashpad&quot;</code> for the stash passed to it. </p>
<pre><code>        PADOFFSET alloccopstash(HV *hv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>block_end</dt>
<dd> <p> Handles compile-time scope exit. <code>&quot;floor&quot;</code> is the savestack index returned by <code>&quot;block_start&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;seq&quot;</code> is the body of the block. Returns the block, possibly modified. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * block_end(I32 floor, OP *seq)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>block_start</dt>
<dd> <p> Handles compile-time scope entry. Arranges for hints to be restored on block exit and also handles pad sequence numbers to make lexical variables scope right. Returns a savestack index for use with <code>&quot;block_end&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        int block_start(int full)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ck_entersub_args_list</dt>
<dd> <p> Performs the default fixup of the arguments part of an <code>&quot;entersub&quot;</code> op tree. This consists of applying list context to each of the argument ops. This is the standard treatment used on a call marked with <code>&quot;&amp;&quot;</code>, or a method call, or a call through a subroutine reference, or any other call where the callee can&#8217;t be identified at compile time, or a call where the callee has no prototype. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * ck_entersub_args_list(OP *entersubop)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ck_entersub_args_proto</dt>
<dd> <p> Performs the fixup of the arguments part of an <code>&quot;entersub&quot;</code> op tree based on a subroutine prototype. This makes various modifications to the argument ops, from applying context up to inserting <code>&quot;refgen&quot;</code> ops, and checking the number and syntactic types of arguments, as directed by the prototype. This is the standard treatment used on a subroutine call, not marked with <code>&quot;&amp;&quot;</code>, where the callee can be identified at compile time and has a prototype. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;protosv&quot;</code> supplies the subroutine prototype to be applied to the call. It may be a normal defined scalar, of which the string value will be used. Alternatively, for convenience, it may be a subroutine object (a <code>&quot;CV*&quot;</code> that has been cast to <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code>) which has a prototype. The prototype supplied, in whichever form, does not need to match the actual callee referenced by the op tree. </p>
<p>If the argument ops disagree with the prototype, for example by having an unacceptable number of arguments, a valid op tree is returned anyway. The error is reflected in the parser state, normally resulting in a single exception at the top level of parsing which covers all the compilation errors that occurred. In the error message, the callee is referred to by the name defined by the <code>&quot;namegv&quot;</code> parameter. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * ck_entersub_args_proto(OP *entersubop,
                                       GV *namegv, SV *protosv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ck_entersub_args_proto_or_list</dt>
<dd> <p> Performs the fixup of the arguments part of an <code>&quot;entersub&quot;</code> op tree either based on a subroutine prototype or using default list-context processing. This is the standard treatment used on a subroutine call, not marked with <code>&quot;&amp;&quot;</code>, where the callee can be identified at compile time. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;protosv&quot;</code> supplies the subroutine prototype to be applied to the call, or indicates that there is no prototype. It may be a normal scalar, in which case if it is defined then the string value will be used as a prototype, and if it is undefined then there is no prototype. Alternatively, for convenience, it may be a subroutine object (a <code>&quot;CV*&quot;</code> that has been cast to <code>&quot;SV*&quot;</code>), of which the prototype will be used if it has one. The prototype (or lack thereof) supplied, in whichever form, does not need to match the actual callee referenced by the op tree. </p>
<p>If the argument ops disagree with the prototype, for example by having an unacceptable number of arguments, a valid op tree is returned anyway. The error is reflected in the parser state, normally resulting in a single exception at the top level of parsing which covers all the compilation errors that occurred. In the error message, the callee is referred to by the name defined by the <code>&quot;namegv&quot;</code> parameter. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * ck_entersub_args_proto_or_list(OP *entersubop,
                                               GV *namegv,
                                               SV *protosv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>cv_const_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> If <code>&quot;cv&quot;</code> is a constant sub eligible for inlining, returns the constant value returned by the sub. Otherwise, returns <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Constant subs can be created with <code>&quot;newCONSTSUB&quot;</code> or as described in ``Constant Functions&#8217;&#8216; in perlsub. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* cv_const_sv(const CV *const cv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>cv_get_call_checker</dt>
<dd> <p> The original form of ``cv_get_call_checker_flags&#8217;&#8216;, which does not return checker flags. When using a checker function returned by this function, it is only safe to call it with a genuine GV as its <code>&quot;namegv&quot;</code> argument. </p>
<pre><code>        void cv_get_call_checker(CV *cv,
                                    Perl_call_checker *ckfun_p,
                                    SV **ckobj_p)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>cv_get_call_checker_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Retrieves the function that will be used to fix up a call to <code>&quot;cv&quot;</code>. Specifically, the function is applied to an <code>&quot;entersub&quot;</code> op tree for a subroutine call, not marked with <code>&quot;&amp;&quot;</code>, where the callee can be identified at compile time as <code>&quot;cv&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>The C-level function pointer is returned in <code>*ckfun_p</code>, an SV argument for it is returned in <code>*ckobj_p</code>, and control flags are returned in <code>*ckflags_p</code>. The function is intended to be called in this manner: </p>
<pre><code> entersubop = (*ckfun_p)(aTHX_ entersubop, namegv, (*ckobj_p));
</code></pre>
<p>In this call, <code>&quot;entersubop&quot;</code> is a pointer to the <code>&quot;entersub&quot;</code> op, which may be replaced by the check function, and <code>&quot;namegv&quot;</code> supplies the name that should be used by the check function to refer to the callee of the <code>&quot;entersub&quot;</code> op if it needs to emit any diagnostics. It is permitted to apply the check function in non-standard situations, such as to a call to a different subroutine or to a method call. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;namegv&quot;</code> may not actually be a GV. If the <code>&quot;CALL_CHECKER_REQUIRE_GV&quot;</code> bit is clear in <code>*ckflags_p</code>, it is permitted to pass a CV or other SV instead, anything that can be used as the first argument to ``cv_name&#8217;&#8216;. If the <code>&quot;CALL_CHECKER_REQUIRE_GV&quot;</code> bit is set in <code>*ckflags_p</code> then the check function requires <code>&quot;namegv&quot;</code> to be a genuine GV. </p>
<p>By default, the check function is Perl_ck_entersub_args_proto_or_list, the SV parameter is <code>&quot;cv&quot;</code> itself, and the <code>&quot;CALL_CHECKER_REQUIRE_GV&quot;</code> flag is clear. This implements standard prototype processing. It can be changed, for a particular subroutine, by ``cv_set_call_checker_flags&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>If the <code>&quot;CALL_CHECKER_REQUIRE_GV&quot;</code> bit is set in <code>&quot;gflags&quot;</code> then it indicates that the caller only knows about the genuine GV version of <code>&quot;namegv&quot;</code>, and accordingly the corresponding bit will always be set in <code>*ckflags_p</code>, regardless of the check function&#8217;s recorded requirements. If the <code>&quot;CALL_CHECKER_REQUIRE_GV&quot;</code> bit is clear in <code>&quot;gflags&quot;</code> then it indicates the caller knows about the possibility of passing something other than a GV as <code>&quot;namegv&quot;</code>, and accordingly the corresponding bit may be either set or clear in <code>*ckflags_p</code>, indicating the check function&#8217;s recorded requirements. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;gflags&quot;</code> is a bitset passed into <code>&quot;cv_get_call_checker_flags&quot;</code>, in which only the <code>&quot;CALL_CHECKER_REQUIRE_GV&quot;</code> bit currently has a defined meaning (for which see above). All other bits should be clear. </p>
<pre><code>        void cv_get_call_checker_flags(
                    CV *cv, U32 gflags,
                    Perl_call_checker *ckfun_p, SV **ckobj_p,
                    U32 *ckflags_p
                )
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>cv_set_call_checker</dt>
<dd> <p> The original form of ``cv_set_call_checker_flags&#8217;&#8216;, which passes it the <code>&quot;CALL_CHECKER_REQUIRE_GV&quot;</code> flag for backward-compatibility. The effect of that flag setting is that the check function is guaranteed to get a genuine GV as its <code>&quot;namegv&quot;</code> argument. </p>
<pre><code>        void cv_set_call_checker(CV *cv,
                                    Perl_call_checker ckfun,
                                    SV *ckobj)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>cv_set_call_checker_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Sets the function that will be used to fix up a call to <code>&quot;cv&quot;</code>. Specifically, the function is applied to an <code>&quot;entersub&quot;</code> op tree for a subroutine call, not marked with <code>&quot;&amp;&quot;</code>, where the callee can be identified at compile time as <code>&quot;cv&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>The C-level function pointer is supplied in <code>&quot;ckfun&quot;</code>, an SV argument for it is supplied in <code>&quot;ckobj&quot;</code>, and control flags are supplied in <code>&quot;ckflags&quot;</code>. The function should be defined like this: </p>
<pre><code>    STATIC OP * ckfun(pTHX_ OP *op, GV *namegv, SV *ckobj)
</code></pre>
<p>It is intended to be called in this manner: </p>
<pre><code>    entersubop = ckfun(aTHX_ entersubop, namegv, ckobj);
</code></pre>
<p>In this call, <code>&quot;entersubop&quot;</code> is a pointer to the <code>&quot;entersub&quot;</code> op, which may be replaced by the check function, and <code>&quot;namegv&quot;</code> supplies the name that should be used by the check function to refer to the callee of the <code>&quot;entersub&quot;</code> op if it needs to emit any diagnostics. It is permitted to apply the check function in non-standard situations, such as to a call to a different subroutine or to a method call. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;namegv&quot;</code> may not actually be a GV. For efficiency, perl may pass a CV or other SV instead. Whatever is passed can be used as the first argument to ``cv_name&#8217;&#8216;. You can force perl to pass a GV by including <code>&quot;CALL_CHECKER_REQUIRE_GV&quot;</code> in the <code>&quot;ckflags&quot;</code>. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;ckflags&quot;</code> is a bitset, in which only the <code>&quot;CALL_CHECKER_REQUIRE_GV&quot;</code> bit currently has a defined meaning (for which see above). All other bits should be clear. </p>
<p>The current setting for a particular CV can be retrieved by ``cv_get_call_checker_flags&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        void cv_set_call_checker_flags(
                    CV *cv, Perl_call_checker ckfun, SV *ckobj,
                    U32 ckflags
                )
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>LINKLIST</dt>
<dd> <p> Given the root of an optree, link the tree in execution order using the <code>&quot;op_next&quot;</code> pointers and return the first op executed. If this has already been done, it will not be redone, and <code>&quot;o-&gt;op_next&quot;</code> will be returned. If <code>&quot;o-&gt;op_next&quot;</code> is not already set, <code>&quot;o&quot;</code> should be at least an <code>&quot;UNOP&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        OP* LINKLIST(OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newCONSTSUB</dt>
<dd> <p> Behaves like ``newCONSTSUB_flags&#8217;&#8216;, except that <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> is nul-terminated rather than of counted length, and no flags are set. (This means that <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> is always interpreted as Latin-1.) </p>
<pre><code>        CV * newCONSTSUB(HV *stash, const char *name, SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newCONSTSUB_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Construct a constant subroutine, also performing some surrounding jobs. A scalar constant-valued subroutine is eligible for inlining at compile-time, and in Perl code can be created by <code>&quot;sub FOO () { 123 }&quot;</code>. Other kinds of constant subroutine have other treatment. </p>
<p>The subroutine will have an empty prototype and will ignore any arguments when called. Its constant behaviour is determined by <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>. If <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is null, the subroutine will yield an empty list. If <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> points to a scalar, the subroutine will always yield that scalar. If <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> points to an array, the subroutine will always yield a list of the elements of that array in list context, or the number of elements in the array in scalar context. This function takes ownership of one counted reference to the scalar or array, and will arrange for the object to live as long as the subroutine does. If <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> points to a scalar then the inlining assumes that the value of the scalar will never change, so the caller must ensure that the scalar is not subsequently written to. If <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> points to an array then no such assumption is made, so it is ostensibly safe to mutate the array or its elements, but whether this is really supported has not been determined. </p>
<p>The subroutine will have <code>&quot;CvFILE&quot;</code> set according to <code>&quot;PL_curcop&quot;</code>. Other aspects of the subroutine will be left in their default state. The caller is free to mutate the subroutine beyond its initial state after this function has returned. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> is null then the subroutine will be anonymous, with its <code>&quot;CvGV&quot;</code> referring to an <code>&quot;__ANON__&quot;</code> glob. If <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> is non-null then the subroutine will be named accordingly, referenced by the appropriate glob. <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> is a string of length <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> bytes giving a sigilless symbol name, in UTF-8 if <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;SVf_UTF8&quot;</code> bit set and in Latin-1 otherwise. The name may be either qualified or unqualified. If the name is unqualified then it defaults to being in the stash specified by <code>&quot;stash&quot;</code> if that is non-null, or to <code>&quot;PL_curstash&quot;</code> if <code>&quot;stash&quot;</code> is null. The symbol is always added to the stash if necessary, with <code>&quot;GV_ADDMULTI&quot;</code> semantics. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> should not have bits set other than <code>&quot;SVf_UTF8&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>If there is already a subroutine of the specified name, then the new sub will replace the existing one in the glob. A warning may be generated about the redefinition. </p>
<p>If the subroutine has one of a few special names, such as <code>&quot;BEGIN&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;END&quot;</code>, then it will be claimed by the appropriate queue for automatic running of phase-related subroutines. In this case the relevant glob will be left not containing any subroutine, even if it did contain one before. Execution of the subroutine will likely be a no-op, unless <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> was a tied array or the caller modified the subroutine in some interesting way before it was executed. In the case of <code>&quot;BEGIN&quot;</code>, the treatment is buggy: the sub will be executed when only half built, and may be deleted prematurely, possibly causing a crash. </p>
<p>The function returns a pointer to the constructed subroutine. If the sub is anonymous then ownership of one counted reference to the subroutine is transferred to the caller. If the sub is named then the caller does not get ownership of a reference. In most such cases, where the sub has a non-phase name, the sub will be alive at the point it is returned by virtue of being contained in the glob that names it. A phase-named subroutine will usually be alive by virtue of the reference owned by the phase&#8217;s automatic run queue. A <code>&quot;BEGIN&quot;</code> subroutine may have been destroyed already by the time this function returns, but currently bugs occur in that case before the caller gets control. It is the caller&#8217;s responsibility to ensure that it knows which of these situations applies. </p>
<pre><code>        CV * newCONSTSUB_flags(HV *stash, const char *name,
                                  STRLEN len, U32 flags, SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newXS</dt>
<dd> <p> Used by <code>&quot;xsubpp&quot;</code> to hook up XSUBs as Perl subs. <code>&quot;filename&quot;</code> needs to be static storage, as it is used directly as <code>CvFILE()</code>, without a copy being made. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>op_append_elem</dt>
<dd> <p> Append an item to the list of ops contained directly within a list-type op, returning the lengthened list. <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> is the list-type op, and <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> is the op to append to the list. <code>&quot;optype&quot;</code> specifies the intended opcode for the list. If <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> is not already a list of the right type, it will be upgraded into one. If either <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> is null, the other is returned unchanged. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * op_append_elem(I32 optype, OP *first, OP *last)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>op_append_list</dt>
<dd> <p> Concatenate the lists of ops contained directly within two list-type ops, returning the combined list. <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> are the list-type ops to concatenate. <code>&quot;optype&quot;</code> specifies the intended opcode for the list. If either <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> is not already a list of the right type, it will be upgraded into one. If either <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> is null, the other is returned unchanged. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * op_append_list(I32 optype, OP *first, OP *last)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>OP_CLASS</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the class of the provided OP: that is, which of the *OP structures it uses. For core ops this currently gets the information out of <code>&quot;PL_opargs&quot;</code>, which does not always accurately reflect the type used; in v5.26 onwards, see also the function <code>&quot;op_class&quot;</code> which can do a better job of determining the used type. </p>
<p>For custom ops the type is returned from the registration, and it is up to the registree to ensure it is accurate. The value returned will be one of the <code>&quot;OA_&quot;</code>* constants from <i>op.h</i>. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 OP_CLASS(OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>op_contextualize</dt>
<dd> <p> Applies a syntactic context to an op tree representing an expression. <code>&quot;o&quot;</code> is the op tree, and <code>&quot;context&quot;</code> must be <code>&quot;G_SCALAR&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;G_ARRAY&quot;</code>, or <code>&quot;G_VOID&quot;</code> to specify the context to apply. The modified op tree is returned. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * op_contextualize(OP *o, I32 context)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>op_convert_list</dt>
<dd> <p> Converts <code>&quot;o&quot;</code> into a list op if it is not one already, and then converts it into the specified <code>&quot;type&quot;</code>, calling its check function, allocating a target if it needs one, and folding constants. </p>
<p>A list-type op is usually constructed one kid at a time via <code>&quot;newLISTOP&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;op_prepend_elem&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;op_append_elem&quot;</code>. Then finally it is passed to <code>&quot;op_convert_list&quot;</code> to make it the right type. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * op_convert_list(I32 type, I32 flags, OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>OP_DESC</dt>
<dd> <p> Return a short description of the provided OP. </p>
<pre><code>        const char * OP_DESC(OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>op_free</dt>
<dd> <p> Free an op. Only use this when an op is no longer linked to from any optree. </p>
<pre><code>        void op_free(OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>OpHAS_SIBLING</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns true if <code>&quot;o&quot;</code> has a sibling </p>
<pre><code>        bool OpHAS_SIBLING(OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>OpLASTSIB_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Marks <code>&quot;o&quot;</code> as having no further siblings and marks o as having the specified parent. See also <code>&quot;OpMORESIB_set&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;OpMAYBESIB_set&quot;</code>. For a higher-level interface, see <code>&quot;op_sibling_splice&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void OpLASTSIB_set(OP *o, OP *parent)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>op_linklist</dt>
<dd> <p> This function is the implementation of the ``LINKLIST&#8217;&#8216; macro. It should not be called directly. </p>
<pre><code>        OP* op_linklist(OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>op_lvalue</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Propagate lvalue (``modifiable&#8217;&#8216;) context to an op and its children. <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> represents the context type, roughly based on the type of op that would do the modifying, although <code>&quot;local()&quot;</code> is represented by <code>&quot;OP_NULL&quot;</code>, because it has no op type of its own (it is signalled by a flag on the lvalue op). </p>
<p>This function detects things that can&#8217;t be modified, such as <code>&quot;$x+1&quot;</code>, and generates errors for them. For example, <code>&quot;$x+1 = 2&quot;</code> would cause it to be called with an op of type <code>&quot;OP_ADD&quot;</code> and a <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> argument of <code>&quot;OP_SASSIGN&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>It also flags things that need to behave specially in an lvalue context, such as <code>&quot;$$x = 5&quot;</code> which might have to vivify a reference in <code>$x</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * op_lvalue(OP *o, I32 type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>OpMAYBESIB_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Conditionally does <code>&quot;OpMORESIB_set&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;OpLASTSIB_set&quot;</code> depending on whether <code>&quot;sib&quot;</code> is non-null. For a higher-level interface, see <code>&quot;op_sibling_splice&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void OpMAYBESIB_set(OP *o, OP *sib, OP *parent)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>OpMORESIB_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Sets the sibling of <code>&quot;o&quot;</code> to the non-zero value <code>&quot;sib&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;OpLASTSIB_set&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;OpMAYBESIB_set&quot;</code>. For a higher-level interface, see <code>&quot;op_sibling_splice&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void OpMORESIB_set(OP *o, OP *sib)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>OP_NAME</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the name of the provided OP. For core ops this looks up the name from the op_type; for custom ops from the op_ppaddr. </p>
<pre><code>        const char * OP_NAME(OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>op_null</dt>
<dd> <p> Neutralizes an op when it is no longer needed, but is still linked to from other ops. </p>
<pre><code>        void op_null(OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>op_parent</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the parent OP of <code>&quot;o&quot;</code>, if it has a parent. Returns <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> otherwise. </p>
<pre><code>        OP* op_parent(OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>op_prepend_elem</dt>
<dd> <p> Prepend an item to the list of ops contained directly within a list-type op, returning the lengthened list. <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> is the op to prepend to the list, and <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> is the list-type op. <code>&quot;optype&quot;</code> specifies the intended opcode for the list. If <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> is not already a list of the right type, it will be upgraded into one. If either <code>&quot;first&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;last&quot;</code> is null, the other is returned unchanged. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * op_prepend_elem(I32 optype, OP *first, OP *last)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>op_scope</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Wraps up an op tree with some additional ops so that at runtime a dynamic scope will be created. The original ops run in the new dynamic scope, and then, provided that they exit normally, the scope will be unwound. The additional ops used to create and unwind the dynamic scope will normally be an <code>&quot;enter&quot;</code>/<code>&quot;leave&quot;</code> pair, but a <code>&quot;scope&quot;</code> op may be used instead if the ops are simple enough to not need the full dynamic scope structure. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * op_scope(OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>OpSIBLING</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the sibling of <code>&quot;o&quot;</code>, or <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> if there is no sibling </p>
<pre><code>        OP* OpSIBLING(OP *o)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>op_sibling_splice</dt>
<dd> <p> A general function for editing the structure of an existing chain of op_sibling nodes. By analogy with the perl-level <code>&quot;splice()&quot;</code> function, allows you to delete zero or more sequential nodes, replacing them with zero or more different nodes. Performs the necessary op_first/op_last housekeeping on the parent node and op_sibling manipulation on the children. The last deleted node will be marked as as the last node by updating the op_sibling/op_sibparent or op_moresib field as appropriate. </p>
<p>Note that op_next is not manipulated, and nodes are not freed; that is the responsibility of the caller. It also won&#8217;t create a new list op for an empty list etc; use higher-level functions like <code>op_append_elem()</code> for that. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;parent&quot;</code> is the parent node of the sibling chain. It may passed as <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> if the splicing doesn&#8217;t affect the first or last op in the chain. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;start&quot;</code> is the node preceding the first node to be spliced. Node(s) following it will be deleted, and ops will be inserted after it. If it is <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>, the first node onwards is deleted, and nodes are inserted at the beginning. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;del_count&quot;</code> is the number of nodes to delete. If zero, no nodes are deleted. If -1 or greater than or equal to the number of remaining kids, all remaining kids are deleted. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;insert&quot;</code> is the first of a chain of nodes to be inserted in place of the nodes. If <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>, no nodes are inserted. </p>
<p>The head of the chain of deleted ops is returned, or <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> if no ops were deleted. </p>
<p>For example: </p>
<pre><code>    action before after returns
    ------ ----- ----- -------
                              P P
    splice(P, A, 2, X-Y-Z) | | B-C
                              A-B-C-D A-X-Y-Z-D
                              P P
    splice(P, NULL, 1, X-Y) | | A
                              A-B-C-D X-Y-B-C-D
                              P P
    splice(P, NULL, 3, NULL) | | A-B-C
                              A-B-C-D D
                              P P
    splice(P, B, 0, X-Y) | | NULL
                              A-B-C-D A-B-X-Y-C-D
</code></pre>
<p>For lower-level direct manipulation of <code>&quot;op_sibparent&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;op_moresib&quot;</code>, see <code>&quot;OpMORESIB_set&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;OpLASTSIB_set&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;OpMAYBESIB_set&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        OP* op_sibling_splice(OP *parent, OP *start,
                                  int del_count, OP* insert)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>OP_TYPE_IS</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns true if the given OP is not a <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> pointer and if it is of the given type. </p>
<p>The negation of this macro, <code>&quot;OP_TYPE_ISNT&quot;</code> is also available as well as <code>&quot;OP_TYPE_IS_NN&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;OP_TYPE_ISNT_NN&quot;</code> which elide the NULL pointer check. </p>
<pre><code>        bool OP_TYPE_IS(OP *o, Optype type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>OP_TYPE_IS_OR_WAS</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns true if the given OP is not a NULL pointer and if it is of the given type or used to be before being replaced by an OP of type OP_NULL. </p>
<p>The negation of this macro, <code>&quot;OP_TYPE_ISNT_AND_WASNT&quot;</code> is also available as well as <code>&quot;OP_TYPE_IS_OR_WAS_NN&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;OP_TYPE_ISNT_AND_WASNT_NN&quot;</code> which elide the <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> pointer check. </p>
<pre><code>        bool OP_TYPE_IS_OR_WAS(OP *o, Optype type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>rv2cv_op_cv</dt>
<dd> <p> Examines an op, which is expected to identify a subroutine at runtime, and attempts to determine at compile time which subroutine it identifies. This is normally used during Perl compilation to determine whether a prototype can be applied to a function call. <code>&quot;cvop&quot;</code> is the op being considered, normally an <code>&quot;rv2cv&quot;</code> op. A pointer to the identified subroutine is returned, if it could be determined statically, and a null pointer is returned if it was not possible to determine statically. </p>
<p>Currently, the subroutine can be identified statically if the RV that the <code>&quot;rv2cv&quot;</code> is to operate on is provided by a suitable <code>&quot;gv&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;const&quot;</code> op. A <code>&quot;gv&quot;</code> op is suitable if the GV&#8216;s CV slot is populated. A <code>&quot;const&quot;</code> op is suitable if the constant value must be an RV pointing to a CV. Details of this process may change in future versions of Perl. If the <code>&quot;rv2cv&quot;</code> op has the <code>&quot;OPpENTERSUB_AMPER&quot;</code> flag set then no attempt is made to identify the subroutine statically: this flag is used to suppress compile-time magic on a subroutine call, forcing it to use default runtime behaviour. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the bit <code>&quot;RV2CVOPCV_MARK_EARLY&quot;</code> set, then the handling of a GV reference is modified.
