If a GV was examined and its CV slot was found to be empty, then the <code>&quot;gv&quot;</code> op has the <code>&quot;OPpEARLY_CV&quot;</code> flag set. If the op is not optimised away, and the CV slot is later populated with a subroutine having a prototype, that flag eventually triggers the warning ``called too early to check prototype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the bit <code>&quot;RV2CVOPCV_RETURN_NAME_GV&quot;</code> set, then instead of returning a pointer to the subroutine it returns a pointer to the GV giving the most appropriate name for the subroutine in this context. Normally this is just the <code>&quot;CvGV&quot;</code> of the subroutine, but for an anonymous (<code>&quot;CvANON&quot;</code>) subroutine that is referenced through a GV it will be the referencing GV. The resulting <code>&quot;GV*&quot;</code> is cast to <code>&quot;CV*&quot;</code> to be returned. A null pointer is returned as usual if there is no statically-determinable subroutine. </p>
<pre><code>        CV * rv2cv_op_cv(OP *cvop, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Pack and Unpack</h2>
<dl>
<dt>packlist</dt>
<dd> <p> The engine implementing <code>&quot;pack()&quot;</code> Perl function. </p>
<pre><code>        void packlist(SV *cat, const char *pat,
                         const char *patend, SV **beglist,
                         SV **endlist)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>unpackstring</dt>
<dd> <p> The engine implementing the <code>&quot;unpack()&quot;</code> Perl function. </p>
<p>Using the template <code>&quot;pat..patend&quot;</code>, this function unpacks the string <code>&quot;s..strend&quot;</code> into a number of mortal SVs, which it pushes onto the perl argument (<code>@_</code>) stack (so you will need to issue a <code>&quot;PUTBACK&quot;</code> before and <code>&quot;SPAGAIN&quot;</code> after the call to this function). It returns the number of pushed elements. </p>
<p>The <code>&quot;strend&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;patend&quot;</code> pointers should point to the byte following the last character of each string. </p>
<p>Although this function returns its values on the perl argument stack, it doesn&#8217;t take any parameters from that stack (and thus in particular there&#8217;s no need to do a <code>&quot;PUSHMARK&quot;</code> before calling it, unlike ``call_pv&#8217;&#8216; for example). </p>
<pre><code>        SSize_t unpackstring(const char *pat,
                             const char *patend, const char *s,
                             const char *strend, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Pad Data Structures</h2>
<dl>
<dt>CvPADLIST</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p> CV&#8216;s can have CvPADLIST(cv) set to point to a PADLIST. This is the CV&#8216;s scratchpad, which stores lexical variables and opcode temporary and per-thread values. </p>
<p>For these purposes ``formats&#8217;&#8216; are a kind-of CV; eval&quot;``s are too (except they&#8217;re not callable at will and are always thrown away after the eval&#8217;&#8216;&quot; is done executing). Require&#8217;d files are simply evals without any outer lexical scope. </p>
<p>XSUBs do not have a <code>&quot;CvPADLIST&quot;</code>. <code>&quot;dXSTARG&quot;</code> fetches values from <code>&quot;PL_curpad&quot;</code>, but that is really the callers pad (a slot of which is allocated by every entersub). Do not get or set <code>&quot;CvPADLIST&quot;</code> if a CV is an XSUB (as determined by <code>&quot;CvISXSUB()&quot;</code>), <code>&quot;CvPADLIST&quot;</code> slot is reused for a different internal purpose in XSUBs. </p>
<p>The PADLIST has a C array where pads are stored. </p>
<p>The 0th entry of the PADLIST is a PADNAMELIST which represents the ``names&#8217;&#8216; or rather the ``static type information&#8217;&#8216; for lexicals. The individual elements of a PADNAMELIST are PADNAMEs. Future refactorings might stop the PADNAMELIST from being stored in the PADLIST&#8216;s array, so don&#8217;t rely on it. See ``PadlistNAMES&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>The CvDEPTH&#8217;th entry of a PADLIST is a PAD (an AV) which is the stack frame at that depth of recursion into the CV. The 0th slot of a frame AV is an AV which is <code>@_</code>. Other entries are storage for variables and op targets. </p>
<p>Iterating over the PADNAMELIST iterates over all possible pad items. Pad slots for targets (<code>&quot;SVs_PADTMP&quot;</code>) and GVs end up having &amp;PL_padname_undef ``names&#8217;&#8216;, while slots for constants have <code>&amp;PL_padname_const</code> ``names&#8217;&#8216; (see <code>&quot;pad_alloc&quot;</code>). That <code>&amp;PL_padname_undef</code> and <code>&amp;PL_padname_const</code> are used is an implementation detail subject to change. To test for them, use <code>&quot;!PadnamePV(name)&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;PadnamePV(name) &amp;&amp; !PadnameLEN(name)&quot;</code>, respectively. </p>
<p>Only <code>&quot;my&quot;</code>/<code>&quot;our&quot;</code> variable slots get valid names. The rest are op targets/GVs/constants which are statically allocated or resolved at compile time. These don&#8217;t have names by which they can be looked up from Perl code at run time through eval&quot;&quot; the way <code>&quot;my&quot;</code>/<code>&quot;our&quot;</code> variables can be. Since they can&#8217;t be looked up by ``name&#8217;&#8216; but only by their index allocated at compile time (which is usually in <code>&quot;PL_op-&gt;op_targ&quot;</code>), wasting a name SV for them doesn&#8217;t make sense. </p>
<p>The pad names in the PADNAMELIST have their PV holding the name of the variable. The <code>&quot;COP_SEQ_RANGE_LOW&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;_HIGH&quot;</code> fields form a range (low+1..high inclusive) of cop_seq numbers for which the name is valid. During compilation, these fields may hold the special value PERL_PADSEQ_INTRO to indicate various stages: </p>
<pre><code> COP_SEQ_RANGE_LOW _HIGH
 ----------------- -----
 PERL_PADSEQ_INTRO 0 variable not yet introduced:
                                  { my ($x
 valid-seq# PERL_PADSEQ_INTRO variable in scope:
                                  { my ($x);
 valid-seq# valid-seq# compilation of scope complete:
                                  { my ($x); .... }
</code></pre>
<p>When a lexical var hasn&#8217;t yet been introduced, it already exists from the perspective of duplicate declarations, but not for variable lookups, e.g. </p>
<pre><code>    my ($x, $x); # '&quot;my&quot; variable $x masks earlier declaration'
    my $x = $x; # equal to my $x = $::x;
</code></pre>
<p>For typed lexicals <code>&quot;PadnameTYPE&quot;</code> points at the type stash. For <code>&quot;our&quot;</code> lexicals, <code>&quot;PadnameOURSTASH&quot;</code> points at the stash of the associated global (so that duplicate <code>&quot;our&quot;</code> declarations in the same package can be detected). <code>&quot;PadnameGEN&quot;</code> is sometimes used to store the generation number during compilation. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;PadnameOUTER&quot;</code> is set on the pad name, then that slot in the frame AV is a REFCNT&#8216;ed reference to a lexical from ``outside&#8217;&#8216;. Such entries are sometimes referred to as &#8216;fake&#8217;. In this case, the name does not use &#8216;low&#8217; and &#8216;high&#8217; to store a cop_seq range, since it is in scope throughout. Instead &#8216;high&#8217; stores some flags containing info about the real lexical (is it declared in an anon, and is it capable of being instantiated multiple times?), and for fake ANONs, &#8216;low&#8217; contains the index within the parent&#8217;s pad where the lexical&#8217;s value is stored, to make cloning quicker. </p>
<p>If the &#8216;name&#8217; is <code>&quot;&amp;&quot;</code> the corresponding entry in the PAD is a CV representing a possible closure. </p>
<p>Note that formats are treated as anon subs, and are cloned each time write is called (if necessary). </p>
<p>The flag <code>&quot;SVs_PADSTALE&quot;</code> is cleared on lexicals each time the <code>&quot;my()&quot;</code> is executed, and set on scope exit. This allows the <code>&quot;Variable $x is not available&quot;</code> warning to be generated in evals, such as </p>
<pre><code>    { my $x = 1; sub f { eval '$x'} } f();
</code></pre>
<p>For state vars, <code>&quot;SVs_PADSTALE&quot;</code> is overloaded to mean &#8216;not yet initialised&#8217;, but this internal state is stored in a separate pad entry. </p>
<pre><code>        PADLIST * CvPADLIST(CV *cv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_add_name_pvs</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``pad_add_name_pvn&#8217;&#8216;, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        PADOFFSET pad_add_name_pvs(&quot;literal string&quot; name,
                                   U32 flags, HV *typestash,
                                   HV *ourstash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadARRAY</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The C array of pad entries. </p>
<pre><code>        SV ** PadARRAY(PAD pad)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_findmy_pvs</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``pad_findmy_pvn&#8217;&#8216;, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        PADOFFSET pad_findmy_pvs(&quot;literal string&quot; name,
                                 U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadlistARRAY</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The C array of a padlist, containing the pads. Only subscript it with numbers &gt;= 1, as the 0th entry is not guaranteed to remain usable. </p>
<pre><code>        PAD ** PadlistARRAY(PADLIST padlist)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadlistMAX</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The index of the last allocated space in the padlist. Note that the last pad may be in an earlier slot. Any entries following it will be <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> in that case. </p>
<pre><code>        SSize_t PadlistMAX(PADLIST padlist)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadlistNAMES</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The names associated with pad entries. </p>
<pre><code>        PADNAMELIST * PadlistNAMES(PADLIST padlist)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadlistNAMESARRAY</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The C array of pad names. </p>
<pre><code>        PADNAME ** PadlistNAMESARRAY(PADLIST padlist)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadlistNAMESMAX</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The index of the last pad name. </p>
<pre><code>        SSize_t PadlistNAMESMAX(PADLIST padlist)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadlistREFCNT</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The reference count of the padlist. Currently this is always 1. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 PadlistREFCNT(PADLIST padlist)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadMAX</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The index of the last pad entry. </p>
<pre><code>        SSize_t PadMAX(PAD pad)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadnameLEN</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The length of the name. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN PadnameLEN(PADNAME pn)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadnamelistARRAY</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The C array of pad names. </p>
<pre><code>        PADNAME ** PadnamelistARRAY(PADNAMELIST pnl)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadnamelistMAX</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The index of the last pad name. </p>
<pre><code>        SSize_t PadnamelistMAX(PADNAMELIST pnl)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadnamelistREFCNT</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The reference count of the pad name list. </p>
<pre><code>        SSize_t PadnamelistREFCNT(PADNAMELIST pnl)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadnamelistREFCNT_dec</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Lowers the reference count of the pad name list. </p>
<pre><code>        void PadnamelistREFCNT_dec(PADNAMELIST pnl)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadnamePV</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The name stored in the pad name struct. This returns <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> for a target slot. </p>
<pre><code>        char * PadnamePV(PADNAME pn)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadnameREFCNT</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>The reference count of the pad name. </p>
<pre><code>        SSize_t PadnameREFCNT(PADNAME pn)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadnameREFCNT_dec</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Lowers the reference count of the pad name. </p>
<pre><code>        void PadnameREFCNT_dec(PADNAME pn)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadnameSV</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Returns the pad name as a mortal SV. </p>
<pre><code>        SV * PadnameSV(PADNAME pn)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PadnameUTF8</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Whether PadnamePV is in UTF-8. Currently, this is always true. </p>
<pre><code>        bool PadnameUTF8(PADNAME pn)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pad_new</dt>
<dd> <p> Create a new padlist, updating the global variables for the currently-compiling padlist to point to the new padlist. The following flags can be OR&#8216;ed together: </p>
<pre><code>    padnew_CLONE this pad is for a cloned CV
    padnew_SAVE save old globals on the save stack
    padnew_SAVESUB also save extra stuff for start of sub
        PADLIST * pad_new(int flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_comppad</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>During compilation, this points to the array containing the values part of the pad for the currently-compiling code. (At runtime a CV may have many such value arrays; at compile time just one is constructed.) At runtime, this points to the array containing the currently-relevant values for the pad for the currently-executing code. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_comppad_name</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>During compilation, this points to the array containing the names part of the pad for the currently-compiling code. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_curpad</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Points directly to the body of the ``PL_comppad&#8217;&#8216; array. (I.e., this is <code>&quot;PadARRAY(PL_comppad)&quot;</code>.) </p>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Per-Interpreter Variables</h2>
<dl>
<dt>PL_modglobal</dt>
<dd> <p> <code>&quot;PL_modglobal&quot;</code> is a general purpose, interpreter global HV for use by extensions that need to keep information on a per-interpreter basis. In a pinch, it can also be used as a symbol table for extensions to share data among each other. It is a good idea to use keys prefixed by the package name of the extension that owns the data. </p>
<pre><code>        HV* PL_modglobal
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_na</dt>
<dd> <p> A convenience variable which is typically used with <code>&quot;SvPV&quot;</code> when one doesn&#8217;t care about the length of the string. It is usually more efficient to either declare a local variable and use that instead or to use the <code>&quot;SvPV_nolen&quot;</code> macro. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN PL_na
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_opfreehook</dt>
<dd> <p> When non-<code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>, the function pointed by this variable will be called each time an OP is freed with the corresponding OP as the argument. This allows extensions to free any extra attribute they have locally attached to an OP. It is also assured to first fire for the parent OP and then for its kids. </p>
<p>When you replace this variable, it is considered a good practice to store the possibly previously installed hook and that you recall it inside your own. </p>
<pre><code>        Perl_ophook_t PL_opfreehook
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_peepp</dt>
<dd> <p> Pointer to the per-subroutine peephole optimiser. This is a function that gets called at the end of compilation of a Perl subroutine (or equivalently independent piece of Perl code) to perform fixups of some ops and to perform small-scale optimisations. The function is called once for each subroutine that is compiled, and is passed, as sole parameter, a pointer to the op that is the entry point to the subroutine. It modifies the op tree in place. </p>
<p>The peephole optimiser should never be completely replaced. Rather, add code to it by wrapping the existing optimiser. The basic way to do this can be seen in ``Compile pass 3: peephole optimization&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts. If the new code wishes to operate on ops throughout the subroutine&#8217;s structure, rather than just at the top level, it is likely to be more convenient to wrap the ``PL_rpeepp&#8217;&#8216; hook. </p>
<pre><code>        peep_t PL_peepp
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_rpeepp</dt>
<dd> <p> Pointer to the recursive peephole optimiser. This is a function that gets called at the end of compilation of a Perl subroutine (or equivalently independent piece of Perl code) to perform fixups of some ops and to perform small-scale optimisations. The function is called once for each chain of ops linked through their <code>&quot;op_next&quot;</code> fields; it is recursively called to handle each side chain. It is passed, as sole parameter, a pointer to the op that is at the head of the chain. It modifies the op tree in place. </p>
<p>The peephole optimiser should never be completely replaced. Rather, add code to it by wrapping the existing optimiser. The basic way to do this can be seen in ``Compile pass 3: peephole optimization&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts. If the new code wishes to operate only on ops at a subroutine&#8217;s top level, rather than throughout the structure, it is likely to be more convenient to wrap the ``PL_peepp&#8217;&#8216; hook. </p>
<pre><code>        peep_t PL_rpeepp
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_sv_no</dt>
<dd> <p> This is the <code>&quot;false&quot;</code> SV. See <code>&quot;PL_sv_yes&quot;</code>. Always refer to this as <code>&amp;PL_sv_no</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV PL_sv_no
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_sv_undef</dt>
<dd> <p> This is the <code>&quot;undef&quot;</code> SV. Always refer to this as <code>&amp;PL_sv_undef</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV PL_sv_undef
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_sv_yes</dt>
<dd> <p> This is the <code>&quot;true&quot;</code> SV. See <code>&quot;PL_sv_no&quot;</code>. Always refer to this as <code>&amp;PL_sv_yes</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV PL_sv_yes
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PL_sv_zero</dt>
<dd> <p> This readonly SV has a zero numeric value and a <code>&quot;0&quot;</code> string value. It&#8217;s similar to <code>&quot;PL_sv_no&quot;</code> except for its string value. Can be used as a cheap alternative to <code>mXPUSHi(0)</code> for example. Always refer to this as <code>&amp;PL_sv_zero</code>. Introduced in 5.28. </p>
<pre><code>        SV PL_sv_zero
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>REGEXP Functions</h2>
<dl>
<dt>SvRX</dt>
<dd> <p> Convenience macro to get the REGEXP from a SV. This is approximately equivalent to the following snippet: </p>
<pre><code>    if (SvMAGICAL(sv))
        mg_get(sv);
    if (SvROK(sv))
        sv = MUTABLE_SV(SvRV(sv));
    if (SvTYPE(sv) == SVt_REGEXP)
        return (REGEXP*) sv;
</code></pre>
<p> <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> will be returned if a REGEXP* is not found. </p>
<pre><code>        REGEXP * SvRX(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvRXOK</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the SV (or the one it references) is a REGEXP. </p>
<p>If you want to do something with the REGEXP* later use SvRX instead and check for NULL. </p>
<pre><code>        bool SvRXOK(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Stack Manipulation Macros</h2>
<dl>
<dt>dMARK</dt>
<dd> <p> Declare a stack marker variable, <code>&quot;mark&quot;</code>, for the XSUB. See <code>&quot;MARK&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;dORIGMARK&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>                dMARK;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>dORIGMARK</dt>
<dd> <p> Saves the original stack mark for the XSUB. See <code>&quot;ORIGMARK&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>                dORIGMARK;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>dSP</dt>
<dd> <p> Declares a local copy of perl&#8217;s stack pointer for the XSUB, available via the <code>&quot;SP&quot;</code> macro. See <code>&quot;SP&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>                dSP;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>EXTEND</dt>
<dd> <p> Used to extend the argument stack for an XSUB&#8216;s return values. Once used, guarantees that there is room for at least <code>&quot;nitems&quot;</code> to be pushed onto the stack. </p>
<pre><code>        void EXTEND(SP, SSize_t nitems)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>MARK</dt>
<dd> <p> Stack marker variable for the XSUB. See <code>&quot;dMARK&quot;</code>. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>mPUSHi</dt>
<dd> <p> Push an integer onto the stack. The stack must have room for this element. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;PUSHi&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;mXPUSHi&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;XPUSHi&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mPUSHi(IV iv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mPUSHn</dt>
<dd> <p> Push a double onto the stack. The stack must have room for this element. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;PUSHn&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;mXPUSHn&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;XPUSHn&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mPUSHn(NV nv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mPUSHp</dt>
<dd> <p> Push a string onto the stack. The stack must have room for this element. The <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> indicates the length of the string. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;PUSHp&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;mXPUSHp&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;XPUSHp&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mPUSHp(char* str, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mPUSHs</dt>
<dd> <p> Push an SV onto the stack and mortalizes the SV. The stack must have room for this element. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;PUSHs&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;mXPUSHs&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mPUSHs(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mPUSHu</dt>
<dd> <p> Push an unsigned integer onto the stack. The stack must have room for this element. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;PUSHu&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;mXPUSHu&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;XPUSHu&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mPUSHu(UV uv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mXPUSHi</dt>
<dd> <p> Push an integer onto the stack, extending the stack if necessary. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;XPUSHi&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;mPUSHi&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;PUSHi&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mXPUSHi(IV iv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mXPUSHn</dt>
<dd> <p> Push a double onto the stack, extending the stack if necessary. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;XPUSHn&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;mPUSHn&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;PUSHn&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mXPUSHn(NV nv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mXPUSHp</dt>
<dd> <p> Push a string onto the stack, extending the stack if necessary. The <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> indicates the length of the string. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;XPUSHp&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;mPUSHp&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;PUSHp&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mXPUSHp(char* str, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mXPUSHs</dt>
<dd> <p> Push an SV onto the stack, extending the stack if necessary and mortalizes the SV. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;XPUSHs&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;mPUSHs&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mXPUSHs(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>mXPUSHu</dt>
<dd> <p> Push an unsigned integer onto the stack, extending the stack if necessary. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;XPUSHu&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;mPUSHu&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;PUSHu&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void mXPUSHu(UV uv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ORIGMARK</dt>
<dd> <p> The original stack mark for the XSUB. See <code>&quot;dORIGMARK&quot;</code>. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>POPi</dt>
<dd> <p> Pops an integer off the stack. </p>
<pre><code>        IV POPi
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>POPl</dt>
<dd> <p> Pops a long off the stack. </p>
<pre><code>        long POPl
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>POPn</dt>
<dd> <p> Pops a double off the stack. </p>
<pre><code>        NV POPn
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>POPp</dt>
<dd> <p> Pops a string off the stack. </p>
<pre><code>        char* POPp
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>POPpbytex</dt>
<dd> <p> Pops a string off the stack which must consist of bytes i.e. characters &lt; 256. </p>
<pre><code>        char* POPpbytex
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>POPpx</dt>
<dd> <p> Pops a string off the stack. Identical to POPp. There are two names for historical reasons. </p>
<pre><code>        char* POPpx
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>POPs</dt>
<dd> <p> Pops an SV off the stack. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* POPs
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>POPu</dt>
<dd> <p> Pops an unsigned integer off the stack. </p>
<pre><code>        UV POPu
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>POPul</dt>
<dd> <p> Pops an unsigned long off the stack. </p>
<pre><code>        long POPul
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PUSHi</dt>
<dd> <p> Push an integer onto the stack. The stack must have room for this element. Handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. Uses <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>, so <code>&quot;dTARGET&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;dXSTARG&quot;</code> should be called to declare it. Do not call multiple <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>-oriented macros to return lists from XSUB&#8216;s - see <code>&quot;mPUSHi&quot;</code> instead. See also <code>&quot;XPUSHi&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;mXPUSHi&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void PUSHi(IV iv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PUSHMARK</dt>
<dd> <p> Opening bracket for arguments on a callback. See <code>&quot;PUTBACK&quot;</code> and perlcall. </p>
<pre><code>        void PUSHMARK(SP)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PUSHmortal</dt>
<dd> <p> Push a new mortal SV onto the stack. The stack must have room for this element. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;PUSHs&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;XPUSHmortal&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;XPUSHs&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void PUSHmortal()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PUSHn</dt>
<dd> <p> Push a double onto the stack. The stack must have room for this element. Handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. Uses <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>, so <code>&quot;dTARGET&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;dXSTARG&quot;</code> should be called to declare it. Do not call multiple <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>-oriented macros to return lists from XSUB&#8216;s - see <code>&quot;mPUSHn&quot;</code> instead. See also <code>&quot;XPUSHn&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;mXPUSHn&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void PUSHn(NV nv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PUSHp</dt>
<dd> <p> Push a string onto the stack. The stack must have room for this element. The <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> indicates the length of the string. Handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. Uses <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>, so <code>&quot;dTARGET&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;dXSTARG&quot;</code> should be called to declare it. Do not call multiple <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>-oriented macros to return lists from XSUB&#8216;s - see <code>&quot;mPUSHp&quot;</code> instead. See also <code>&quot;XPUSHp&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;mXPUSHp&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void PUSHp(char* str, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PUSHs</dt>
<dd> <p> Push an SV onto the stack. The stack must have room for this element. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;PUSHmortal&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;XPUSHs&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;XPUSHmortal&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void PUSHs(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PUSHu</dt>
<dd> <p> Push an unsigned integer onto the stack. The stack must have room for this element. Handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. Uses <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>, so <code>&quot;dTARGET&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;dXSTARG&quot;</code> should be called to declare it. Do not call multiple <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>-oriented macros to return lists from XSUB&#8216;s - see <code>&quot;mPUSHu&quot;</code> instead. See also <code>&quot;XPUSHu&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;mXPUSHu&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void PUSHu(UV uv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>PUTBACK</dt>
<dd> <p> Closing bracket for XSUB arguments. This is usually handled by <code>&quot;xsubpp&quot;</code>. See <code>&quot;PUSHMARK&quot;</code> and perlcall for other uses. </p>
<pre><code>                PUTBACK;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SP</dt>
<dd> <p> Stack pointer. This is usually handled by <code>&quot;xsubpp&quot;</code>. See <code>&quot;dSP&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SPAGAIN&quot;</code>. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SPAGAIN</dt>
<dd> <p> Refetch the stack pointer. Used after a callback. See perlcall. </p>
<pre><code>                SPAGAIN;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XPUSHi</dt>
<dd> <p> Push an integer onto the stack, extending the stack if necessary. Handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. Uses <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>, so <code>&quot;dTARGET&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;dXSTARG&quot;</code> should be called to declare it. Do not call multiple <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>-oriented macros to return lists from XSUB&#8216;s - see <code>&quot;mXPUSHi&quot;</code> instead. See also <code>&quot;PUSHi&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;mPUSHi&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void XPUSHi(IV iv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XPUSHmortal</dt>
<dd> <p> Push a new mortal SV onto the stack, extending the stack if necessary. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;XPUSHs&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;PUSHmortal&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;PUSHs&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void XPUSHmortal()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XPUSHn</dt>
<dd> <p> Push a double onto the stack, extending the stack if necessary. Handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. Uses <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>, so <code>&quot;dTARGET&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;dXSTARG&quot;</code> should be called to declare it. Do not call multiple <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>-oriented macros to return lists from XSUB&#8216;s - see <code>&quot;mXPUSHn&quot;</code> instead. See also <code>&quot;PUSHn&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;mPUSHn&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void XPUSHn(NV nv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XPUSHp</dt>
<dd> <p> Push a string onto the stack, extending the stack if necessary. The <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> indicates the length of the string. Handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. Uses <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>, so <code>&quot;dTARGET&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;dXSTARG&quot;</code> should be called to declare it. Do not call multiple <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>-oriented macros to return lists from XSUB&#8216;s - see <code>&quot;mXPUSHp&quot;</code> instead. See also <code>&quot;PUSHp&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;mPUSHp&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void XPUSHp(char* str, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XPUSHs</dt>
<dd> <p> Push an SV onto the stack, extending the stack if necessary. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. Does not use <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;XPUSHmortal&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;PUSHs&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;PUSHmortal&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void XPUSHs(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XPUSHu</dt>
<dd> <p> Push an unsigned integer onto the stack, extending the stack if necessary. Handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. Uses <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>, so <code>&quot;dTARGET&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;dXSTARG&quot;</code> should be called to declare it. Do not call multiple <code>&quot;TARG&quot;</code>-oriented macros to return lists from XSUB&#8216;s - see <code>&quot;mXPUSHu&quot;</code> instead. See also <code>&quot;PUSHu&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;mPUSHu&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void XPUSHu(UV uv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XSRETURN</dt>
<dd> <p> Return from XSUB, indicating number of items on the stack. This is usually handled by <code>&quot;xsubpp&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void XSRETURN(int nitems)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XSRETURN_EMPTY</dt>
<dd> <p> Return an empty list from an XSUB immediately. </p>
<pre><code>                XSRETURN_EMPTY;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XSRETURN_IV</dt>
<dd> <p> Return an integer from an XSUB immediately. Uses <code>&quot;XST_mIV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void XSRETURN_IV(IV iv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XSRETURN_NO</dt>
<dd> <p> Return <code>&amp;PL_sv_no</code> from an XSUB immediately. Uses <code>&quot;XST_mNO&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>                XSRETURN_NO;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XSRETURN_NV</dt>
<dd> <p> Return a double from an XSUB immediately. Uses <code>&quot;XST_mNV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void XSRETURN_NV(NV nv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XSRETURN_PV</dt>
<dd> <p> Return a copy of a string from an XSUB immediately. Uses <code>&quot;XST_mPV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void XSRETURN_PV(char* str)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XSRETURN_UNDEF</dt>
<dd> <p> Return <code>&amp;PL_sv_undef</code> from an XSUB immediately. Uses <code>&quot;XST_mUNDEF&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>                XSRETURN_UNDEF;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XSRETURN_UV</dt>
<dd> <p> Return an integer from an XSUB immediately. Uses <code>&quot;XST_mUV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void XSRETURN_UV(IV uv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XSRETURN_YES</dt>
<dd> <p> Return <code>&amp;PL_sv_yes</code> from an XSUB immediately. Uses <code>&quot;XST_mYES&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>                XSRETURN_YES;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XST_mIV</dt>
<dd> <p> Place an integer into the specified position <code>&quot;pos&quot;</code> on the stack. The value is stored in a new mortal SV. </p>
<pre><code>        void XST_mIV(int pos, IV iv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XST_mNO</dt>
<dd> <p> Place <code>&amp;PL_sv_no</code> into the specified position <code>&quot;pos&quot;</code> on the stack. </p>
<pre><code>        void XST_mNO(int pos)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XST_mNV</dt>
<dd> <p> Place a double into the specified position <code>&quot;pos&quot;</code> on the stack. The value is stored in a new mortal SV. </p>
<pre><code>        void XST_mNV(int pos, NV nv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XST_mPV</dt>
<dd> <p> Place a copy of a string into the specified position <code>&quot;pos&quot;</code> on the stack. The value is stored in a new mortal SV. </p>
<pre><code>        void XST_mPV(int pos, char* str)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XST_mUNDEF</dt>
<dd> <p> Place <code>&amp;PL_sv_undef</code> into the specified position <code>&quot;pos&quot;</code> on the stack. </p>
<pre><code>        void XST_mUNDEF(int pos)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XST_mYES</dt>
<dd> <p> Place <code>&amp;PL_sv_yes</code> into the specified position <code>&quot;pos&quot;</code> on the stack. </p>
<pre><code>        void XST_mYES(int pos)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>SV Flags</h2>
<dl>
<dt>SVt_INVLIST</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for scalars. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_IV</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for scalars. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_NULL</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for scalars. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_NV</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for scalars. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_PV</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for scalars. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_PVAV</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for arrays. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_PVCV</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for subroutines. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_PVFM</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for formats. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_PVGV</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for typeglobs. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_PVHV</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for hashes. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_PVIO</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for I/O objects. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_PVIV</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for scalars. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_PVLV</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for scalars. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_PVMG</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for scalars. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_PVNV</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for scalars. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>SVt_REGEXP</dt>
<dd> <p> Type flag for regular expressions. See ``svtype&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>svtype</dt>
<dd> <p> An enum of flags for Perl types. These are found in the file <i>sv.h</i> in the <code>&quot;svtype&quot;</code> enum. Test these flags with the <code>&quot;SvTYPE&quot;</code> macro. </p>
<p>The types are: </p>
<pre><code>    SVt_NULL
    SVt_IV
    SVt_NV
    SVt_RV
    SVt_PV
    SVt_PVIV
    SVt_PVNV
    SVt_PVMG
    SVt_INVLIST
    SVt_REGEXP
    SVt_PVGV
    SVt_PVLV
    SVt_PVAV
    SVt_PVHV
    SVt_PVCV
    SVt_PVFM
    SVt_PVIO
</code></pre>
<p>These are most easily explained from the bottom up. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;SVt_PVIO&quot;</code> is for I/O objects, <code>&quot;SVt_PVFM&quot;</code> for formats, <code>&quot;SVt_PVCV&quot;</code> for subroutines, <code>&quot;SVt_PVHV&quot;</code> for hashes and <code>&quot;SVt_PVAV&quot;</code> for arrays. </p>
<p>All the others are scalar types, that is, things that can be bound to a <code>&quot;$&quot;</code> variable. For these, the internal types are mostly orthogonal to types in the Perl language. </p>
<p>Hence, checking <code>&quot;SvTYPE(sv) &lt; SVt_PVAV&quot;</code> is the best way to see whether something is a scalar. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;SVt_PVGV&quot;</code> represents a typeglob. If <code>&quot;!SvFAKE(sv)&quot;</code>, then it is a real, incoercible typeglob. If <code>&quot;SvFAKE(sv)&quot;</code>, then it is a scalar to which a typeglob has been assigned. Assigning to it again will stop it from being a typeglob. <code>&quot;SVt_PVLV&quot;</code> represents a scalar that delegates to another scalar behind the scenes. It is used, e.g., for the return value of <code>&quot;substr&quot;</code> and for tied hash and array elements. It can hold any scalar value, including a typeglob. <code>&quot;SVt_REGEXP&quot;</code> is for regular expressions. <code>&quot;SVt_INVLIST&quot;</code> is for Perl core internal use only. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;SVt_PVMG&quot;</code> represents a ``normal&#8217;&#8216; scalar (not a typeglob, regular expression, or delegate). Since most scalars do not need all the internal fields of a PVMG, we save memory by allocating smaller structs when possible. All the other types are just simpler forms of <code>&quot;SVt_PVMG&quot;</code>, with fewer internal fields. <code>&quot;SVt_NULL&quot;</code> can only hold undef. <code>&quot;SVt_IV&quot;</code> can hold undef, an integer, or a reference. (<code>&quot;SVt_RV&quot;</code> is an alias for <code>&quot;SVt_IV&quot;</code>, which exists for backward compatibility.) <code>&quot;SVt_NV&quot;</code> can hold any of those or a double. <code>&quot;SVt_PV&quot;</code> can only hold <code>&quot;undef&quot;</code> or a string. <code>&quot;SVt_PVIV&quot;</code> is a superset of <code>&quot;SVt_PV&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SVt_IV&quot;</code>. <code>&quot;SVt_PVNV&quot;</code> is similar. <code>&quot;SVt_PVMG&quot;</code> can hold anything <code>&quot;SVt_PVNV&quot;</code> can hold, but it can, but does not have to, be blessed or magical. </p>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>SV Manipulation Functions</h2>
<dl>
<dt>boolSV</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a true SV if <code>&quot;b&quot;</code> is a true value, or a false SV if <code>&quot;b&quot;</code> is 0. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;PL_sv_yes&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;PL_sv_no&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV * boolSV(bool b)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>croak_xs_usage</dt>
<dd> <p> A specialised variant of <code>&quot;croak()&quot;</code> for emitting the usage message for xsubs </p>
<pre><code>    croak_xs_usage(cv, &quot;eee_yow&quot;);
</code></pre>
<p>works out the package name and subroutine name from <code>&quot;cv&quot;</code>, and then calls <code>&quot;croak()&quot;</code>. Hence if <code>&quot;cv&quot;</code> is <code>&amp;ouch::awk</code>, it would call <code>&quot;croak&quot;</code> as: </p>
<pre><code> Perl_croak(aTHX_ &quot;Usage: %&quot; SVf &quot;::%&quot; SVf &quot;(%s)&quot;, &quot;ouch&quot; &quot;awk&quot;,
                                                     &quot;eee_yow&quot;);
        void croak_xs_usage(const CV *const cv,
                               const char *const params)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>get_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the SV of the specified Perl scalar. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> are passed to <code>&quot;gv_fetchpv&quot;</code>. If <code>&quot;GV_ADD&quot;</code> is set and the Perl variable does not exist then it will be created. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is zero and the variable does not exist then NULL is returned. </p>
<p> NOTE: the perl_ form of this function is deprecated. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* get_sv(const char *name, I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>looks_like_number</dt>
<dd> <p> Test if the content of an SV looks like a number (or is a number). <code>&quot;Inf&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;Infinity&quot;</code> are treated as numbers (so will not issue a non-numeric warning), even if your <code>&quot;atof()&quot;</code> doesn&#8217;t grok them. Get-magic is ignored. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 looks_like_number(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newRV_inc</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates an RV wrapper for an SV. The reference count for the original SV is incremented. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newRV_inc(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newRV_noinc</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates an RV wrapper for an SV. The reference count for the original SV is <code>not</code> incremented. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newRV_noinc(SV *const tmpRef)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSV</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV. A non-zero <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> parameter indicates the number of bytes of preallocated string space the SV should have. An extra byte for a trailing <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> is also reserved. (<code>&quot;SvPOK&quot;</code> is not set for the SV even if string space is allocated.) The reference count for the new SV is set to 1. </p>
<p>In 5.9.3, <code>&quot;newSV()&quot;</code> replaces the older <code>&quot;NEWSV()&quot;</code> API, and drops the first parameter, <i>x</i>, a debug aid which allowed callers to identify themselves. This aid has been superseded by a new build option, <code>&quot;PERL_MEM_LOG&quot;</code> (see ``PERL_MEM_LOG&#8217;&#8216; in perlhacktips). The older API is still there for use in XS modules supporting older perls. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSV(const STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVhek</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV from the hash key structure. It will generate scalars that point to the shared string table where possible. Returns a new (undefined) SV if <code>&quot;hek&quot;</code> is NULL. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVhek(const HEK *const hek)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSViv</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV and copies an integer into it. The reference count for the SV is set to 1. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSViv(const IV i)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVnv</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV and copies a floating point value into it. The reference count for the SV is set to 1. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVnv(const NV n)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVpadname</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Creates a new SV containing the pad name. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVpadname(PADNAME *pn)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVpv</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV and copies a string (which may contain <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> (<code>&quot;\0&quot;</code>) characters) into it. The reference count for the SV is set to 1. If <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> is zero, Perl will compute the length using <code>&quot;strlen()&quot;</code>, (which means if you use this option, that <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> can&#8217;t have embedded <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters and has to have a terminating <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> byte). </p>
<p>This function can cause reliability issues if you are likely to pass in empty strings that are not null terminated, because it will run strlen on the string and potentially run past valid memory. </p>
<p>Using ``newSVpvn&#8217;&#8216; is a safer alternative for non <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> terminated strings. For string literals use ``newSVpvs&#8217;&#8216; instead. This function will work fine for <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> terminated strings, but if you want to avoid the if statement on whether to call <code>&quot;strlen&quot;</code> use <code>&quot;newSVpvn&quot;</code> instead (calling <code>&quot;strlen&quot;</code> yourself). </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVpv(const char *const s, const STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVpvf</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV and initializes it with the string formatted like <code>&quot;sv_catpvf&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVpvf(const char *const pat, ...)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVpvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV and copies a string into it, which may contain <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters (<code>&quot;\0&quot;</code>) and other binary data. The reference count for the SV is set to 1. Note that if <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> is zero, Perl will create a zero length (Perl) string. You are responsible for ensuring that the source buffer is at least <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> bytes long. If the <code>&quot;buffer&quot;</code> argument is NULL the new SV will be undefined. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVpvn(const char *const buffer,
                         const STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVpvn_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV and copies a string (which may contain <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> (<code>&quot;\0&quot;</code>) characters) into it. The reference count for the SV is set to 1. Note that if <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> is zero, Perl will create a zero length string. You are responsible for ensuring that the source string is at least <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> bytes long. If the <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> argument is NULL the new SV will be undefined. Currently the only flag bits accepted are <code>&quot;SVf_UTF8&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SVs_TEMP&quot;</code>. If <code>&quot;SVs_TEMP&quot;</code> is set, then <code>&quot;sv_2mortal()&quot;</code> is called on the result before returning. If <code>&quot;SVf_UTF8&quot;</code> is set, <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> is considered to be in UTF-8 and the <code>&quot;SVf_UTF8&quot;</code> flag will be set on the new SV. <code>&quot;newSVpvn_utf8()&quot;</code> is a convenience wrapper for this function, defined as </p>
<pre><code>    #define newSVpvn_utf8(s, len, u) \
        newSVpvn_flags((s), (len), (u) ? SVf_UTF8 : 0)
        SV* newSVpvn_flags(const char *const s,
                               const STRLEN len,
                               const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVpvn_share</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV with its <code>&quot;SvPVX_const&quot;</code> pointing to a shared string in the string table. If the string does not already exist in the table, it is created first. Turns on the <code>&quot;SvIsCOW&quot;</code> flag (or <code>&quot;READONLY&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;FAKE&quot;</code> in 5.16 and earlier). If the <code>&quot;hash&quot;</code> parameter is non-zero, that value is used; otherwise the hash is computed. The string&#8217;s hash can later be retrieved from the SV with the <code>&quot;SvSHARED_HASH()&quot;</code> macro. The idea here is that as the string table is used for shared hash keys these strings will have <code>&quot;SvPVX_const == HeKEY&quot;</code> and hash lookup will avoid string compare. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVpvn_share(const char* s, I32 len, U32 hash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVpvn_utf8</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV and copies a string (which may contain <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> (<code>&quot;\0&quot;</code>) characters) into it. If <code>&quot;utf8&quot;</code> is true, calls <code>&quot;SvUTF8_on&quot;</code> on the new SV. Implemented as a wrapper around <code>&quot;newSVpvn_flags&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVpvn_utf8(const char* s, STRLEN len,
                              U32 utf8)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVpvs</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;newSVpvn&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVpvs(&quot;literal string&quot; s)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVpvs_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;newSVpvn_flags&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVpvs_flags(&quot;literal string&quot; s, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVpv_share</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;newSVpvn_share&quot;</code>, but takes a <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVpv_share(const char* s, U32 hash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVpvs_share</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;newSVpvn_share&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair and omits the hash parameter. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVpvs_share(&quot;literal string&quot; s)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVrv</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV for the existing RV, <code>&quot;rv&quot;</code>, to point to. If <code>&quot;rv&quot;</code> is not an RV then it will be upgraded to one. If <code>&quot;classname&quot;</code> is non-null then the new SV will be blessed in the specified package. The new SV is returned and its reference count is 1. The reference count 1 is owned by <code>&quot;rv&quot;</code>. See also <code>newRV_inc()</code> and <code>newRV_noinc()</code> for creating a new RV properly. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVrv(SV *const rv,
                        const char *const classname)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVsv</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV which is an exact duplicate of the original SV. (Uses <code>&quot;sv_setsv&quot;</code>.) </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVsv(SV *const old)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVsv_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;newSVsv&quot;</code> but does not process get magic. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVsv_nomg(SV *const old)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSV_type</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV, of the type specified. The reference count for the new SV is set to 1. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSV_type(const svtype type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>newSVuv</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV and copies an unsigned integer into it. The reference count for the SV is set to 1. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* newSVuv(const UV u)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2bool</dt>
<dd> <p> This macro is only used by <code>&quot;sv_true()&quot;</code> or its macro equivalent, and only if the latter&#8217;s argument is neither <code>&quot;SvPOK&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;SvIOK&quot;</code> nor <code>&quot;SvNOK&quot;</code>. It calls <code>&quot;sv_2bool_flags&quot;</code> with the <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> flag. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_2bool(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2bool_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> This function is only used by <code>&quot;sv_true()&quot;</code> and friends, and only if the latter&#8217;s argument is neither <code>&quot;SvPOK&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;SvIOK&quot;</code> nor <code>&quot;SvNOK&quot;</code>. If the flags contain <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code>, then it does an <code>&quot;mg_get()&quot;</code> first. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_2bool_flags(SV *sv, I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2cv</dt>
<dd> <p> Using various gambits, try to get a CV from an SV; in addition, try if possible to set <code>*st</code> and <code>*gvp</code> to the stash and GV associated with it. The flags in <code>&quot;lref&quot;</code> are passed to <code>&quot;gv_fetchsv&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        CV* sv_2cv(SV* sv, HV **const st, GV **const gvp,
                       const I32 lref)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2io</dt>
<dd> <p> Using various gambits, try to get an IO from an SV: the IO slot if its a GV; or the recursive result if we&#8217;re an RV; or the IO slot of the symbol named after the PV if we&#8217;re a string. </p>
<p>&#8216;Get&#8217; magic is ignored on the <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> passed in, but will be called on <code>&quot;SvRV(sv)&quot;</code> if <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is an RV. </p>
<pre><code>        IO* sv_2io(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2iv_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the integer value of an SV, doing any necessary string conversion. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, does an <code>&quot;mg_get()&quot;</code> first. Normally used via the <code>&quot;SvIV(sv)&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SvIVx(sv)&quot;</code> macros. </p>
<pre><code>        IV sv_2iv_flags(SV *const sv, const I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2mortal</dt>
<dd> <p> Marks an existing SV as mortal. The SV will be destroyed ``soon&#8217;&#8216;, either by an explicit call to <code>&quot;FREETMPS&quot;</code>, or by an implicit call at places such as statement boundaries. <code>&quot;SvTEMP()&quot;</code> is turned on which means that the SV&#8216;s string buffer can be ``stolen&#8217;&#8216; if this SV is copied. See also <code>&quot;sv_newmortal&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;sv_mortalcopy&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_2mortal(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2nv_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the num value of an SV, doing any necessary string or integer conversion. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, does an <code>&quot;mg_get()&quot;</code> first. Normally used via the <code>&quot;SvNV(sv)&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SvNVx(sv)&quot;</code> macros. </p>
<pre><code>        NV sv_2nv_flags(SV *const sv, const I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2pvbyte</dt>
<dd> <p> Return a pointer to the byte-encoded representation of the SV, and set <code>*lp</code> to its length. May cause the SV to be downgraded from UTF-8 as a side-effect. </p>
<p>Usually accessed via the <code>&quot;SvPVbyte&quot;</code> macro. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_2pvbyte(SV *sv, STRLEN *const lp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2pvutf8</dt>
<dd> <p> Return a pointer to the UTF-8-encoded representation of the SV, and set <code>*lp</code> to its length. May cause the SV to be upgraded to UTF-8 as a side-effect. </p>
<p>Usually accessed via the <code>&quot;SvPVutf8&quot;</code> macro. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_2pvutf8(SV *sv, STRLEN *const lp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2pv_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a pointer to the string value of an SV, and sets <code>*lp</code> to its length. If flags has the <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, does an <code>&quot;mg_get()&quot;</code> first. Coerces <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to a string if necessary. Normally invoked via the <code>&quot;SvPV_flags&quot;</code> macro. <code>&quot;sv_2pv()&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;sv_2pv_nomg&quot;</code> usually end up here too. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_2pv_flags(SV *const sv, STRLEN *const lp,
                             const I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_2uv_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the unsigned integer value of an SV, doing any necessary string conversion. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, does an <code>&quot;mg_get()&quot;</code> first. Normally used via the <code>&quot;SvUV(sv)&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SvUVx(sv)&quot;</code> macros. </p>
<pre><code>        UV sv_2uv_flags(SV *const sv, const I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_backoff</dt>
<dd> <p> Remove any string offset. You should normally use the <code>&quot;SvOOK_off&quot;</code> macro wrapper instead. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_backoff(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_bless</dt>
<dd> <p> Blesses an SV into a specified package. The SV must be an RV. The package must be designated by its stash (see <code>&quot;gv_stashpv&quot;</code>). The reference count of the SV is unaffected. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_bless(SV *const sv, HV *const stash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpv</dt>
<dd> <p> Concatenates the <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated string onto the end of the string which is in the SV. If the SV has the UTF-8 status set, then the bytes appended should be valid UTF-8. Handles &#8216;get&#8217; magic, but not &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See <code>&quot;sv_catpv_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpv(SV *const sv, const char* ptr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpvf</dt>
<dd> <p> Processes its arguments like <code>&quot;sprintf&quot;</code>, and appends the formatted output to an SV. As with <code>&quot;sv_vcatpvfn&quot;</code> called with a non-null C-style variable argument list, argument reordering is not supported. If the appended data contains ``wide&#8217;&#8216; characters (including, but not limited to, SVs with a UTF-8 PV formatted with <code>%s</code>, and characters &gt;255 formatted with <code>%c</code>), the original SV might get upgraded to UTF-8. Handles &#8216;get&#8217; magic, but not &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See <code>&quot;sv_catpvf_mg&quot;</code>. If the original SV was UTF-8, the pattern should be valid UTF-8; if the original SV was bytes, the pattern should be too. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpvf(SV *const sv, const char *const pat,
                          ...)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpvf_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_catpvf&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpvf_mg(SV *const sv,
                             const char *const pat, ...)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Concatenates the string onto the end of the string which is in the SV. <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> indicates number of bytes to copy. If the SV has the UTF-8 status set, then the bytes appended should be valid UTF-8. Handles &#8216;get&#8217; magic, but not &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See <code>&quot;sv_catpvn_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpvn(SV *dsv, const char *sstr, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpvn_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Concatenates the string onto the end of the string which is in the SV. The <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> indicates number of bytes to copy. </p>
<p>By default, the string appended is assumed to be valid UTF-8 if the SV has the UTF-8 status set, and a string of bytes otherwise. One can force the appended string to be interpreted as UTF-8 by supplying the <code>&quot;SV_CATUTF8&quot;</code> flag, and as bytes by supplying the <code>&quot;SV_CATBYTES&quot;</code> flag; the SV or the string appended will be upgraded to UTF-8 if necessary. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;SV_SMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, will <code>&quot;mg_set&quot;</code> on <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code> afterwards if appropriate. <code>&quot;sv_catpvn&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;sv_catpvn_nomg&quot;</code> are implemented in terms of this function. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpvn_flags(SV *const dstr,
                                const char *sstr,
                                const STRLEN len,
                                const I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpvn_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_catpvn&quot;</code> but doesn&#8217;t process magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpvn_nomg(SV* sv, const char* ptr,
                               STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpvs</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_catpvn&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpvs(SV* sv, &quot;literal string&quot; s)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpvs_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_catpvn_flags&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpvs_flags(SV* sv, &quot;literal string&quot; s,
                                I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpvs_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_catpvn_mg&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpvs_mg(SV* sv, &quot;literal string&quot; s)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpvs_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_catpvn_nomg&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpvs_nomg(SV* sv, &quot;literal string&quot; s)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpv_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Concatenates the <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated string onto the end of the string which is in the SV. If the SV has the UTF-8 status set, then the bytes appended should be valid UTF-8. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;SV_SMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, will <code>&quot;mg_set&quot;</code> on the modified SV if appropriate. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpv_flags(SV *dstr, const char *sstr,
                               const I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpv_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_catpv&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpv_mg(SV *const sv, const char *const ptr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catpv_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_catpv&quot;</code> but doesn&#8217;t process magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catpv_nomg(SV* sv, const char* ptr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catsv</dt>
<dd> <p> Concatenates the string from SV <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code> onto the end of the string in SV <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code>. If <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code> is null, does nothing; otherwise modifies only <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code>. Handles &#8216;get&#8217; magic on both SVs, but no &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See <code>&quot;sv_catsv_mg&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;sv_catsv_nomg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catsv(SV *dstr, SV *sstr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catsv_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Concatenates the string from SV <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code> onto the end of the string in SV <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code>. If <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code> is null, does nothing; otherwise modifies only <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code>. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, will call <code>&quot;mg_get&quot;</code> on both SVs if appropriate. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;SV_SMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, <code>&quot;mg_set&quot;</code> will be called on the modified SV afterward, if appropriate. <code>&quot;sv_catsv&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;sv_catsv_nomg&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;sv_catsv_mg&quot;</code> are implemented in terms of this function. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catsv_flags(SV *const dsv, SV *const ssv,
                               const I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_catsv_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_catsv&quot;</code> but doesn&#8217;t process magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_catsv_nomg(SV* dsv, SV* ssv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_chop</dt>
<dd> <p> Efficient removal of characters from the beginning of the string buffer. <code>&quot;SvPOK(sv)&quot;</code>, or at least <code>&quot;SvPOKp(sv)&quot;</code>, must be true and <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code> must be a pointer to somewhere inside the string buffer. <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code> becomes the first character of the adjusted string. Uses the <code>&quot;OOK&quot;</code> hack. On return, only <code>&quot;SvPOK(sv)&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SvPOKp(sv)&quot;</code> among the <code>&quot;OK&quot;</code> flags will be true. </p>
<p>Beware: after this function returns, <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code> and SvPVX_const(sv) may no longer refer to the same chunk of data. </p>
<p>The unfortunate similarity of this function&#8217;s name to that of Perl&#8217;s <code>&quot;chop&quot;</code> operator is strictly coincidental. This function works from the left; <code>&quot;chop&quot;</code> works from the right. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_chop(SV *const sv, const char *const ptr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_clear</dt>
<dd> <p> Clear an SV: call any destructors, free up any memory used by the body, and free the body itself. The SV&#8216;s head is <i>not</i> freed, although its type is set to all 1&#8217;s so that it won&#8217;t inadvertently be assumed to be live during global destruction etc. This function should only be called when <code>&quot;REFCNT&quot;</code> is zero. Most of the time you&#8217;ll want to call <code>&quot;sv_free()&quot;</code> (or its macro wrapper <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_dec&quot;</code>) instead. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_clear(SV *const orig_sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_cmp</dt>
<dd> <p> Compares the strings in two SVs. Returns -1, 0, or 1 indicating whether the string in <code>&quot;sv1&quot;</code> is less than, equal to, or greater than the string in <code>&quot;sv2&quot;</code>. Is UTF-8 and <code>&#8216;use bytes&#8217;</code> aware, handles get magic, and will coerce its args to strings if necessary. See also <code>&quot;sv_cmp_locale&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 sv_cmp(SV *const sv1, SV *const sv2)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_cmp_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Compares the strings in two SVs. Returns -1, 0, or 1 indicating whether the string in <code>&quot;sv1&quot;</code> is less than, equal to, or greater than the string in <code>&quot;sv2&quot;</code>. Is UTF-8 and <code>&#8216;use bytes&#8217;</code> aware and will coerce its args to strings if necessary. If the flags has the <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, it handles get magic. See also <code>&quot;sv_cmp_locale_flags&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 sv_cmp_flags(SV *const sv1, SV *const sv2,
                             const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_cmp_locale</dt>
<dd> <p> Compares the strings in two SVs in a locale-aware manner. Is UTF-8 and <code>&#8216;use bytes&#8217;</code> aware, handles get magic, and will coerce its args to strings if necessary. See also <code>&quot;sv_cmp&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 sv_cmp_locale(SV *const sv1, SV *const sv2)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_cmp_locale_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Compares the strings in two SVs in a locale-aware manner. Is UTF-8 and <code>&#8216;use bytes&#8217;</code> aware and will coerce its args to strings if necessary. If the flags contain <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code>, it handles get magic. See also <code>&quot;sv_cmp_flags&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 sv_cmp_locale_flags(SV *const sv1,
                                    SV *const sv2,
                                    const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_collxfrm</dt>
<dd> <p> This calls <code>&quot;sv_collxfrm_flags&quot;</code> with the SV_GMAGIC flag. See <code>&quot;sv_collxfrm_flags&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_collxfrm(SV *const sv, STRLEN *const nxp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_collxfrm_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Add Collate Transform magic to an SV if it doesn&#8217;t already have it. If the flags contain <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code>, it handles get-magic. </p>
<p>Any scalar variable may carry <code>&quot;PERL_MAGIC_collxfrm&quot;</code> magic that contains the scalar data of the variable, but transformed to such a format that a normal memory comparison can be used to compare the data according to the locale settings. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_collxfrm_flags(SV *const sv,
                                  STRLEN *const nxp,
                                  I32 const flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_copypv</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies a stringified representation of the source SV into the destination SV. Automatically performs any necessary <code>&quot;mg_get&quot;</code> and coercion of numeric values into strings. Guaranteed to preserve <code>&quot;UTF8&quot;</code> flag even from overloaded objects. Similar in nature to <code>&quot;sv_2pv[_flags]&quot;</code> but operates directly on an SV instead of just the string. Mostly uses <code>&quot;sv_2pv_flags&quot;</code> to do its work, except when that would lose the UTF-8&#8216;ness of the PV. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_copypv(SV *const dsv, SV *const ssv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_copypv_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Implementation of <code>&quot;sv_copypv&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;sv_copypv_nomg&quot;</code>. Calls get magic iff flags has the <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_copypv_flags(SV *const dsv, SV *const ssv,
                                const I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_copypv_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_copypv&quot;</code>, but doesn&#8217;t invoke get magic first. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_copypv_nomg(SV *const dsv, SV *const ssv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvCUR</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the length of the string which is in the SV. See <code>&quot;SvLEN&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN SvCUR(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvCUR_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Set the current length of the string which is in the SV. See <code>&quot;SvCUR&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SvIV_set&quot;</code>&gt;. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvCUR_set(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_dec</dt>
<dd> <p> Auto-decrement of the value in the SV, doing string to numeric conversion if necessary. Handles &#8216;get&#8217; magic and operator overloading. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_dec(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_dec_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Auto-decrement of the value in the SV, doing string to numeric conversion if necessary. Handles operator overloading. Skips handling &#8216;get&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_dec_nomg(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_derived_from</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``sv_derived_from_pv&#8217;&#8216;, but doesn&#8217;t take a <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_derived_from(SV* sv, const char *const name)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_derived_from_pv</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``sv_derived_from_pvn&#8217;&#8216;, but takes a nul-terminated string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_derived_from_pv(SV* sv,
                                   const char *const name,
                                   U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_derived_from_pvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the SV is derived from the specified class <i>at the C level</i>. To check derivation at the Perl level, call <code>&quot;isa()&quot;</code> as a normal Perl method. </p>
<p>Currently, the only significant value for <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is SVf_UTF8. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_derived_from_pvn(SV* sv,
                                    const char *const name,
                                    const STRLEN len, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_derived_from_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly like ``sv_derived_from_pvn&#8217;&#8216;, but takes the name string in the form of an SV instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_derived_from_sv(SV* sv, SV *namesv,
                                   U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_does</dt>
<dd> <p> Like ``sv_does_pv&#8217;&#8216;, but doesn&#8217;t take a <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_does(SV* sv, const char *const name)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_does_pv</dt>
<dd> <p> Like ``sv_does_sv&#8217;&#8216;, but takes a nul-terminated string instead of an SV. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_does_pv(SV* sv, const char *const name,
                           U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_does_pvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Like ``sv_does_sv&#8217;&#8216;, but takes a string/length pair instead of an SV. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_does_pvn(SV* sv, const char *const name,
                            const STRLEN len, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_does_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the SV performs a specific, named role. The SV can be a Perl object or the name of a Perl class. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_does_sv(SV* sv, SV* namesv, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvEND</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a pointer to the spot just after the last character in the string which is in the SV, where there is usually a trailing <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> character (even though Perl scalars do not strictly require it). See <code>&quot;SvCUR&quot;</code>. Access the character as <code>&quot;*(SvEND(sv))&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Warning: If <code>&quot;SvCUR&quot;</code> is equal to <code>&quot;SvLEN&quot;</code>, then <code>&quot;SvEND&quot;</code> points to unallocated memory. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvEND(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_eq</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the strings in the two SVs are identical. Is UTF-8 and <code>&#8216;use bytes&#8217;</code> aware, handles get magic, and will coerce its args to strings if necessary. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 sv_eq(SV* sv1, SV* sv2)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_eq_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the strings in the two SVs are identical. Is UTF-8 and <code>&#8216;use bytes&#8217;</code> aware and coerces its args to strings if necessary. If the flags has the <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, it handles get-magic, too. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 sv_eq_flags(SV* sv1, SV* sv2, const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_force_normal_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Undo various types of fakery on an SV, where fakery means ``more than&#8217;&#8216; a string: if the PV is a shared string, make a private copy; if we&#8217;re a ref, stop refing; if we&#8217;re a glob, downgrade to an <code>&quot;xpvmg&quot;</code>; if we&#8217;re a copy-on-write scalar, this is the on-write time when we do the copy, and is also used locally; if this is a vstring, drop the vstring magic. If <code>&quot;SV_COW_DROP_PV&quot;</code> is set then a copy-on-write scalar drops its PV buffer (if any) and becomes <code>&quot;SvPOK_off&quot;</code> rather than making a copy. (Used where this scalar is about to be set to some other value.) In addition, the <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter gets passed to <code>&quot;sv_unref_flags()&quot;</code> when unreffing. <code>&quot;sv_force_normal&quot;</code> calls this function with flags set to 0. </p>
<p>This function is expected to be used to signal to perl that this SV is about to be written to, and any extra book-keeping needs to be taken care of. Hence, it croaks on read-only values. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_force_normal_flags(SV *const sv,
                                      const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_free</dt>
<dd> <p> Decrement an SV&#8216;s reference count, and if it drops to zero, call <code>&quot;sv_clear&quot;</code> to invoke destructors and free up any memory used by the body; finally, deallocating the SV&#8216;s head itself. Normally called via a wrapper macro <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_dec&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_free(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvGAMAGIC</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns true if the SV has get magic or overloading. If either is true then the scalar is active data, and has the potential to return a new value every time it is accessed. Hence you must be careful to only read it once per user logical operation and work with that returned value. If neither is true then the scalar&#8217;s value cannot change unless written to. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvGAMAGIC(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_gets</dt>
<dd> <p> Get a line from the filehandle and store it into the SV, optionally appending to the currently-stored string. If <code>&quot;append&quot;</code> is not 0, the line is appended to the SV instead of overwriting it. <code>&quot;append&quot;</code> should be set to the byte offset that the appended string should start at in the SV (typically, <code>&quot;SvCUR(sv)&quot;</code> is a suitable choice). </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_gets(SV *const sv, PerlIO *const fp,
                        I32 append)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_get_backrefs</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is the target of a weak reference then it returns the back references structure associated with the sv; otherwise return <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>When returning a non-null result the type of the return is relevant. If it is an AV then the elements of the AV are the weak reference RVs which point at this item. If it is any other type then the item itself is the weak reference. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;Perl_sv_add_backref()&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;Perl_sv_del_backref()&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;Perl_sv_kill_backrefs()&quot;</code> </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_get_backrefs(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvGROW</dt>
<dd> <p> Expands the character buffer in the SV so that it has room for the indicated number of bytes (remember to reserve space for an extra trailing <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> character). Calls <code>&quot;sv_grow&quot;</code> to perform the expansion if necessary. Returns a pointer to the character buffer. SV must be of type &gt;= <code>&quot;SVt_PV&quot;</code>. One alternative is to call <code>&quot;sv_grow&quot;</code> if you are not sure of the type of SV. </p>
<p>You might mistakenly think that <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> is the number of bytes to add to the existing size, but instead it is the total size <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> should be. </p>
<pre><code>        char * SvGROW(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_grow</dt>
<dd> <p> Expands the character buffer in the SV. If necessary, uses <code>&quot;sv_unref&quot;</code> and upgrades the SV to <code>&quot;SVt_PV&quot;</code>. Returns a pointer to the character buffer. Use the <code>&quot;SvGROW&quot;</code> wrapper instead. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_grow(SV *const sv, STRLEN newlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_inc</dt>
<dd> <p> Auto-increment of the value in the SV, doing string to numeric conversion if necessary. Handles &#8216;get&#8217; magic and operator overloading. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_inc(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_inc_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Auto-increment of the value in the SV, doing string to numeric conversion if necessary. Handles operator overloading. Skips handling &#8216;get&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_inc_nomg(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_insert</dt>
<dd> <p> Inserts and/or replaces a string at the specified offset/length within the SV. Similar to the Perl <code>&quot;substr()&quot;</code> function, with <code>&quot;littlelen&quot;</code> bytes starting at <code>&quot;little&quot;</code> replacing <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> bytes of the string in <code>&quot;bigstr&quot;</code> starting at <code>&quot;offset&quot;</code>. Handles get magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_insert(SV *const bigstr, const STRLEN offset,
