                          const STRLEN len,
                          const char *const little,
                          const STRLEN littlelen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_insert_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Same as <code>&quot;sv_insert&quot;</code>, but the extra <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> are passed to the <code>&quot;SvPV_force_flags&quot;</code> that applies to <code>&quot;bigstr&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_insert_flags(SV *const bigstr,
                                const STRLEN offset,
                                const STRLEN len,
                                const char *little,
                                const STRLEN littlelen,
                                const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIOK</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a U32 value indicating whether the SV contains an integer. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvIOK(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIOK_notUV</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the SV contains a signed integer. </p>
<pre><code>        bool SvIOK_notUV(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIOK_off</dt>
<dd> <p> Unsets the IV status of an SV. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvIOK_off(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIOK_on</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells an SV that it is an integer. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvIOK_on(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIOK_only</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells an SV that it is an integer and disables all other <code>&quot;OK&quot;</code> bits. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvIOK_only(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIOK_only_UV</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells an SV that it is an unsigned integer and disables all other <code>&quot;OK&quot;</code> bits. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvIOK_only_UV(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIOKp</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a U32 value indicating whether the SV contains an integer. Checks the <code>private</code> setting. Use <code>&quot;SvIOK&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvIOKp(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIOK_UV</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the SV contains an integer that must be interpreted as unsigned. A non-negative integer whose value is within the range of both an IV and a UV may be be flagged as either <code>&quot;SvUOK&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;SVIOK&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool SvIOK_UV(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_isa</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the SV is blessed into the specified class. This does not check for subtypes; use <code>&quot;sv_derived_from&quot;</code> to verify an inheritance relationship. </p>
<pre><code>        int sv_isa(SV* sv, const char *const name)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIsCOW</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a U32 value indicating whether the SV is Copy-On-Write (either shared hash key scalars, or full Copy On Write scalars if 5.9.0 is configured for COW). </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvIsCOW(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIsCOW_shared_hash</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the SV is Copy-On-Write shared hash key scalar. </p>
<pre><code>        bool SvIsCOW_shared_hash(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_isobject</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the SV is an RV pointing to a blessed object. If the SV is not an RV, or if the object is not blessed, then this will return false. </p>
<pre><code>        int sv_isobject(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIV</dt>
<dd> <p> Coerces the given SV to IV and returns it. The returned value in many circumstances will get stored in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>&#8216;s IV slot, but not in all cases. (Use <code>&quot;sv_setiv&quot;</code> to make sure it does). </p>
<p>See <code>&quot;SvIVx&quot;</code> for a version which guarantees to evaluate <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> only once. </p>
<pre><code>        IV SvIV(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIV_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvIV&quot;</code> but doesn&#8217;t process magic. </p>
<pre><code>        IV SvIV_nomg(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIV_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Set the value of the IV pointer in sv to val. It is possible to perform the same function of this macro with an lvalue assignment to <code>&quot;SvIVX&quot;</code>. With future Perls, however, it will be more efficient to use <code>&quot;SvIV_set&quot;</code> instead of the lvalue assignment to <code>&quot;SvIVX&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvIV_set(SV* sv, IV val)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIVX</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the raw value in the SV&#8216;s IV slot, without checks or conversions. Only use when you are sure <code>&quot;SvIOK&quot;</code> is true. See also <code>&quot;SvIV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        IV SvIVX(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvIVx</dt>
<dd> <p> Coerces the given SV to IV and returns it. The returned value in many circumstances will get stored in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>&#8216;s IV slot, but not in all cases. (Use <code>&quot;sv_setiv&quot;</code> to make sure it does). </p>
<p>This form guarantees to evaluate <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> only once. Only use this if <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is an expression with side effects, otherwise use the more efficient <code>&quot;SvIV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        IV SvIVx(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvLEN</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the size of the string buffer in the SV, not including any part attributable to <code>&quot;SvOOK&quot;</code>. See <code>&quot;SvCUR&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN SvLEN(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_len</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the length of the string in the SV. Handles magic and type coercion and sets the UTF8 flag appropriately. See also <code>&quot;SvCUR&quot;</code>, which gives raw access to the <code>&quot;xpv_cur&quot;</code> slot. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN sv_len(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvLEN_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Set the size of the string buffer for the SV. See <code>&quot;SvLEN&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvLEN_set(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_len_utf8</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the number of characters in the string in an SV, counting wide UTF-8 bytes as a single character. Handles magic and type coercion. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN sv_len_utf8(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_magic</dt>
<dd> <p> Adds magic to an SV. First upgrades <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to type <code>&quot;SVt_PVMG&quot;</code> if necessary, then adds a new magic item of type <code>&quot;how&quot;</code> to the head of the magic list. </p>
<p>See <code>&quot;sv_magicext&quot;</code> (which <code>&quot;sv_magic&quot;</code> now calls) for a description of the handling of the <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;namlen&quot;</code> arguments. </p>
<p>You need to use <code>&quot;sv_magicext&quot;</code> to add magic to <code>&quot;SvREADONLY&quot;</code> SVs and also to add more than one instance of the same <code>&quot;how&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_magic(SV *const sv, SV *const obj,
                         const int how, const char *const name,
                         const I32 namlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_magicext</dt>
<dd> <p> Adds magic to an SV, upgrading it if necessary. Applies the supplied <code>&quot;vtable&quot;</code> and returns a pointer to the magic added. </p>
<p>Note that <code>&quot;sv_magicext&quot;</code> will allow things that <code>&quot;sv_magic&quot;</code> will not. In particular, you can add magic to <code>&quot;SvREADONLY&quot;</code> SVs, and add more than one instance of the same <code>&quot;how&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;namlen&quot;</code> is greater than zero then a <code>&quot;savepvn&quot;</code> <i>copy</i> of <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> is stored, if <code>&quot;namlen&quot;</code> is zero then <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> is stored as-is and - as another special case - if <code>&quot;(name &amp;&amp; namlen == HEf_SVKEY)&quot;</code> then <code>&quot;name&quot;</code> is assumed to contain an SV* and is stored as-is with its <code>&quot;REFCNT&quot;</code> incremented. </p>
<p>(This is now used as a subroutine by <code>&quot;sv_magic&quot;</code>.) </p>
<pre><code>        MAGIC * sv_magicext(SV *const sv, SV *const obj,
                            const int how,
                            const MGVTBL *const vtbl,
                            const char *const name,
                            const I32 namlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvMAGIC_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Set the value of the MAGIC pointer in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to val. See <code>&quot;SvIV_set&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvMAGIC_set(SV* sv, MAGIC* val)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_mortalcopy</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new SV which is a copy of the original SV (using <code>&quot;sv_setsv&quot;</code>). The new SV is marked as mortal. It will be destroyed ``soon&#8217;&#8216;, either by an explicit call to <code>&quot;FREETMPS&quot;</code>, or by an implicit call at places such as statement boundaries. See also <code>&quot;sv_newmortal&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;sv_2mortal&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_mortalcopy(SV *const oldsv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_newmortal</dt>
<dd> <p> Creates a new null SV which is mortal. The reference count of the SV is set to 1. It will be destroyed ``soon&#8217;&#8216;, either by an explicit call to <code>&quot;FREETMPS&quot;</code>, or by an implicit call at places such as statement boundaries. See also <code>&quot;sv_mortalcopy&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;sv_2mortal&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_newmortal()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_newref</dt>
<dd> <p> Increment an SV&#8216;s reference count. Use the <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc()&quot;</code> wrapper instead. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_newref(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNIOK</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a U32 value indicating whether the SV contains a number, integer or double. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvNIOK(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNIOK_off</dt>
<dd> <p> Unsets the NV/IV status of an SV. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvNIOK_off(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNIOKp</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a U32 value indicating whether the SV contains a number, integer or double. Checks the <code>private</code> setting. Use <code>&quot;SvNIOK&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvNIOKp(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNOK</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a U32 value indicating whether the SV contains a double. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvNOK(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNOK_off</dt>
<dd> <p> Unsets the NV status of an SV. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvNOK_off(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNOK_on</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells an SV that it is a double. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvNOK_on(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNOK_only</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells an SV that it is a double and disables all other OK bits. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvNOK_only(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNOKp</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a U32 value indicating whether the SV contains a double. Checks the <code>private</code> setting. Use <code>&quot;SvNOK&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvNOKp(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNV</dt>
<dd> <p> Coerces the given SV to NV and returns it. The returned value in many circumstances will get stored in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>&#8216;s NV slot, but not in all cases. (Use <code>&quot;sv_setnv&quot;</code> to make sure it does). </p>
<p>See <code>&quot;SvNVx&quot;</code> for a version which guarantees to evaluate <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> only once. </p>
<pre><code>        NV SvNV(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNV_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvNV&quot;</code> but doesn&#8217;t process magic. </p>
<pre><code>        NV SvNV_nomg(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNV_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Set the value of the NV pointer in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to val. See <code>&quot;SvIV_set&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvNV_set(SV* sv, NV val)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNVX</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the raw value in the SV&#8216;s NV slot, without checks or conversions. Only use when you are sure <code>&quot;SvNOK&quot;</code> is true. See also <code>&quot;SvNV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        NV SvNVX(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvNVx</dt>
<dd> <p> Coerces the given SV to NV and returns it. The returned value in many circumstances will get stored in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>&#8216;s NV slot, but not in all cases. (Use <code>&quot;sv_setnv&quot;</code> to make sure it does). </p>
<p>This form guarantees to evaluate <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> only once. Only use this if <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is an expression with side effects, otherwise use the more efficient <code>&quot;SvNV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        NV SvNVx(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvOK</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a U32 value indicating whether the value is defined. This is only meaningful for scalars. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvOK(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvOOK</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a U32 indicating whether the pointer to the string buffer is offset. This hack is used internally to speed up removal of characters from the beginning of a <code>&quot;SvPV&quot;</code>. When <code>&quot;SvOOK&quot;</code> is true, then the start of the allocated string buffer is actually <code>&quot;SvOOK_offset()&quot;</code> bytes before <code>&quot;SvPVX&quot;</code>. This offset used to be stored in <code>&quot;SvIVX&quot;</code>, but is now stored within the spare part of the buffer. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvOOK(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvOOK_offset</dt>
<dd> <p> Reads into <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> the offset from <code>&quot;SvPVX&quot;</code> back to the true start of the allocated buffer, which will be non-zero if <code>&quot;sv_chop&quot;</code> has been used to efficiently remove characters from start of the buffer. Implemented as a macro, which takes the address of <code>&quot;len&quot;</code>, which must be of type <code>&quot;STRLEN&quot;</code>. Evaluates <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> more than once. Sets <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> to 0 if <code>&quot;SvOOK(sv)&quot;</code> is false. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvOOK_offset(SV*sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPOK</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a U32 value indicating whether the SV contains a character string. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvPOK(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPOK_off</dt>
<dd> <p> Unsets the PV status of an SV. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvPOK_off(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPOK_on</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells an SV that it is a string. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvPOK_on(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPOK_only</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells an SV that it is a string and disables all other <code>&quot;OK&quot;</code> bits. Will also turn off the UTF-8 status. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvPOK_only(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPOK_only_UTF8</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells an SV that it is a string and disables all other <code>&quot;OK&quot;</code> bits, and leaves the UTF-8 status as it was. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvPOK_only_UTF8(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPOKp</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a U32 value indicating whether the SV contains a character string. Checks the <code>private</code> setting. Use <code>&quot;SvPOK&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvPOKp(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pos_b2u</dt>
<dd> <p> Converts the value pointed to by <code>&quot;offsetp&quot;</code> from a count of bytes from the start of the string, to a count of the equivalent number of UTF-8 chars. Handles magic and type coercion. </p>
<p>Use <code>&quot;sv_pos_b2u_flags&quot;</code> in preference, which correctly handles strings longer than 2Gb. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_pos_b2u(SV *const sv, I32 *const offsetp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pos_b2u_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Converts <code>&quot;offset&quot;</code> from a count of bytes from the start of the string, to a count of the equivalent number of UTF-8 chars. Handles type coercion. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is passed to <code>&quot;SvPV_flags&quot;</code>, and usually should be <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC|SV_CONST_RETURN&quot;</code> to handle magic. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN sv_pos_b2u_flags(SV *const sv,
                                 STRLEN const offset, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pos_u2b</dt>
<dd> <p> Converts the value pointed to by <code>&quot;offsetp&quot;</code> from a count of UTF-8 chars from the start of the string, to a count of the equivalent number of bytes; if <code>&quot;lenp&quot;</code> is non-zero, it does the same to <code>&quot;lenp&quot;</code>, but this time starting from the offset, rather than from the start of the string. Handles magic and type coercion. </p>
<p>Use <code>&quot;sv_pos_u2b_flags&quot;</code> in preference, which correctly handles strings longer than 2Gb. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_pos_u2b(SV *const sv, I32 *const offsetp,
                           I32 *const lenp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pos_u2b_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Converts the offset from a count of UTF-8 chars from the start of the string, to a count of the equivalent number of bytes; if <code>&quot;lenp&quot;</code> is non-zero, it does the same to <code>&quot;lenp&quot;</code>, but this time starting from <code>&quot;offset&quot;</code>, rather than from the start of the string. Handles type coercion. <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is passed to <code>&quot;SvPV_flags&quot;</code>, and usually should be <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC|SV_CONST_RETURN&quot;</code> to handle magic. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN sv_pos_u2b_flags(SV *const sv, STRLEN uoffset,
                                 STRLEN *const lenp, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPV</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a pointer to the string in the SV, or a stringified form of the SV if the SV does not contain a string. The SV may cache the stringified version becoming <code>&quot;SvPOK&quot;</code>. Handles &#8216;get&#8217; magic. The <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> variable will be set to the length of the string (this is a macro, so don&#8217;t use <code>&amp;len</code>). See also <code>&quot;SvPVx&quot;</code> for a version which guarantees to evaluate <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> only once. </p>
<p>Note that there is no guarantee that the return value of <code>&quot;SvPV()&quot;</code> is equal to <code>&quot;SvPVX(sv)&quot;</code>, or that <code>&quot;SvPVX(sv)&quot;</code> contains valid data, or that successive calls to <code>&quot;SvPV(sv)&quot;</code> will return the same pointer value each time. This is due to the way that things like overloading and Copy-On-Write are handled. In these cases, the return value may point to a temporary buffer or similar. If you absolutely need the <code>&quot;SvPVX&quot;</code> field to be valid (for example, if you intend to write to it), then see <code>&quot;SvPV_force&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPV(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVbyte</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV&quot;</code>, but converts <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to byte representation first if necessary. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPVbyte(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVbyte_force</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV_force&quot;</code>, but converts <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to byte representation first if necessary. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPVbyte_force(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVbyte_nolen</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV_nolen&quot;</code>, but converts <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to byte representation first if necessary. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPVbyte_nolen(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pvbyten_force</dt>
<dd> <p> The backend for the <code>&quot;SvPVbytex_force&quot;</code> macro. Always use the macro instead. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_pvbyten_force(SV *const sv, STRLEN *const lp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVbytex</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV&quot;</code>, but converts <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to byte representation first if necessary. Guarantees to evaluate <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> only once; use the more efficient <code>&quot;SvPVbyte&quot;</code> otherwise. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPVbytex(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVbytex_force</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV_force&quot;</code>, but converts <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to byte representation first if necessary. Guarantees to evaluate <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> only once; use the more efficient <code>&quot;SvPVbyte_force&quot;</code> otherwise. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPVbytex_force(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVCLEAR</dt>
<dd> <p> Ensures that sv is a SVt_PV and that its SvCUR is 0, and that it is properly null terminated. Equivalent to sv_setpvs(&quot;&quot;), but more efficient. </p>
<pre><code>        char * SvPVCLEAR(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPV_force</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV&quot;</code> but will force the SV into containing a string (<code>&quot;SvPOK&quot;</code>), and only a string (<code>&quot;SvPOK_only&quot;</code>), by hook or by crook. You need force if you are going to update the <code>&quot;SvPVX&quot;</code> directly. Processes get magic. </p>
<p>Note that coercing an arbitrary scalar into a plain PV will potentially strip useful data from it. For example if the SV was <code>&quot;SvROK&quot;</code>, then the referent will have its reference count decremented, and the SV itself may be converted to an <code>&quot;SvPOK&quot;</code> scalar with a string buffer containing a value such as <code>&quot;ARRAY(0x1234)&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPV_force(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPV_force_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV_force&quot;</code>, but doesn&#8217;t process get magic. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPV_force_nomg(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPV_nolen</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV&quot;</code> but doesn&#8217;t set a length variable. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPV_nolen(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPV_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV&quot;</code> but doesn&#8217;t process magic. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPV_nomg(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPV_nomg_nolen</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV_nolen&quot;</code> but doesn&#8217;t process magic. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPV_nomg_nolen(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pvn_force</dt>
<dd> <p> Get a sensible string out of the SV somehow. A private implementation of the <code>&quot;SvPV_force&quot;</code> macro for compilers which can&#8217;t cope with complex macro expressions. Always use the macro instead. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_pvn_force(SV* sv, STRLEN* lp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pvn_force_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Get a sensible string out of the SV somehow. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has the <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, will <code>&quot;mg_get&quot;</code> on <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> if appropriate, else not. <code>&quot;sv_pvn_force&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;sv_pvn_force_nomg&quot;</code> are implemented in terms of this function. You normally want to use the various wrapper macros instead: see <code>&quot;SvPV_force&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SvPV_force_nomg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_pvn_force_flags(SV *const sv,
                                   STRLEN *const lp,
                                   const I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPV_set</dt>
<dd> <p> This is probably not what you want to use, you probably wanted ``sv_usepvn_flags&#8217;&#8216; or ``sv_setpvn&#8217;&#8216; or ``sv_setpvs&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>Set the value of the PV pointer in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to the Perl allocated <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated string <code>&quot;val&quot;</code>. See also <code>&quot;SvIV_set&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Remember to free the previous PV buffer. There are many things to check. Beware that the existing pointer may be involved in copy-on-write or other mischief, so do <code>&quot;SvOOK_off(sv)&quot;</code> and use <code>&quot;sv_force_normal&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;SvPV_force&quot;</code> (or check the <code>&quot;SvIsCOW&quot;</code> flag) first to make sure this modification is safe. Then finally, if it is not a COW, call <code>&quot;SvPV_free&quot;</code> to free the previous PV buffer. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvPV_set(SV* sv, char* val)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVutf8</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV&quot;</code>, but converts <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to UTF-8 first if necessary. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPVutf8(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_pvutf8n_force</dt>
<dd> <p> The backend for the <code>&quot;SvPVutf8x_force&quot;</code> macro. Always use the macro instead. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_pvutf8n_force(SV *const sv, STRLEN *const lp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVutf8x</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV&quot;</code>, but converts <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to UTF-8 first if necessary. Guarantees to evaluate <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> only once; use the more efficient <code>&quot;SvPVutf8&quot;</code> otherwise. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPVutf8x(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVutf8x_force</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV_force&quot;</code>, but converts <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to UTF-8 first if necessary. Guarantees to evaluate <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> only once; use the more efficient <code>&quot;SvPVutf8_force&quot;</code> otherwise. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPVutf8x_force(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVutf8_force</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV_force&quot;</code>, but converts <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to UTF-8 first if necessary. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPVutf8_force(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVutf8_nolen</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvPV_nolen&quot;</code>, but converts <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to UTF-8 first if necessary. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPVutf8_nolen(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVX</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a pointer to the physical string in the SV. The SV must contain a string. Prior to 5.9.3 it is not safe to execute this macro unless the SV&#8216;s type &gt;= <code>&quot;SVt_PV&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>This is also used to store the name of an autoloaded subroutine in an XS AUTOLOAD routine. See ``Autoloading with XSUBs&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPVX(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvPVx</dt>
<dd> <p> A version of <code>&quot;SvPV&quot;</code> which guarantees to evaluate <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> only once. Only use this if <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is an expression with side effects, otherwise use the more efficient <code>&quot;SvPV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        char* SvPVx(SV* sv, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREADONLY</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns true if the argument is readonly, otherwise returns false. Exposed to to perl code via <code>Internals::SvREADONLY()</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvREADONLY(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREADONLY_off</dt>
<dd> <p> Mark an object as not-readonly. Exactly what this mean depends on the object type. Exposed to perl code via <code>Internals::SvREADONLY()</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvREADONLY_off(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREADONLY_on</dt>
<dd> <p> Mark an object as readonly. Exactly what this means depends on the object type. Exposed to perl code via <code>Internals::SvREADONLY()</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvREADONLY_on(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_ref</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a SV describing what the SV passed in is a reference to. </p>
<p>dst can be a SV to be set to the description or NULL, in which case a mortal SV is returned. </p>
<p>If ob is true and the SV is blessed, the description is the class name, otherwise it is the type of the SV, ``SCALAR&#8217;&#8216;, ``ARRAY&#8217;&#8216; etc. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_ref(SV *dst, const SV *const sv,
                       const int ob)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREFCNT</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the value of the object&#8217;s reference count. Exposed to perl code via <code>Internals::SvREFCNT()</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvREFCNT(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREFCNT_dec</dt>
<dd> <p> Decrements the reference count of the given SV. <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> may be <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvREFCNT_dec(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREFCNT_dec_NN</dt>
<dd> <p> Same as <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_dec&quot;</code>, but can only be used if you know <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is not <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>. Since we don&#8217;t have to check the NULLness, it&#8217;s faster and smaller. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvREFCNT_dec_NN(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREFCNT_inc</dt>
<dd> <p> Increments the reference count of the given SV, returning the SV. </p>
<p>All of the following <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc&quot;</code>* macros are optimized versions of <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc&quot;</code>, and can be replaced with <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* SvREFCNT_inc(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREFCNT_inc_NN</dt>
<dd> <p> Same as <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc&quot;</code>, but can only be used if you know <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is not <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>. Since we don&#8217;t have to check the NULLness, it&#8217;s faster and smaller. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* SvREFCNT_inc_NN(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREFCNT_inc_simple</dt>
<dd> <p> Same as <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc&quot;</code>, but can only be used with expressions without side effects. Since we don&#8217;t have to store a temporary value, it&#8217;s faster. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* SvREFCNT_inc_simple(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREFCNT_inc_simple_NN</dt>
<dd> <p> Same as <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc_simple&quot;</code>, but can only be used if you know <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is not <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>. Since we don&#8217;t have to check the NULLness, it&#8217;s faster and smaller. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* SvREFCNT_inc_simple_NN(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREFCNT_inc_simple_void</dt>
<dd> <p> Same as <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc_simple&quot;</code>, but can only be used if you don&#8217;t need the return value. The macro doesn&#8217;t need to return a meaningful value. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvREFCNT_inc_simple_void(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREFCNT_inc_simple_void_NN</dt>
<dd> <p> Same as <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc&quot;</code>, but can only be used if you don&#8217;t need the return value, and you know that <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is not <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>. The macro doesn&#8217;t need to return a meaningful value, or check for NULLness, so it&#8217;s smaller and faster. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvREFCNT_inc_simple_void_NN(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREFCNT_inc_void</dt>
<dd> <p> Same as <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc&quot;</code>, but can only be used if you don&#8217;t need the return value. The macro doesn&#8217;t need to return a meaningful value. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvREFCNT_inc_void(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvREFCNT_inc_void_NN</dt>
<dd> <p> Same as <code>&quot;SvREFCNT_inc&quot;</code>, but can only be used if you don&#8217;t need the return value, and you know that <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is not <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>. The macro doesn&#8217;t need to return a meaningful value, or check for NULLness, so it&#8217;s smaller and faster. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvREFCNT_inc_void_NN(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_reftype</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a string describing what the SV is a reference to. </p>
<p>If ob is true and the SV is blessed, the string is the class name, otherwise it is the type of the SV, ``SCALAR&#8217;&#8216;, ``ARRAY&#8217;&#8216; etc. </p>
<pre><code>        const char* sv_reftype(const SV *const sv, const int ob)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_replace</dt>
<dd> <p> Make the first argument a copy of the second, then delete the original. The target SV physically takes over ownership of the body of the source SV and inherits its flags; however, the target keeps any magic it owns, and any magic in the source is discarded. Note that this is a rather specialist SV copying operation; most of the time you&#8217;ll want to use <code>&quot;sv_setsv&quot;</code> or one of its many macro front-ends. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_replace(SV *const sv, SV *const nsv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_report_used</dt>
<dd> <p> Dump the contents of all SVs not yet freed (debugging aid). </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_report_used()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_reset</dt>
<dd> <p> Underlying implementation for the <code>&quot;reset&quot;</code> Perl function. Note that the perl-level function is vaguely deprecated. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_reset(const char* s, HV *const stash)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvROK</dt>
<dd> <p> Tests if the SV is an RV. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvROK(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvROK_off</dt>
<dd> <p> Unsets the RV status of an SV. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvROK_off(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvROK_on</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells an SV that it is an RV. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvROK_on(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvRV</dt>
<dd> <p> Dereferences an RV to return the SV. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* SvRV(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvRV_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Set the value of the RV pointer in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to val. See <code>&quot;SvIV_set&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvRV_set(SV* sv, SV* val)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_rvunweaken</dt>
<dd> <p> Unweaken a reference: Clear the <code>&quot;SvWEAKREF&quot;</code> flag on this RV; remove the backreference to this RV from the array of backreferences associated with the target SV, increment the refcount of the target. Silently ignores <code>&quot;undef&quot;</code> and warns on non-weak references. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_rvunweaken(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_rvweaken</dt>
<dd> <p> Weaken a reference: set the <code>&quot;SvWEAKREF&quot;</code> flag on this RV; give the referred-to SV <code>&quot;PERL_MAGIC_backref&quot;</code> magic if it hasn&#8217;t already; and push a back-reference to this RV onto the array of backreferences associated with that magic. If the RV is magical, set magic will be called after the RV is cleared. Silently ignores <code>&quot;undef&quot;</code> and warns on already-weak references. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_rvweaken(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setiv</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies an integer into the given SV, upgrading first if necessary. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See also <code>&quot;sv_setiv_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setiv(SV *const sv, const IV num)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setiv_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_setiv&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setiv_mg(SV *const sv, const IV i)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setnv</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies a double into the given SV, upgrading first if necessary. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See also <code>&quot;sv_setnv_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setnv(SV *const sv, const NV num)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setnv_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_setnv&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setnv_mg(SV *const sv, const NV num)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setpv</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies a string into an SV. The string must be terminated with a <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> character, and not contain embeded <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>&#8216;s. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See <code>&quot;sv_setpv_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setpv(SV *const sv, const char *const ptr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setpvf</dt>
<dd> <p> Works like <code>&quot;sv_catpvf&quot;</code> but copies the text into the SV instead of appending it. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See <code>&quot;sv_setpvf_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setpvf(SV *const sv, const char *const pat,
                          ...)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setpvf_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_setpvf&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setpvf_mg(SV *const sv,
                             const char *const pat, ...)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setpviv</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies an integer into the given SV, also updating its string value. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See <code>&quot;sv_setpviv_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setpviv(SV *const sv, const IV num)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setpviv_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_setpviv&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setpviv_mg(SV *const sv, const IV iv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setpvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies a string (possibly containing embedded <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters) into an SV. The <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> parameter indicates the number of bytes to be copied. If the <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code> argument is NULL the SV will become undefined. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See <code>&quot;sv_setpvn_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setpvn(SV *const sv, const char *const ptr,
                          const STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setpvn_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_setpvn&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setpvn_mg(SV *const sv,
                             const char *const ptr,
                             const STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setpvs</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_setpvn&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setpvs(SV* sv, &quot;literal string&quot; s)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setpvs_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_setpvn_mg&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setpvs_mg(SV* sv, &quot;literal string&quot; s)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setpv_bufsize</dt>
<dd> <p> Sets the SV to be a string of cur bytes length, with at least len bytes available. Ensures that there is a null byte at SvEND. Returns a char * pointer to the SvPV buffer. </p>
<pre><code>        char * sv_setpv_bufsize(SV *const sv, const STRLEN cur,
                                 const STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setpv_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_setpv&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setpv_mg(SV *const sv, const char *const ptr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setref_iv</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies an integer into a new SV, optionally blessing the SV. The <code>&quot;rv&quot;</code> argument will be upgraded to an RV. That RV will be modified to point to the new SV. The <code>&quot;classname&quot;</code> argument indicates the package for the blessing. Set <code>&quot;classname&quot;</code> to <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> to avoid the blessing. The new SV will have a reference count of 1, and the RV will be returned. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_setref_iv(SV *const rv,
                             const char *const classname,
                             const IV iv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setref_nv</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies a double into a new SV, optionally blessing the SV. The <code>&quot;rv&quot;</code> argument will be upgraded to an RV. That RV will be modified to point to the new SV. The <code>&quot;classname&quot;</code> argument indicates the package for the blessing. Set <code>&quot;classname&quot;</code> to <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> to avoid the blessing. The new SV will have a reference count of 1, and the RV will be returned. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_setref_nv(SV *const rv,
                             const char *const classname,
                             const NV nv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setref_pv</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies a pointer into a new SV, optionally blessing the SV. The <code>&quot;rv&quot;</code> argument will be upgraded to an RV. That RV will be modified to point to the new SV. If the <code>&quot;pv&quot;</code> argument is <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>, then <code>&quot;PL_sv_undef&quot;</code> will be placed into the SV. The <code>&quot;classname&quot;</code> argument indicates the package for the blessing. Set <code>&quot;classname&quot;</code> to <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> to avoid the blessing. The new SV will have a reference count of 1, and the RV will be returned. </p>
<p>Do not use with other Perl types such as HV, AV, SV, CV, because those objects will become corrupted by the pointer copy process. </p>
<p>Note that <code>&quot;sv_setref_pvn&quot;</code> copies the string while this copies the pointer. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_setref_pv(SV *const rv,
                             const char *const classname,
                             void *const pv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setref_pvn</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies a string into a new SV, optionally blessing the SV. The length of the string must be specified with <code>&quot;n&quot;</code>. The <code>&quot;rv&quot;</code> argument will be upgraded to an RV. That RV will be modified to point to the new SV. The <code>&quot;classname&quot;</code> argument indicates the package for the blessing. Set <code>&quot;classname&quot;</code> to <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> to avoid the blessing. The new SV will have a reference count of 1, and the RV will be returned. </p>
<p>Note that <code>&quot;sv_setref_pv&quot;</code> copies the pointer while this copies the string. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_setref_pvn(SV *const rv,
                              const char *const classname,
                              const char *const pv,
                              const STRLEN n)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setref_pvs</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_setref_pvn&quot;</code>, but takes a literal string instead of a string/length pair. </p>
<pre><code>        SV * sv_setref_pvs(&quot;literal string&quot; s)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setref_uv</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies an unsigned integer into a new SV, optionally blessing the SV. The <code>&quot;rv&quot;</code> argument will be upgraded to an RV. That RV will be modified to point to the new SV. The <code>&quot;classname&quot;</code> argument indicates the package for the blessing. Set <code>&quot;classname&quot;</code> to <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> to avoid the blessing. The new SV will have a reference count of 1, and the RV will be returned. </p>
<pre><code>        SV* sv_setref_uv(SV *const rv,
                             const char *const classname,
                             const UV uv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setsv</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies the contents of the source SV <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code> into the destination SV <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code>. The source SV may be destroyed if it is mortal, so don&#8217;t use this function if the source SV needs to be reused. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic on destination SV. Calls &#8216;get&#8217; magic on source SV. Loosely speaking, it performs a copy-by-value, obliterating any previous content of the destination. </p>
<p>You probably want to use one of the assortment of wrappers, such as <code>&quot;SvSetSV&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;SvSetSV_nosteal&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;SvSetMagicSV&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SvSetMagicSV_nosteal&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setsv(SV *dstr, SV *sstr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setsv_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies the contents of the source SV <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code> into the destination SV <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code>. The source SV may be destroyed if it is mortal, so don&#8217;t use this function if the source SV needs to be reused. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. Loosely speaking, it performs a copy-by-value, obliterating any previous content of the destination. If the <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter has the <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, will <code>&quot;mg_get&quot;</code> on <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code> if appropriate, else not. If the <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter has the <code>&quot;SV_NOSTEAL&quot;</code> bit set then the buffers of temps will not be stolen. <code>&quot;sv_setsv&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;sv_setsv_nomg&quot;</code> are implemented in terms of this function. </p>
<p>You probably want to use one of the assortment of wrappers, such as <code>&quot;SvSetSV&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;SvSetSV_nosteal&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;SvSetMagicSV&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;SvSetMagicSV_nosteal&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>This is the primary function for copying scalars, and most other copy-ish functions and macros use this underneath. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setsv_flags(SV *dstr, SV *sstr,
                               const I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setsv_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_setsv&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setsv_mg(SV *const dstr, SV *const sstr)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setsv_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_setsv&quot;</code> but doesn&#8217;t process magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setsv_nomg(SV* dsv, SV* ssv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setuv</dt>
<dd> <p> Copies an unsigned integer into the given SV, upgrading first if necessary. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See also <code>&quot;sv_setuv_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setuv(SV *const sv, const UV num)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_setuv_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_setuv&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_setuv_mg(SV *const sv, const UV u)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_set_undef</dt>
<dd> <p> Equivalent to <code>&quot;sv_setsv(sv, &amp;PL_sv_undef)&quot;</code>, but more efficient. Doesn&#8217;t handle set magic. </p>
<p>The perl equivalent is <code>&quot;$sv = undef;&quot;</code>. Note that it doesn&#8217;t free any string buffer, unlike <code>&quot;undef $sv&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Introduced in perl 5.25.12. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_set_undef(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvSTASH</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the stash of the SV. </p>
<pre><code>        HV* SvSTASH(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvSTASH_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Set the value of the STASH pointer in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to val. See <code>&quot;SvIV_set&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvSTASH_set(SV* sv, HV* val)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvTAINT</dt>
<dd> <p> Taints an SV if tainting is enabled, and if some input to the current expression is tainted---usually a variable, but possibly also implicit inputs such as locale settings. <code>&quot;SvTAINT&quot;</code> propagates that taintedness to the outputs of an expression in a pessimistic fashion; i.e., without paying attention to precisely which outputs are influenced by which inputs. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvTAINT(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvTAINTED</dt>
<dd> <p> Checks to see if an SV is tainted. Returns TRUE if it is, FALSE if not. </p>
<pre><code>        bool SvTAINTED(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_tainted</dt>
<dd> <p> Test an SV for taintedness. Use <code>&quot;SvTAINTED&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_tainted(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvTAINTED_off</dt>
<dd> <p> Untaints an SV. Be <i>very</i> careful with this routine, as it short-circuits some of Perl&#8217;s fundamental security features. XS module authors should not use this function unless they fully understand all the implications of unconditionally untainting the value. Untainting should be done in the standard perl fashion, via a carefully crafted regexp, rather than directly untainting variables. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvTAINTED_off(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvTAINTED_on</dt>
<dd> <p> Marks an SV as tainted if tainting is enabled. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvTAINTED_on(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvTRUE</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether Perl would evaluate the SV as true or false. See <code>&quot;SvOK&quot;</code> for a defined/undefined test. Handles &#8216;get&#8217; magic unless the scalar is already <code>&quot;SvPOK&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;SvIOK&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;SvNOK&quot;</code> (the public, not the private flags). </p>
<pre><code>        bool SvTRUE(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_true</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns true if the SV has a true value by Perl&#8217;s rules. Use the <code>&quot;SvTRUE&quot;</code> macro instead, which may call <code>&quot;sv_true()&quot;</code> or may instead use an in-line version. </p>
<pre><code>        I32 sv_true(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvTRUE_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether Perl would evaluate the SV as true or false. See <code>&quot;SvOK&quot;</code> for a defined/undefined test. Does not handle &#8216;get&#8217; magic. </p>
<pre><code>        bool SvTRUE_nomg(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvTYPE</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the type of the SV. See <code>&quot;svtype&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        svtype SvTYPE(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_unmagic</dt>
<dd> <p> Removes all magic of type <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> from an SV. </p>
<pre><code>        int sv_unmagic(SV *const sv, const int type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_unmagicext</dt>
<dd> <p> Removes all magic of type <code>&quot;type&quot;</code> with the specified <code>&quot;vtbl&quot;</code> from an SV. </p>
<pre><code>        int sv_unmagicext(SV *const sv, const int type,
                              MGVTBL *vtbl)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_unref_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Unsets the RV status of the SV, and decrements the reference count of whatever was being referenced by the RV. This can almost be thought of as a reversal of <code>&quot;newSVrv&quot;</code>. The <code>&quot;cflags&quot;</code> argument can contain <code>&quot;SV_IMMEDIATE_UNREF&quot;</code> to force the reference count to be decremented (otherwise the decrementing is conditional on the reference count being different from one or the reference being a readonly SV). See <code>&quot;SvROK_off&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_unref_flags(SV *const ref, const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_untaint</dt>
<dd> <p> Untaint an SV. Use <code>&quot;SvTAINTED_off&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_untaint(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvUOK</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the SV contains an integer that must be interpreted as unsigned. A non-negative integer whose value is within the range of both an IV and a UV may be be flagged as either <code>&quot;SvUOK&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;SVIOK&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool SvUOK(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvUPGRADE</dt>
<dd> <p> Used to upgrade an SV to a more complex form. Uses <code>&quot;sv_upgrade&quot;</code> to perform the upgrade if necessary. See <code>&quot;svtype&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvUPGRADE(SV* sv, svtype type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_upgrade</dt>
<dd> <p> Upgrade an SV to a more complex form. Generally adds a new body type to the SV, then copies across as much information as possible from the old body. It croaks if the SV is already in a more complex form than requested. You generally want to use the <code>&quot;SvUPGRADE&quot;</code> macro wrapper, which checks the type before calling <code>&quot;sv_upgrade&quot;</code>, and hence does not croak. See also <code>&quot;svtype&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_upgrade(SV *const sv, svtype new_type)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_usepvn_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Tells an SV to use <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code> to find its string value. Normally the string is stored inside the SV, but sv_usepvn allows the SV to use an outside string. <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code> should point to memory that was allocated by <code>&quot;Newx&quot;</code>. It must be the start of a <code>&quot;Newx&quot;</code>-ed block of memory, and not a pointer to the middle of it (beware of <code>&quot;OOK&quot;</code> and copy-on-write), and not be from a non-<code>&quot;Newx&quot;</code> memory allocator like <code>&quot;malloc&quot;</code>. The string length, <code>&quot;len&quot;</code>, must be supplied. By default this function will <code>&quot;Renew&quot;</code> (i.e. realloc, move) the memory pointed to by <code>&quot;ptr&quot;</code>, so that pointer should not be freed or used by the programmer after giving it to <code>&quot;sv_usepvn&quot;</code>, and neither should any pointers from ``behind&#8217;&#8216; that pointer (e.g. ptr + 1) be used. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;flags &amp; SV_SMAGIC&quot;</code> is true, will call <code>&quot;SvSETMAGIC&quot;</code>. If <code>&quot;flags &amp; SV_HAS_TRAILING_NUL&quot;</code> is true, then <code>&quot;ptr[len]&quot;</code> must be <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>, and the realloc will be skipped (i.e. the buffer is actually at least 1 byte longer than <code>&quot;len&quot;</code>, and already meets the requirements for storing in <code>&quot;SvPVX&quot;</code>). </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_usepvn_flags(SV *const sv, char* ptr,
                                const STRLEN len,
                                const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvUTF8</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a U32 value indicating the UTF-8 status of an SV. If things are set-up properly, this indicates whether or not the SV contains UTF-8 encoded data. You should use this <i>after</i> a call to <code>&quot;SvPV()&quot;</code> or one of its variants, in case any call to string overloading updates the internal flag. </p>
<p>If you want to take into account the bytes pragma, use <code>&quot;DO_UTF8&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<pre><code>        U32 SvUTF8(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_utf8_decode</dt>
<dd> <p> If the PV of the SV is an octet sequence in Perl&#8217;s extended UTF-8 and contains a multiple-byte character, the <code>&quot;SvUTF8&quot;</code> flag is turned on so that it looks like a character. If the PV contains only single-byte characters, the <code>&quot;SvUTF8&quot;</code> flag stays off. Scans PV for validity and returns FALSE if the PV is invalid UTF-8. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_utf8_decode(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_utf8_downgrade</dt>
<dd> <p> Attempts to convert the PV of an SV from characters to bytes. If the PV contains a character that cannot fit in a byte, this conversion will fail; in this case, either returns false or, if <code>&quot;fail_ok&quot;</code> is not true, croaks. </p>
<p>This is not a general purpose Unicode to byte encoding interface: use the <code>&quot;Encode&quot;</code> extension for that. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_utf8_downgrade(SV *const sv,
                                  const bool fail_ok)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_utf8_encode</dt>
<dd> <p> Converts the PV of an SV to UTF-8, but then turns the <code>&quot;SvUTF8&quot;</code> flag off so that it looks like octets again. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_utf8_encode(SV *const sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_utf8_upgrade</dt>
<dd> <p> Converts the PV of an SV to its UTF-8-encoded form. Forces the SV to string form if it is not already. Will <code>&quot;mg_get&quot;</code> on <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> if appropriate. Always sets the <code>&quot;SvUTF8&quot;</code> flag to avoid future validity checks even if the whole string is the same in UTF-8 as not. Returns the number of bytes in the converted string </p>
<p>This is not a general purpose byte encoding to Unicode interface: use the Encode extension for that. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN sv_utf8_upgrade(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_utf8_upgrade_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Converts the PV of an SV to its UTF-8-encoded form. Forces the SV to string form if it is not already. Always sets the SvUTF8 flag to avoid future validity checks even if all the bytes are invariant in UTF-8. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> has <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, will <code>&quot;mg_get&quot;</code> on <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> if appropriate, else not. </p>
<p>The <code>&quot;SV_FORCE_UTF8_UPGRADE&quot;</code> flag is now ignored. </p>
<p>Returns the number of bytes in the converted string. </p>
<p>This is not a general purpose byte encoding to Unicode interface: use the Encode extension for that. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN sv_utf8_upgrade_flags(SV *const sv,
                                      const I32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_utf8_upgrade_flags_grow</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_utf8_upgrade_flags&quot;</code>, but has an additional parameter <code>&quot;extra&quot;</code>, which is the number of unused bytes the string of <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is guaranteed to have free after it upon return. This allows the caller to reserve extra space that it intends to fill, to avoid extra grows. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;sv_utf8_upgrade&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;sv_utf8_upgrade_nomg&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;sv_utf8_upgrade_flags&quot;</code> are implemented in terms of this function. </p>
<p>Returns the number of bytes in the converted string (not including the spares). </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN sv_utf8_upgrade_flags_grow(SV *const sv,
                                           const I32 flags,
                                           STRLEN extra)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_utf8_upgrade_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_utf8_upgrade&quot;</code>, but doesn&#8217;t do magic on <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN sv_utf8_upgrade_nomg(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvUTF8_off</dt>
<dd> <p> Unsets the UTF-8 status of an SV (the data is not changed, just the flag). Do not use frivolously. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvUTF8_off(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvUTF8_on</dt>
<dd> <p> Turn on the UTF-8 status of an SV (the data is not changed, just the flag). Do not use frivolously. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvUTF8_on(SV *sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvUV</dt>
<dd> <p> Coerces the given SV to UV and returns it. The returned value in many circumstances will get stored in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>&#8216;s UV slot, but not in all cases. (Use <code>&quot;sv_setuv&quot;</code> to make sure it does). </p>
<p>See <code>&quot;SvUVx&quot;</code> for a version which guarantees to evaluate <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> only once. </p>
<pre><code>        UV SvUV(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvUV_nomg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;SvUV&quot;</code> but doesn&#8217;t process magic. </p>
<pre><code>        UV SvUV_nomg(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvUV_set</dt>
<dd> <p> Set the value of the UV pointer in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> to val. See <code>&quot;SvIV_set&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void SvUV_set(SV* sv, UV val)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvUVX</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the raw value in the SV&#8216;s UV slot, without checks or conversions. Only use when you are sure <code>&quot;SvIOK&quot;</code> is true. See also <code>&quot;SvUV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        UV SvUVX(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvUVx</dt>
<dd> <p> Coerces the given SV to UV and returns it. The returned value in many circumstances will get stored in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>&#8216;s UV slot, but not in all cases. (Use <code>&quot;sv_setuv&quot;</code> to make sure it does). </p>
<p>This form guarantees to evaluate <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> only once. Only use this if <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is an expression with side effects, otherwise use the more efficient <code>&quot;SvUV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        UV SvUVx(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_vcatpvf</dt>
<dd> <p> Processes its arguments like <code>&quot;sv_vcatpvfn&quot;</code> called with a non-null C-style variable argument list, and appends the formatted output to an SV. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See <code>&quot;sv_vcatpvf_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Usually used via its frontend <code>&quot;sv_catpvf&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_vcatpvf(SV *const sv, const char *const pat,
                           va_list *const args)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_vcatpvfn</dt>
<dd> <pre><code>        void sv_vcatpvfn(SV *const sv, const char *const pat,
                            const STRLEN patlen,
                            va_list *const args,
                            SV **const svargs,
                            const Size_t sv_count,
                            bool *const maybe_tainted)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_vcatpvfn_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Processes its arguments like <code>&quot;vsprintf&quot;</code> and appends the formatted output to an SV. Uses an array of SVs if the C-style variable argument list is missing (<code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>). Argument reordering (using format specifiers like <code>&quot;%2$d&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;%*2$d&quot;</code>) is supported only when using an array of SVs; using a C-style <code>&quot;va_list&quot;</code> argument list with a format string that uses argument reordering will yield an exception. </p>
<p>When running with taint checks enabled, indicates via <code>&quot;maybe_tainted&quot;</code> if results are untrustworthy (often due to the use of locales). </p>
<p>If called as <code>&quot;sv_vcatpvfn&quot;</code> or flags has the <code>&quot;SV_GMAGIC&quot;</code> bit set, calls get magic. </p>
<p>It assumes that pat has the same utf8-ness as sv. It&#8217;s the caller&#8217;s responsibility to ensure that this is so. </p>
<p>Usually used via one of its frontends <code>&quot;sv_vcatpvf&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;sv_vcatpvf_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_vcatpvfn_flags(SV *const sv,
                                  const char *const pat,
                                  const STRLEN patlen,
                                  va_list *const args,
                                  SV **const svargs,
                                  const Size_t sv_count,
                                  bool *const maybe_tainted,
                                  const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_vcatpvf_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_vcatpvf&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<p>Usually used via its frontend <code>&quot;sv_catpvf_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_vcatpvf_mg(SV *const sv,
                              const char *const pat,
                              va_list *const args)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>SvVOK</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean indicating whether the SV contains a v-string. </p>
<pre><code>        bool SvVOK(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_vsetpvf</dt>
<dd> <p> Works like <code>&quot;sv_vcatpvf&quot;</code> but copies the text into the SV instead of appending it. Does not handle &#8216;set&#8217; magic. See <code>&quot;sv_vsetpvf_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Usually used via its frontend <code>&quot;sv_setpvf&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_vsetpvf(SV *const sv, const char *const pat,
                           va_list *const args)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_vsetpvfn</dt>
<dd> <p> Works like <code>&quot;sv_vcatpvfn&quot;</code> but copies the text into the SV instead of appending it. </p>
<p>Usually used via one of its frontends <code>&quot;sv_vsetpvf&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;sv_vsetpvf_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_vsetpvfn(SV *const sv, const char *const pat,
                            const STRLEN patlen,
                            va_list *const args,
                            SV **const svargs,
                            const Size_t sv_count,
                            bool *const maybe_tainted)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_vsetpvf_mg</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;sv_vsetpvf&quot;</code>, but also handles &#8216;set&#8217; magic. </p>
<p>Usually used via its frontend <code>&quot;sv_setpvf_mg&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void sv_vsetpvf_mg(SV *const sv,
                              const char *const pat,
                              va_list *const args)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Unicode Support</h2>
<p> ``Unicode Support&#8217;&#8216; in perlguts has an introduction to this API. </p>
<p>See also ``Character classification&#8217;&#8216;, and ``Character case changing&#8217;&#8216;. Various functions outside this section also work specially with Unicode. Search for the string ``utf8&#8217;&#8216; in this document. </p>
<dl>
<dt>BOM_UTF8</dt>
<dd> <p> This is a macro that evaluates to a string constant of the UTF-8 bytes that define the Unicode BYTE ORDER MARK (U+FEFF) for the platform that perl is compiled on. This allows code to use a mnemonic for this character that works on both ASCII and EBCDIC platforms. <code>&quot;sizeof(BOM_UTF8) - 1&quot;</code> can be used to get its length in bytes. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>bytes_cmp_utf8</dt>
<dd> <p> Compares the sequence of characters (stored as octets) in <code>&quot;b&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;blen&quot;</code> with the sequence of characters (stored as UTF-8) in <code>&quot;u&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;ulen&quot;</code>. Returns 0 if they are equal, -1 or -2 if the first string is less than the second string, +1 or +2 if the first string is greater than the second string. </p>
<p>-1 or +1 is returned if the shorter string was identical to the start of the longer string. -2 or +2 is returned if there was a difference between characters within the strings. </p>
<pre><code>        int bytes_cmp_utf8(const U8 *b, STRLEN blen,
                               const U8 *u, STRLEN ulen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>bytes_from_utf8</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Converts a potentially UTF-8 encoded string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> of length <code>*lenp</code> into native byte encoding. On input, the boolean <code>*is_utf8p</code> gives whether or not <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> is actually encoded in UTF-8. </p>
<p>Unlike ``utf8_to_bytes&#8217;&#8216; but like ``bytes_to_utf8&#8217;&#8216;, this is non-destructive of the input string. </p>
<p>Do nothing if <code>*is_utf8p</code> is 0, or if there are code points in the string not expressible in native byte encoding. In these cases, <code>*is_utf8p</code> and <code>*lenp</code> are unchanged, and the return value is the original <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Otherwise, <code>*is_utf8p</code> is set to 0, and the return value is a pointer to a newly created string containing a downgraded copy of <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>, and whose length is returned in <code>*lenp</code>, updated. The new string is <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>-terminated. The caller is responsible for arranging for the memory used by this string to get freed. </p>
<p>Upon successful return, the number of variants in the string can be computed by having saved the value of <code>*lenp</code> before the call, and subtracting the after-call value of <code>*lenp</code> from it. </p>
<pre><code>        U8* bytes_from_utf8(const U8 *s, STRLEN *lenp,
                                bool *is_utf8p)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>bytes_to_utf8</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Converts a string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> of length <code>*lenp</code> bytes from the native encoding into UTF-8. Returns a pointer to the newly-created string, and sets <code>*lenp</code> to reflect the new length in bytes. The caller is responsible for arranging for the memory used by this string to get freed. </p>
<p>Upon successful return, the number of variants in the string can be computed by having saved the value of <code>*lenp</code> before the call, and subtracting it from the after-call value of <code>*lenp</code>. </p>
<p>A <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> character will be written after the end of the string. </p>
<p>If you want to convert to UTF-8 from encodings other than the native (Latin1 or EBCDIC), see ``sv_recode_to_utf8&#8217;&#8216;(). </p>
<pre><code>        U8* bytes_to_utf8(const U8 *s, STRLEN *lenp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>DO_UTF8</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a bool giving whether or not the PV in <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is to be treated as being encoded in UTF-8. </p>
<p>You should use this <i>after</i> a call to <code>&quot;SvPV()&quot;</code> or one of its variants, in case any call to string overloading updates the internal UTF-8 encoding flag. </p>
<pre><code>        bool DO_UTF8(SV* sv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>foldEQ_utf8</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns true if the leading portions of the strings <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;s2&quot;</code> (either or both of which may be in UTF-8) are the same case-insensitively; false otherwise. How far into the strings to compare is determined by other input parameters. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;u1&quot;</code> is true, the string <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code> is assumed to be in UTF-8-encoded Unicode; otherwise it is assumed to be in native 8-bit encoding. Correspondingly for <code>&quot;u2&quot;</code> with respect to <code>&quot;s2&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>If the byte length <code>&quot;l1&quot;</code> is non-zero, it says how far into <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code> to check for fold equality. In other words, <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code>+<code>&quot;l1&quot;</code> will be used as a goal to reach. The scan will not be considered to be a match unless the goal is reached, and scanning won&#8217;t continue past that goal. Correspondingly for <code>&quot;l2&quot;</code> with respect to <code>&quot;s2&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;pe1&quot;</code> is non-<code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code> and the pointer it points to is not <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>, that pointer is considered an end pointer to the position 1 byte past the maximum point in <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code> beyond which scanning will not continue under any circumstances. (This routine assumes that UTF-8 encoded input strings are not malformed; malformed input can cause it to read past <code>&quot;pe1&quot;</code>). This means that if both <code>&quot;l1&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;pe1&quot;</code> are specified, and <code>&quot;pe1&quot;</code> is less than <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code>+<code>&quot;l1&quot;</code>, the match will never be successful because it can never get as far as its goal (and in fact is asserted against). Correspondingly for <code>&quot;pe2&quot;</code> with respect to <code>&quot;s2&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>At least one of <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;s2&quot;</code> must have a goal (at least one of <code>&quot;l1&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;l2&quot;</code> must be non-zero), and if both do, both have to be reached for a successful match. Also, if the fold of a character is multiple characters, all of them must be matched (see tr21 reference below for &#8216;folding&#8217;). </p>
<p>Upon a successful match, if <code>&quot;pe1&quot;</code> is non-<code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>, it will be set to point to the beginning of the <i>next</i> character of <code>&quot;s1&quot;</code> beyond what was matched. Correspondingly for <code>&quot;pe2&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;s2&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>For case-insensitiveness, the ``casefolding&#8217;&#8216; of Unicode is used instead of upper/lowercasing both the characters, see &lt;<a rel="nofollow" href="http://www.unicode.org/unicode/reports/tr21/">http://www.unicode.org/unicode/reports/tr21/</a>&gt; (Case Mappings). </p>
<pre><code>        I32 foldEQ_utf8(const char *s1, char **pe1, UV l1,
                            bool u1, const char *s2, char **pe2,
                            UV l2, bool u2)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_ascii_string</dt>
<dd> <p> This is a misleadingly-named synonym for ``is_utf8_invariant_string&#8217;&#8216;. On ASCII-ish platforms, the name isn&#8217;t misleading: the ASCII-range characters are exactly the UTF-8 invariants. But EBCDIC machines have more invariants than just the ASCII characters, so <code>&quot;is_utf8_invariant_string&quot;</code> is preferred. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_ascii_string(const U8* const s, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_c9strict_utf8_string</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns TRUE if the first <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> bytes of string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> form a valid UTF-8-encoded string that conforms to Unicode Corrigendum #9 &lt;<a rel="nofollow" href="http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html">http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html</a>&gt;; otherwise it returns FALSE. If <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> is 0, it will be calculated using <code>strlen(s)</code> (which means if you use this option, that <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> can&#8217;t have embedded <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters and has to have a terminating <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> byte). Note that all characters being ASCII constitute &#8216;a valid UTF-8 string&#8217;. </p>
<p>This function returns FALSE for strings containing any code points above the Unicode max of 0x10FFFF or surrogate code points, but accepts non-character code points per Corrigendum #9 &lt;<a rel="nofollow" href="http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html">http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html</a>&gt;. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_utf8_invariant_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_invariant_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loc_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loclen_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loc_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loclen_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_c9strict_utf8_string(const U8 *s, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_c9strict_utf8_string_loc</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string&quot;</code> but stores the location of the failure (in the case of ``utf8ness failure&#8217;&#8216;) or the location <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>+<code>&quot;len&quot;</code> (in the case of ``utf8ness success&#8217;&#8216;) in the <code>&quot;ep&quot;</code> pointer. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_c9strict_utf8_string_loc(const U8 *s,
                                            STRLEN len,
                                            const U8 **ep)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_c9strict_utf8_string_loclen</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string&quot;</code> but stores the location of the failure (in the case of ``utf8ness failure&#8217;&#8216;) or the location <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>+<code>&quot;len&quot;</code> (in the case of ``utf8ness success&#8217;&#8216;) in the <code>&quot;ep&quot;</code> pointer, and the number of UTF-8 encoded characters in the <code>&quot;el&quot;</code> pointer. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_c9strict_utf8_string_loclen(const U8 *s,
                                               STRLEN len,
                                               const U8 **ep,
                                               STRLEN *el)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>isC9_STRICT_UTF8_CHAR</dt>
<dd> <p> Evaluates to non-zero if the first few bytes of the string starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> and looking no further than <code>&quot;e - 1&quot;</code> are well-formed UTF-8 that represents some Unicode non-surrogate code point; otherwise it evaluates to 0. If non-zero, the value gives how many bytes starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> comprise the code point&#8217;s representation. Any bytes remaining before <code>&quot;e&quot;</code>, but beyond the ones needed to form the first code point in <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>, are not examined. </p>
<p>The largest acceptable code point is the Unicode maximum 0x10FFFF. This differs from <code>&quot;isSTRICT_UTF8_CHAR&quot;</code> only in that it accepts non-character code points. This corresponds to Unicode Corrigendum #9 &lt;<a rel="nofollow" href="http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html">http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html</a>&gt;. which said that non-character code points are merely discouraged rather than completely forbidden in open interchange. See ``Noncharacter code points&#8217;&#8216; in perlunicode. </p>
<p>Use <code>&quot;isUTF8_CHAR&quot;</code> to check for Perl&#8217;s extended UTF-8; and <code>&quot;isUTF8_CHAR_flags&quot;</code> for a more customized definition. </p>
<p>Use <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code> to check entire strings. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN isC9_STRICT_UTF8_CHAR(const U8 *s, const U8 *e)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_invariant_string</dt>
<dd> <p> This is a somewhat misleadingly-named synonym for ``is_utf8_invariant_string&#8217;&#8216;. <code>&quot;is_utf8_invariant_string&quot;</code> is preferred, as it indicates under what conditions the string is invariant. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_invariant_string(const U8* const s,
                                    STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>isSTRICT_UTF8_CHAR</dt>
<dd> <p> Evaluates to non-zero if the first few bytes of the string starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> and looking no further than <code>&quot;e - 1&quot;</code> are well-formed UTF-8 that represents some Unicode code point completely acceptable for open interchange between all applications; otherwise it evaluates to 0. If non-zero, the value gives how many bytes starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> comprise the code point&#8217;s representation. Any bytes remaining before <code>&quot;e&quot;</code>, but beyond the ones needed to form the first code point in <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>, are not examined. </p>
<p>The largest acceptable code point is the Unicode maximum 0x10FFFF, and must not be a surrogate nor a non-character code point. Thus this excludes any code point from Perl&#8217;s extended UTF-8. </p>
<p>This is used to efficiently decide if the next few bytes in <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> is legal Unicode-acceptable UTF-8 for a single character. </p>
<p>Use <code>&quot;isC9_STRICT_UTF8_CHAR&quot;</code> to use the Unicode Corrigendum #9 &lt;<a rel="nofollow" href="http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html">http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html</a>&gt; definition of allowable code points; <code>&quot;isUTF8_CHAR&quot;</code> to check for Perl&#8217;s extended UTF-8; and <code>&quot;isUTF8_CHAR_flags&quot;</code> for a more customized definition. </p>
<p>Use <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code> to check entire strings. </p>
<pre><code>        Size_t isSTRICT_UTF8_CHAR(const U8 * const s0,
                                   const U8 * const e)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_strict_utf8_string</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns TRUE if the first <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> bytes of string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> form a valid UTF-8-encoded string that is fully interchangeable by any application using Unicode rules; otherwise it returns FALSE. If <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> is 0, it will be calculated using <code>strlen(s)</code> (which means if you use this option, that <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> can&#8217;t have embedded <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters and has to have a terminating <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> byte).
