Note that all characters being ASCII constitute &#8216;a valid UTF-8 string&#8217;. </p>
<p>This function returns FALSE for strings containing any code points above the Unicode max of 0x10FFFF, surrogate code points, or non-character code points. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_utf8_invariant_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_invariant_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loc_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loclen_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loc_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loclen_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_strict_utf8_string(const U8 *s, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_strict_utf8_string_loc</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string&quot;</code> but stores the location of the failure (in the case of ``utf8ness failure&#8217;&#8216;) or the location <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>+<code>&quot;len&quot;</code> (in the case of ``utf8ness success&#8217;&#8216;) in the <code>&quot;ep&quot;</code> pointer. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_strict_utf8_string_loc(const U8 *s,
                                          STRLEN len,
                                          const U8 **ep)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_strict_utf8_string_loclen</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string&quot;</code> but stores the location of the failure (in the case of ``utf8ness failure&#8217;&#8216;) or the location <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>+<code>&quot;len&quot;</code> (in the case of ``utf8ness success&#8217;&#8216;) in the <code>&quot;ep&quot;</code> pointer, and the number of UTF-8 encoded characters in the <code>&quot;el&quot;</code> pointer. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_strict_utf8_string_loclen(const U8 *s,
                                             STRLEN len,
                                             const U8 **ep,
                                             STRLEN *el)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns TRUE if the fixed-width buffer starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> with length <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> is entirely valid UTF-8, subject to the restrictions given by <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code>; otherwise it returns FALSE. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is 0, any well-formed UTF-8, as extended by Perl, is accepted without restriction. If the final few bytes of the buffer do not form a complete code point, this will return TRUE anyway, provided that <code>&quot;is_utf8_valid_partial_char_flags&quot;</code> returns TRUE for them. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> in non-zero, it can be any combination of the <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_</code>foo <code>&quot;</code> flags accepted by <code>&quot;utf8n_to_uvchr&quot;</code>, and with the same meanings. </p>
<p>This function differs from <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_flags&quot;</code> only in that the latter returns FALSE if the final few bytes of the string don&#8217;t form a complete code point. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_flags(
                    const U8 * const s, STRLEN len,
                    const U32 flags
                )
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loclen_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loc_flags&quot;</code> but stores the number of complete, valid characters found in the <code>&quot;el&quot;</code> pointer. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loclen_flags(
                    const U8 * const s, STRLEN len,
                    const U8 **ep, STRLEN *el, const U32 flags
                )
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loc_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_flags&quot;</code> but stores the location of the failure in the <code>&quot;ep&quot;</code> pointer. If the function returns TRUE, <code>*ep</code> will point to the beginning of any partial character at the end of the buffer; if there is no partial character <code>*ep</code> will contain <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>+<code>&quot;len&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loclen_flags&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loc_flags(
                    const U8 * const s, STRLEN len,
                    const U8 **ep, const U32 flags
                )
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_invariant_string</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns TRUE if the first <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> bytes of the string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> are the same regardless of the UTF-8 encoding of the string (or UTF-EBCDIC encoding on EBCDIC machines); otherwise it returns FALSE. That is, it returns TRUE if they are UTF-8 invariant. On ASCII-ish machines, all the ASCII characters and only the ASCII characters fit this definition. On EBCDIC machines, the ASCII-range characters are invariant, but so also are the C1 controls. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> is 0, it will be calculated using <code>strlen(s)</code>, (which means if you use this option, that <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> can&#8217;t have embedded <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters and has to have a terminating <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> byte). </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loc_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loclen_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loc_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loclen_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_invariant_string(const U8* const s,
                                         STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_invariant_string_loc</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;is_utf8_invariant_string&quot;</code> but upon failure, stores the location of the first UTF-8 variant character in the <code>&quot;ep&quot;</code> pointer; if all characters are UTF-8 invariant, this function does not change the contents of <code>*ep</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_invariant_string_loc(const U8* const s,
                                             STRLEN len,
                                             const U8 ** ep)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_string</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns TRUE if the first <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> bytes of string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> form a valid Perl-extended-UTF-8 string; returns FALSE otherwise. If <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> is 0, it will be calculated using <code>strlen(s)</code> (which means if you use this option, that <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> can&#8217;t have embedded <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters and has to have a terminating <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> byte). Note that all characters being ASCII constitute &#8216;a valid UTF-8 string&#8217;. </p>
<p>This function considers Perl&#8217;s extended UTF-8 to be valid. That means that code points above Unicode, surrogates, and non-character code points are considered valid by this function. Use <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string&quot;</code>, or <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_flags&quot;</code> to restrict what code points are considered valid. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_utf8_invariant_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_invariant_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loc_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loclen_flags&quot;</code>, </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_string(const U8 *s, STRLEN len)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_string_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns TRUE if the first <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> bytes of string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> form a valid UTF-8 string, subject to the restrictions imposed by <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code>; returns FALSE otherwise. If <code>&quot;len&quot;</code> is 0, it will be calculated using <code>strlen(s)</code> (which means if you use this option, that <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> can&#8217;t have embedded <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> characters and has to have a terminating <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> byte). Note that all characters being ASCII constitute &#8216;a valid UTF-8 string&#8217;. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is 0, this gives the same results as <code>&quot;is_utf8_string&quot;</code>; if <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_ILLEGAL_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code>, this gives the same results as <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string&quot;</code>; and if <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_ILLEGAL_C9_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code>, this gives the same results as <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string&quot;</code>. Otherwise <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> may be any combination of the <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_</code>foo <code>&quot;</code> flags understood by <code>&quot;utf8n_to_uvchr&quot;</code>, with the same meanings. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_utf8_invariant_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_invariant_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loc_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loclen_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loc_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_fixed_width_buf_loclen_flags&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_strict_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;is_c9strict_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_string_flags(const U8 *s, STRLEN len,
                                     const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_string_loc</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;is_utf8_string&quot;</code> but stores the location of the failure (in the case of ``utf8ness failure&#8217;&#8216;) or the location <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>+<code>&quot;len&quot;</code> (in the case of ``utf8ness success&#8217;&#8216;) in the <code>&quot;ep&quot;</code> pointer. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_string_loc(const U8 *s,
                                   const STRLEN len,
                                   const U8 **ep)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_string_loclen</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;is_utf8_string&quot;</code> but stores the location of the failure (in the case of ``utf8ness failure&#8217;&#8216;) or the location <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>+<code>&quot;len&quot;</code> (in the case of ``utf8ness success&#8217;&#8216;) in the <code>&quot;ep&quot;</code> pointer, and the number of UTF-8 encoded characters in the <code>&quot;el&quot;</code> pointer. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_string_loclen(const U8 *s, STRLEN len,
                                      const U8 **ep, STRLEN *el)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_string_loclen_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_flags&quot;</code> but stores the location of the failure (in the case of ``utf8ness failure&#8217;&#8216;) or the location <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>+<code>&quot;len&quot;</code> (in the case of ``utf8ness success&#8217;&#8216;) in the <code>&quot;ep&quot;</code> pointer, and the number of UTF-8 encoded characters in the <code>&quot;el&quot;</code> pointer. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loc_flags&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_string_loclen_flags(const U8 *s,
                                            STRLEN len,
                                            const U8 **ep,
                                            STRLEN *el,
                                            const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_string_loc_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_flags&quot;</code> but stores the location of the failure (in the case of ``utf8ness failure&#8217;&#8216;) or the location <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>+<code>&quot;len&quot;</code> (in the case of ``utf8ness success&#8217;&#8216;) in the <code>&quot;ep&quot;</code> pointer. </p>
<p>See also <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loclen_flags&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_string_loc_flags(const U8 *s,
                                         STRLEN len,
                                         const U8 **ep,
                                         const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_valid_partial_char</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns 0 if the sequence of bytes starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> and looking no further than <code>&quot;e - 1&quot;</code> is the UTF-8 encoding, as extended by Perl, for one or more code points. Otherwise, it returns 1 if there exists at least one non-empty sequence of bytes that when appended to sequence <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>, starting at position <code>&quot;e&quot;</code> causes the entire sequence to be the well-formed UTF-8 of some code point; otherwise returns 0. </p>
<p>In other words this returns TRUE if <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> points to a partial UTF-8-encoded code point. </p>
<p>This is useful when a fixed-length buffer is being tested for being well-formed UTF-8, but the final few bytes in it don&#8217;t comprise a full character; that is, it is split somewhere in the middle of the final code point&#8217;s UTF-8 representation. (Presumably when the buffer is refreshed with the next chunk of data, the new first bytes will complete the partial code point.) This function is used to verify that the final bytes in the current buffer are in fact the legal beginning of some code point, so that if they aren&#8217;t, the failure can be signalled without having to wait for the next read. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_valid_partial_char(const U8 * const s,
                                           const U8 * const e)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>is_utf8_valid_partial_char_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;is_utf8_valid_partial_char&quot;</code>, it returns a boolean giving whether or not the input is a valid UTF-8 encoded partial character, but it takes an extra parameter, <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code>, which can further restrict which code points are considered valid. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is 0, this behaves identically to <code>&quot;is_utf8_valid_partial_char&quot;</code>. Otherwise <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> can be any combination of the <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_</code>foo <code>&quot;</code> flags accepted by <code>&quot;utf8n_to_uvchr&quot;</code>. If there is any sequence of bytes that can complete the input partial character in such a way that a non-prohibited character is formed, the function returns TRUE; otherwise FALSE. Non character code points cannot be determined based on partial character input. But many of the other possible excluded types can be determined from just the first one or two bytes. </p>
<pre><code>        bool is_utf8_valid_partial_char_flags(
                    const U8 * const s, const U8 * const e,
                    const U32 flags
                )
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>isUTF8_CHAR</dt>
<dd> <p> Evaluates to non-zero if the first few bytes of the string starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> and looking no further than <code>&quot;e - 1&quot;</code> are well-formed UTF-8, as extended by Perl, that represents some code point; otherwise it evaluates to 0. If non-zero, the value gives how many bytes starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> comprise the code point&#8217;s representation. Any bytes remaining before <code>&quot;e&quot;</code>, but beyond the ones needed to form the first code point in <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>, are not examined. </p>
<p>The code point can be any that will fit in an IV on this machine, using Perl&#8217;s extension to official UTF-8 to represent those higher than the Unicode maximum of 0x10FFFF. That means that this macro is used to efficiently decide if the next few bytes in <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> is legal UTF-8 for a single character. </p>
<p>Use <code>&quot;isSTRICT_UTF8_CHAR&quot;</code> to restrict the acceptable code points to those defined by Unicode to be fully interchangeable across applications; <code>&quot;isC9_STRICT_UTF8_CHAR&quot;</code> to use the Unicode Corrigendum #9 &lt;<a rel="nofollow" href="http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html">http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html</a>&gt; definition of allowable code points; and <code>&quot;isUTF8_CHAR_flags&quot;</code> for a more customized definition. </p>
<p>Use <code>&quot;is_utf8_string&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loc&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;is_utf8_string_loclen&quot;</code> to check entire strings. </p>
<p>Note also that a UTF-8 ``invariant&#8217;&#8216; character (i.e. ASCII on non-EBCDIC machines) is a valid UTF-8 character. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN isUTF8_CHAR(const U8 *s, const U8 *e)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>isUTF8_CHAR_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Evaluates to non-zero if the first few bytes of the string starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> and looking no further than <code>&quot;e - 1&quot;</code> are well-formed UTF-8, as extended by Perl, that represents some code point, subject to the restrictions given by <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code>; otherwise it evaluates to 0. If non-zero, the value gives how many bytes starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> comprise the code point&#8217;s representation. Any bytes remaining before <code>&quot;e&quot;</code>, but beyond the ones needed to form the first code point in <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>, are not examined. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is 0, this gives the same results as <code>&quot;isUTF8_CHAR&quot;</code>; if <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_ILLEGAL_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code>, this gives the same results as <code>&quot;isSTRICT_UTF8_CHAR&quot;</code>; and if <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_ILLEGAL_C9_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code>, this gives the same results as <code>&quot;isC9_STRICT_UTF8_CHAR&quot;</code>. Otherwise <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> may be any combination of the <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_</code>foo <code>&quot;</code> flags understood by <code>&quot;utf8n_to_uvchr&quot;</code>, with the same meanings. </p>
<p>The three alternative macros are for the most commonly needed validations; they are likely to run somewhat faster than this more general one, as they can be inlined into your code. </p>
<p>Use ``is_utf8_string_flags&#8217;&#8216;, ``is_utf8_string_loc_flags&#8217;&#8216;, and ``is_utf8_string_loclen_flags&#8217;&#8216; to check entire strings. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN isUTF8_CHAR_flags(const U8 *s, const U8 *e,
                                   const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>pv_uni_display</dt>
<dd> <p> Build to the scalar <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code> a displayable version of the string <code>&quot;spv&quot;</code>, length <code>&quot;len&quot;</code>, the displayable version being at most <code>&quot;pvlim&quot;</code> bytes long (if longer, the rest is truncated and <code>&quot;...&quot;</code> will be appended). </p>
<p>The <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> argument can have <code>&quot;UNI_DISPLAY_ISPRINT&quot;</code> set to display <code>&quot;isPRINT()&quot;</code>able characters as themselves, <code>&quot;UNI_DISPLAY_BACKSLASH&quot;</code> to display the <code>&quot;\\[nrfta\\]&quot;</code> as the backslashed versions (like <code>&quot;\n&quot;</code>) (<code>&quot;UNI_DISPLAY_BACKSLASH&quot;</code> is preferred over <code>&quot;UNI_DISPLAY_ISPRINT&quot;</code> for <code>&quot;\\&quot;</code>). <code>&quot;UNI_DISPLAY_QQ&quot;</code> (and its alias <code>&quot;UNI_DISPLAY_REGEX&quot;</code>) have both <code>&quot;UNI_DISPLAY_BACKSLASH&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;UNI_DISPLAY_ISPRINT&quot;</code> turned on. </p>
<p>The pointer to the PV of the <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code> is returned. </p>
<p>See also ``sv_uni_display&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        char* pv_uni_display(SV *dsv, const U8 *spv,
                               STRLEN len, STRLEN pvlim,
                               UV flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>REPLACEMENT_CHARACTER_UTF8</dt>
<dd> <p> This is a macro that evaluates to a string constant of the UTF-8 bytes that define the Unicode REPLACEMENT CHARACTER (U+FFFD) for the platform that perl is compiled on. This allows code to use a mnemonic for this character that works on both ASCII and EBCDIC platforms. <code>&quot;sizeof(REPLACEMENT_CHARACTER_UTF8) - 1&quot;</code> can be used to get its length in bytes. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_cat_decode</dt>
<dd> <p> <code>&quot;encoding&quot;</code> is assumed to be an <code>&quot;Encode&quot;</code> object, the PV of <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code> is assumed to be octets in that encoding and decoding the input starts from the position which <code>&quot;(PV + *offset)&quot;</code> pointed to. <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code> will be concatenated with the decoded UTF-8 string from <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code>. Decoding will terminate when the string <code>&quot;tstr&quot;</code> appears in decoding output or the input ends on the PV of <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code>. The value which <code>&quot;offset&quot;</code> points will be modified to the last input position on <code>&quot;ssv&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Returns TRUE if the terminator was found, else returns FALSE. </p>
<pre><code>        bool sv_cat_decode(SV* dsv, SV *encoding, SV *ssv,
                              int *offset, char* tstr, int tlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_recode_to_utf8</dt>
<dd> <p> <code>&quot;encoding&quot;</code> is assumed to be an <code>&quot;Encode&quot;</code> object, on entry the PV of <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is assumed to be octets in that encoding, and <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> will be converted into Unicode (and UTF-8). </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> already is UTF-8 (or if it is not <code>&quot;POK&quot;</code>), or if <code>&quot;encoding&quot;</code> is not a reference, nothing is done to <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>. If <code>&quot;encoding&quot;</code> is not an <code>&quot;Encode::XS&quot;</code> Encoding object, bad things will happen. (See <i>cpan/Encode/encoding.pm</i> and Encode.) </p>
<p>The PV of <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code> is returned. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_recode_to_utf8(SV* sv, SV *encoding)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>sv_uni_display</dt>
<dd> <p> Build to the scalar <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code> a displayable version of the scalar <code>&quot;sv&quot;</code>, the displayable version being at most <code>&quot;pvlim&quot;</code> bytes long (if longer, the rest is truncated and ``...&#8216;&#8216; will be appended). </p>
<p>The <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> argument is as in ``pv_uni_display&#8217;&#8216;(). </p>
<p>The pointer to the PV of the <code>&quot;dsv&quot;</code> is returned. </p>
<pre><code>        char* sv_uni_display(SV *dsv, SV *ssv, STRLEN pvlim,
                               UV flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>to_utf8_fold</dt>
<dd> <p> DEPRECATED! It is planned to remove this function from a future release of Perl. Do not use it for new code; remove it from existing code. </p>
<p>Instead use ``toFOLD_utf8_safe&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        UV to_utf8_fold(const U8 *p, U8* ustrp,
                             STRLEN *lenp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>to_utf8_lower</dt>
<dd> <p> DEPRECATED! It is planned to remove this function from a future release of Perl. Do not use it for new code; remove it from existing code. </p>
<p>Instead use ``toLOWER_utf8_safe&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        UV to_utf8_lower(const U8 *p, U8* ustrp,
                              STRLEN *lenp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>to_utf8_title</dt>
<dd> <p> DEPRECATED! It is planned to remove this function from a future release of Perl. Do not use it for new code; remove it from existing code. </p>
<p>Instead use ``toTITLE_utf8_safe&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        UV to_utf8_title(const U8 *p, U8* ustrp,
                              STRLEN *lenp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>to_utf8_upper</dt>
<dd> <p> DEPRECATED! It is planned to remove this function from a future release of Perl. Do not use it for new code; remove it from existing code. </p>
<p>Instead use ``toUPPER_utf8_safe&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        UV to_utf8_upper(const U8 *p, U8* ustrp,
                              STRLEN *lenp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8n_to_uvchr</dt>
<dd> <p> THIS FUNCTION SHOULD BE USED IN ONLY VERY SPECIALIZED CIRCUMSTANCES. Most code should use ``utf8_to_uvchr_buf&#8217;&#8216;() rather than call this directly. </p>
<p>Bottom level UTF-8 decode routine. Returns the native code point value of the first character in the string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>, which is assumed to be in UTF-8 (or UTF-EBCDIC) encoding, and no longer than <code>&quot;curlen&quot;</code> bytes; <code>*retlen</code> (if <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> isn&#8217;t NULL) will be set to the length, in bytes, of that character. </p>
<p>The value of <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> determines the behavior when <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> does not point to a well-formed UTF-8 character. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is 0, encountering a malformation causes zero to be returned and <code>*retlen</code> is set so that (<code>&quot;s&quot;</code> + <code>*retlen</code>) is the next possible position in <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> that could begin a non-malformed character. Also, if UTF-8 warnings haven&#8217;t been lexically disabled, a warning is raised. Some UTF-8 input sequences may contain multiple malformations. This function tries to find every possible one in each call, so multiple warnings can be raised for the same sequence. </p>
<p>Various ALLOW flags can be set in <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> to allow (and not warn on) individual types of malformations, such as the sequence being overlong (that is, when there is a shorter sequence that can express the same code point; overlong sequences are expressly forbidden in the UTF-8 standard due to potential security issues). Another malformation example is the first byte of a character not being a legal first byte. See <i>utf8.h</i> for the list of such flags. Even if allowed, this function generally returns the Unicode REPLACEMENT CHARACTER when it encounters a malformation. There are flags in <i>utf8.h</i> to override this behavior for the overlong malformations, but don&#8217;t do that except for very specialized purposes. </p>
<p>The <code>&quot;UTF8_CHECK_ONLY&quot;</code> flag overrides the behavior when a non-allowed (by other flags) malformation is found. If this flag is set, the routine assumes that the caller will raise a warning, and this function will silently just set <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> to <code>&quot;-1&quot;</code> (cast to <code>&quot;STRLEN&quot;</code>) and return zero. </p>
<p>Note that this API requires disambiguation between successful decoding a <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code> character, and an error return (unless the <code>&quot;UTF8_CHECK_ONLY&quot;</code> flag is set), as in both cases, 0 is returned, and, depending on the malformation, <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> may be set to 1. To disambiguate, upon a zero return, see if the first byte of <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> is 0 as well. If so, the input was a <code>&quot;NUL&quot;</code>; if not, the input had an error. Or you can use <code>&quot;utf8n_to_uvchr_error&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Certain code points are considered problematic. These are Unicode surrogates, Unicode non-characters, and code points above the Unicode maximum of 0x10FFFF. By default these are considered regular code points, but certain situations warrant special handling for them, which can be specified using the <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter. If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> contains <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_ILLEGAL_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code>, all three classes are treated as malformations and handled as such. The flags <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_SURROGATE&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_NONCHAR&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_SUPER&quot;</code> (meaning above the legal Unicode maximum) can be set to disallow these categories individually. <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_ILLEGAL_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code> restricts the allowed inputs to the strict UTF-8 traditionally defined by Unicode. Use <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_ILLEGAL_C9_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code> to use the strictness definition given by Unicode Corrigendum #9 &lt;<a rel="nofollow" href="http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html">http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html</a>&gt;. The difference between traditional strictness and C9 strictness is that the latter does not forbid non-character code points. (They are still discouraged, however.) For more discussion see ``Noncharacter code points&#8217;&#8216; in perlunicode. </p>
<p>The flags <code>&quot;UTF8_WARN_ILLEGAL_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;UTF8_WARN_ILLEGAL_C9_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;UTF8_WARN_SURROGATE&quot;</code>, <code>&quot;UTF8_WARN_NONCHAR&quot;</code>, and <code>&quot;UTF8_WARN_SUPER&quot;</code> will cause warning messages to be raised for their respective categories, but otherwise the code points are considered valid (not malformations). To get a category to both be treated as a malformation and raise a warning, specify both the WARN and DISALLOW flags. (But note that warnings are not raised if lexically disabled nor if <code>&quot;UTF8_CHECK_ONLY&quot;</code> is also specified.) </p>
<p>Extremely high code points were never specified in any standard, and require an extension to UTF-8 to express, which Perl does. It is likely that programs written in something other than Perl would not be able to read files that contain these; nor would Perl understand files written by something that uses a different extension. For these reasons, there is a separate set of flags that can warn and/or disallow these extremely high code points, even if other above-Unicode ones are accepted. They are the <code>&quot;UTF8_WARN_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code> flags. For more information see &quot;<code>&quot;UTF8_GOT_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code>&quot;. Of course <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_SUPER&quot;</code> will treat all above-Unicode code points, including these, as malformations. (Note that the Unicode standard considers anything above 0x10FFFF to be illegal, but there are standards predating it that allow up to 0x7FFF_FFFF (2**31 -1)) </p>
<p>A somewhat misleadingly named synonym for <code>&quot;UTF8_WARN_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code> is retained for backward compatibility: <code>&quot;UTF8_WARN_ABOVE_31_BIT&quot;</code>. Similarly, <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_ABOVE_31_BIT&quot;</code> is usable instead of the more accurately named <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code>. The names are misleading because these flags can apply to code points that actually do fit in 31 bits. This happens on EBCDIC platforms, and sometimes when the overlong malformation is also present. The new names accurately describe the situation in all cases. </p>
<p>All other code points corresponding to Unicode characters, including private use and those yet to be assigned, are never considered malformed and never warn. </p>
<pre><code>        UV utf8n_to_uvchr(const U8 *s, STRLEN curlen,
                               STRLEN *retlen, const U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8n_to_uvchr_error</dt>
<dd> <p> THIS FUNCTION SHOULD BE USED IN ONLY VERY SPECIALIZED CIRCUMSTANCES. Most code should use ``utf8_to_uvchr_buf&#8217;&#8216;() rather than call this directly. </p>
<p>This function is for code that needs to know what the precise malformation(s) are when an error is found. If you also need to know the generated warning messages, use ``utf8n_to_uvchr_msgs&#8217;&#8216;() instead. </p>
<p>It is like <code>&quot;utf8n_to_uvchr&quot;</code> but it takes an extra parameter placed after all the others, <code>&quot;errors&quot;</code>. If this parameter is 0, this function behaves identically to <code>&quot;utf8n_to_uvchr&quot;</code>. Otherwise, <code>&quot;errors&quot;</code> should be a pointer to a <code>&quot;U32&quot;</code> variable, which this function sets to indicate any errors found. Upon return, if <code>*errors</code> is 0, there were no errors found. Otherwise, <code>*errors</code> is the bit-wise <code>&quot;OR&quot;</code> of the bits described in the list below. Some of these bits will be set if a malformation is found, even if the input <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter indicates that the given malformation is allowed; those exceptions are noted: </p>
 </dd>
<dl>
<dd>  </dd>
<dl>
<dt>&quot;UTF8_GOT_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The input sequence is not standard UTF-8, but a Perl extension. This bit is set only if the input <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter contains either the <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code> or the <code>&quot;UTF8_WARN_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code> flags. </p>
<p>Code points above 0x7FFF_FFFF (2**31 &#8211; 1) were never specified in any standard, and so some extension must be used to express them. Perl uses a natural extension to UTF-8 to represent the ones up to 2**36-1, and invented a further extension to represent even higher ones, so that any code point that fits in a 64-bit word can be represented. Text using these extensions is not likely to be portable to non-Perl code. We lump both of these extensions together and refer to them as Perl extended UTF-8. There exist other extensions that people have invented, incompatible with Perl&#8217;s. </p>
<p>On EBCDIC platforms starting in Perl v5.24, the Perl extension for representing extremely high code points kicks in at 0x3FFF_FFFF (2**30 -1), which is lower than on ASCII. Prior to that, code points 2**31 and higher were simply unrepresentable, and a different, incompatible method was used to represent code points between 2**30 and 2**31 - 1. </p>
<p>On both platforms, ASCII and EBCDIC, <code>&quot;UTF8_GOT_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code> is set if Perl extended UTF-8 is used. </p>
<p>In earlier Perls, this bit was named <code>&quot;UTF8_GOT_ABOVE_31_BIT&quot;</code>, which you still may use for backward compatibility. That name is misleading, as this flag may be set when the code point actually does fit in 31 bits. This happens on EBCDIC platforms, and sometimes when the overlong malformation is also present. The new name accurately describes the situation in all cases. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&quot;UTF8_GOT_CONTINUATION&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The input sequence was malformed in that the first byte was a a UTF-8 continuation byte. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&quot;UTF8_GOT_EMPTY&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The input <code>&quot;curlen&quot;</code> parameter was 0. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&quot;UTF8_GOT_LONG&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The input sequence was malformed in that there is some other sequence that evaluates to the same code point, but that sequence is shorter than this one. </p>
<p>Until Unicode 3.1, it was legal for programs to accept this malformation, but it was discovered that this created security issues. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&quot;UTF8_GOT_NONCHAR&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The code point represented by the input UTF-8 sequence is for a Unicode non-character code point. This bit is set only if the input <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter contains either the <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_NONCHAR&quot;</code> or the <code>&quot;UTF8_WARN_NONCHAR&quot;</code> flags. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&quot;UTF8_GOT_NON_CONTINUATION&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The input sequence was malformed in that a non-continuation type byte was found in a position where only a continuation type one should be. See also &quot;<code>&quot;UTF8_GOT_SHORT&quot;</code>&quot;. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&quot;UTF8_GOT_OVERFLOW&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The input sequence was malformed in that it is for a code point that is not representable in the number of bits available in an IV on the current platform. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&quot;UTF8_GOT_SHORT&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The input sequence was malformed in that <code>&quot;curlen&quot;</code> is smaller than required for a complete sequence. In other words, the input is for a partial character sequence. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;UTF8_GOT_SHORT&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;UTF8_GOT_NON_CONTINUATION&quot;</code> both indicate a too short sequence. The difference is that <code>&quot;UTF8_GOT_NON_CONTINUATION&quot;</code> indicates always that there is an error, while <code>&quot;UTF8_GOT_SHORT&quot;</code> means that an incomplete sequence was looked at. If no other flags are present, it means that the sequence was valid as far as it went. Depending on the application, this could mean one of three things: </p>
 </dd>
<dl>
<dd>  </dd>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</dt>
<dd> <p> The <code>&quot;curlen&quot;</code> length parameter passed in was too small, and the function was prevented from examining all the necessary bytes. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&bull;</dt>
<dd> <p> The buffer being looked at is based on reading data, and the data received so far stopped in the middle of a character, so that the next read will read the remainder of this character. (It is up to the caller to deal with the split bytes somehow.) </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&bull;</dt>
<dd> <p> This is a real error, and the partial sequence is all we&#8217;re going to get. </p>
 </dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dd>  </dd></dl>
<dt>&quot;UTF8_GOT_SUPER&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The input sequence was malformed in that it is for a non-Unicode code point; that is, one above the legal Unicode maximum. This bit is set only if the input <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter contains either the <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_SUPER&quot;</code> or the <code>&quot;UTF8_WARN_SUPER&quot;</code> flags. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&quot;UTF8_GOT_SURROGATE&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The input sequence was malformed in that it is for a -Unicode UTF-16 surrogate code point. This bit is set only if the input <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> parameter contains either the <code>&quot;UTF8_DISALLOW_SURROGATE&quot;</code> or the <code>&quot;UTF8_WARN_SURROGATE&quot;</code> flags. </p>
 </dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dd> <p>To do your own error handling, call this function with the <code>&quot;UTF8_CHECK_ONLY&quot;</code> flag to suppress any warnings, and then examine the <code>*errors</code> return. </p>
<pre><code>        UV utf8n_to_uvchr_error(const U8 *s, STRLEN curlen,
                                     STRLEN *retlen,
                                     const U32 flags,
                                     U32 * errors)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<dt>utf8n_to_uvchr_msgs</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p> THIS FUNCTION SHOULD BE USED IN ONLY VERY SPECIALIZED CIRCUMSTANCES. Most code should use ``utf8_to_uvchr_buf&#8217;&#8216;() rather than call this directly. </p>
<p>This function is for code that needs to know what the precise malformation(s) are when an error is found, and wants the corresponding warning and/or error messages to be returned to the caller rather than be displayed. All messages that would have been displayed if all lexcial warnings are enabled will be returned. </p>
<p>It is just like <code>&quot;utf8n_to_uvchr_error&quot;</code> but it takes an extra parameter placed after all the others, <code>&quot;msgs&quot;</code>. If this parameter is 0, this function behaves identically to <code>&quot;utf8n_to_uvchr_error&quot;</code>. Otherwise, <code>&quot;msgs&quot;</code> should be a pointer to an <code>&quot;AV *&quot;</code> variable, in which this function creates a new AV to contain any appropriate messages. The elements of the array are ordered so that the first message that would have been displayed is in the 0th element, and so on. Each element is a hash with three key-value pairs, as follows: </p>
 </dd>
<dl>
<dd>  </dd>
<dl>
<dt>&quot;text&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The text of the message as a <code>&quot;SVpv&quot;</code>. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&quot;warn_categories&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The warning category (or categories) packed into a <code>&quot;SVuv&quot;</code>. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&quot;flag&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> A single flag bit associated with this message, in a <code>&quot;SVuv&quot;</code>. The bit corresponds to some bit in the <code>*errors</code> return value, such as <code>&quot;UTF8_GOT_LONG&quot;</code>. </p>
 </dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dd> <p>It&#8217;s important to note that specifying this parameter as non-null will cause any warnings this function would otherwise generate to be suppressed, and instead be placed in <code>*msgs</code>. The caller can check the lexical warnings state (or not) when choosing what to do with the returned messages. </p>
<p>If the flag <code>&quot;UTF8_CHECK_ONLY&quot;</code> is passed, no warnings are generated, and hence no AV is created. </p>
<p>The caller, of course, is responsible for freeing any returned AV. </p>
<pre><code>        UV utf8n_to_uvchr_msgs(const U8 *s, STRLEN curlen,
                                    STRLEN *retlen,
                                    const U32 flags,
                                    U32 * errors, AV ** msgs)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<dt>utf8n_to_uvuni</dt>
<dd> <p> Instead use ``utf8_to_uvchr_buf&#8217;&#8216;, or rarely, ``utf8n_to_uvchr&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>This function was useful for code that wanted to handle both EBCDIC and ASCII platforms with Unicode properties, but starting in Perl v5.20, the distinctions between the platforms have mostly been made invisible to most code, so this function is quite unlikely to be what you want. If you do need this precise functionality, use instead <code>&quot;NATIVE_TO_UNI(utf8_to_uvchr_buf(...))&quot;</code> or <code>&quot;NATIVE_TO_UNI(utf8n_to_uvchr(...))&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        UV utf8n_to_uvuni(const U8 *s, STRLEN curlen,
                               STRLEN *retlen, U32 flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>UTF8SKIP</dt>
<dd> <p> returns the number of bytes in the UTF-8 encoded character whose first (perhaps only) byte is pointed to by <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN UTF8SKIP(char* s)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8_distance</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the number of UTF-8 characters between the UTF-8 pointers <code>&quot;a&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;b&quot;</code>. </p>
<p> WARNING: use only if you *know* that the pointers point inside the same UTF-8 buffer. </p>
<pre><code>        IV utf8_distance(const U8 *a, const U8 *b)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8_hop</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the UTF-8 pointer <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> displaced by <code>&quot;off&quot;</code> characters, either forward or backward. </p>
<p> WARNING: do not use the following unless you *know* <code>&quot;off&quot;</code> is within the UTF-8 data pointed to by <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> *and* that on entry <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> is aligned on the first byte of character or just after the last byte of a character. </p>
<pre><code>        U8* utf8_hop(const U8 *s, SSize_t off)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8_hop_back</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the UTF-8 pointer <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> displaced by up to <code>&quot;off&quot;</code> characters, backward. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;off&quot;</code> must be non-positive. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> must be after or equal to <code>&quot;start&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>When moving backward it will not move before <code>&quot;start&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Will not exceed this limit even if the string is not valid ``UTF-8&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        U8* utf8_hop_back(const U8 *s, SSize_t off,
                              const U8 *start)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8_hop_forward</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the UTF-8 pointer <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> displaced by up to <code>&quot;off&quot;</code> characters, forward. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;off&quot;</code> must be non-negative. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> must be before or equal to <code>&quot;end&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>When moving forward it will not move beyond <code>&quot;end&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Will not exceed this limit even if the string is not valid ``UTF-8&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        U8* utf8_hop_forward(const U8 *s, SSize_t off,
                                 const U8 *end)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8_hop_safe</dt>
<dd> <p> Return the UTF-8 pointer <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> displaced by up to <code>&quot;off&quot;</code> characters, either forward or backward. </p>
<p>When moving backward it will not move before <code>&quot;start&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>When moving forward it will not move beyond <code>&quot;end&quot;</code>. </p>
<p>Will not exceed those limits even if the string is not valid ``UTF-8&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        U8* utf8_hop_safe(const U8 *s, SSize_t off,
                              const U8 *start, const U8 *end)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>UTF8_IS_INVARIANT</dt>
<dd> <p> Evaluates to 1 if the byte <code>&quot;c&quot;</code> represents the same character when encoded in UTF-8 as when not; otherwise evaluates to 0. UTF-8 invariant characters can be copied as-is when converting to/from UTF-8, saving time. </p>
<p>In spite of the name, this macro gives the correct result if the input string from which <code>&quot;c&quot;</code> comes is not encoded in UTF-8. </p>
<p>See <code>&quot;UVCHR_IS_INVARIANT&quot;</code> for checking if a UV is invariant. </p>
<pre><code>        bool UTF8_IS_INVARIANT(char c)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>UTF8_IS_NONCHAR</dt>
<dd> <p> Evaluates to non-zero if the first few bytes of the string starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> and looking no further than <code>&quot;e - 1&quot;</code> are well-formed UTF-8 that represents one of the Unicode non-character code points; otherwise it evaluates to 0. If non-zero, the value gives how many bytes starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> comprise the code point&#8217;s representation. </p>
<pre><code>        bool UTF8_IS_NONCHAR(const U8 *s, const U8 *e)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>UTF8_IS_SUPER</dt>
<dd> <p> Recall that Perl recognizes an extension to UTF-8 that can encode code points larger than the ones defined by Unicode, which are 0..0x10FFFF. </p>
<p>This macro evaluates to non-zero if the first few bytes of the string starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> and looking no further than <code>&quot;e - 1&quot;</code> are from this UTF-8 extension; otherwise it evaluates to 0. If non-zero, the value gives how many bytes starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> comprise the code point&#8217;s representation. </p>
<p>0 is returned if the bytes are not well-formed extended UTF-8, or if they represent a code point that cannot fit in a UV on the current platform. Hence this macro can give different results when run on a 64-bit word machine than on one with a 32-bit word size. </p>
<p>Note that it is illegal to have code points that are larger than what can fit in an IV on the current machine. </p>
<pre><code>        bool UTF8_IS_SUPER(const U8 *s, const U8 *e)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>UTF8_IS_SURROGATE</dt>
<dd> <p> Evaluates to non-zero if the first few bytes of the string starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> and looking no further than <code>&quot;e - 1&quot;</code> are well-formed UTF-8 that represents one of the Unicode surrogate code points; otherwise it evaluates to 0. If non-zero, the value gives how many bytes starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> comprise the code point&#8217;s representation. </p>
<pre><code>        bool UTF8_IS_SURROGATE(const U8 *s, const U8 *e)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8_length</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the number of characters in the sequence of UTF-8-encoded bytes starting at <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> and ending at the byte just before <code>&quot;e&quot;</code>. If &lt;s&gt; and &lt;e&gt; point to the same place, it returns 0 with no warning raised. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;e &lt; s&quot;</code> or if the scan would end up past <code>&quot;e&quot;</code>, it raises a UTF8 warning and returns the number of valid characters. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN utf8_length(const U8* s, const U8 *e)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>UTF8_SAFE_SKIP</dt>
<dd> <p> returns 0 if <code>&quot;s &gt;= e&quot;</code>; otherwise returns the number of bytes in the UTF-8 encoded character whose first byte is pointed to by <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>. But it never returns beyond <code>&quot;e&quot;</code>. On DEBUGGING builds, it asserts that <code>&quot;s &lt;= e&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN UTF8_SAFE_SKIP(char* s, char* e)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8_to_bytes</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p>Converts a string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> of length <code>*lenp</code> from UTF-8 into native byte encoding. Unlike ``bytes_to_utf8&#8217;&#8216;, this over-writes the original string, and updates <code>*lenp</code> to contain the new length. Returns zero on failure (leaving <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> unchanged) setting <code>*lenp</code> to -1. </p>
<p>Upon successful return, the number of variants in the string can be computed by having saved the value of <code>*lenp</code> before the call, and subtracting the after-call value of <code>*lenp</code> from it. </p>
<p>If you need a copy of the string, see ``bytes_from_utf8&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        U8* utf8_to_bytes(U8 *s, STRLEN *lenp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8_to_uvchr</dt>
<dd> <p> DEPRECATED! It is planned to remove this function from a future release of Perl. Do not use it for new code; remove it from existing code. </p>
<p>Returns the native code point of the first character in the string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> which is assumed to be in UTF-8 encoding; <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> will be set to the length, in bytes, of that character. </p>
<p>Some, but not all, UTF-8 malformations are detected, and in fact, some malformed input could cause reading beyond the end of the input buffer, which is why this function is deprecated. Use ``utf8_to_uvchr_buf&#8217;&#8216; instead. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> points to one of the detected malformations, and UTF8 warnings are enabled, zero is returned and <code>*retlen</code> is set (if <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> isn&#8217;t <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>) to -1. If those warnings are off, the computed value if well-defined (or the Unicode REPLACEMENT CHARACTER, if not) is silently returned, and <code>*retlen</code> is set (if <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> isn&#8217;t NULL) so that (<code>&quot;s&quot;</code> + <code>*retlen</code>) is the next possible position in <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> that could begin a non-malformed character. See ``utf8n_to_uvchr&#8217;&#8216; for details on when the REPLACEMENT CHARACTER is returned. </p>
<pre><code>        UV utf8_to_uvchr(const U8 *s, STRLEN *retlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8_to_uvchr_buf</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns the native code point of the first character in the string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> which is assumed to be in UTF-8 encoding; <code>&quot;send&quot;</code> points to 1 beyond the end of <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>. <code>*retlen</code> will be set to the length, in bytes, of that character. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> does not point to a well-formed UTF-8 character and UTF8 warnings are enabled, zero is returned and <code>*retlen</code> is set (if <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> isn&#8217;t <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>) to -1. If those warnings are off, the computed value, if well-defined (or the Unicode REPLACEMENT CHARACTER if not), is silently returned, and <code>*retlen</code> is set (if <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> isn&#8217;t <code>&quot;NULL&quot;</code>) so that (<code>&quot;s&quot;</code> + <code>*retlen</code>) is the next possible position in <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> that could begin a non-malformed character. See ``utf8n_to_uvchr&#8217;&#8216; for details on when the REPLACEMENT CHARACTER is returned. </p>
<pre><code>        UV utf8_to_uvchr_buf(const U8 *s, const U8 *send,
                                  STRLEN *retlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>utf8_to_uvuni_buf</dt>
<dd> <p> DEPRECATED! It is planned to remove this function from a future release of Perl. Do not use it for new code; remove it from existing code. </p>
<p>Only in very rare circumstances should code need to be dealing in Unicode (as opposed to native) code points. In those few cases, use <code>&quot;NATIVE_TO_UNI(utf8_to_uvchr_buf(...))&quot;</code> instead. If you are not absolutely sure this is one of those cases, then assume it isn&#8217;t and use plain <code>&quot;utf8_to_uvchr_buf&quot;</code> instead. </p>
<p>Returns the Unicode (not-native) code point of the first character in the string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> which is assumed to be in UTF-8 encoding; <code>&quot;send&quot;</code> points to 1 beyond the end of <code>&quot;s&quot;</code>. <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> will be set to the length, in bytes, of that character. </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> does not point to a well-formed UTF-8 character and UTF8 warnings are enabled, zero is returned and <code>*retlen</code> is set (if <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> isn&#8217;t NULL) to -1. If those warnings are off, the computed value if well-defined (or the Unicode REPLACEMENT CHARACTER, if not) is silently returned, and <code>*retlen</code> is set (if <code>&quot;retlen&quot;</code> isn&#8217;t NULL) so that (<code>&quot;s&quot;</code> + <code>*retlen</code>) is the next possible position in <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> that could begin a non-malformed character. See ``utf8n_to_uvchr&#8217;&#8216; for details on when the REPLACEMENT CHARACTER is returned. </p>
<pre><code>        UV utf8_to_uvuni_buf(const U8 *s, const U8 *send,
                                  STRLEN *retlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>UVCHR_IS_INVARIANT</dt>
<dd> <p> Evaluates to 1 if the representation of code point <code>&quot;cp&quot;</code> is the same whether or not it is encoded in UTF-8; otherwise evaluates to 0. UTF-8 invariant characters can be copied as-is when converting to/from UTF-8, saving time. <code>&quot;cp&quot;</code> is Unicode if above 255; otherwise is platform-native. </p>
<pre><code>        bool UVCHR_IS_INVARIANT(UV cp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>UVCHR_SKIP</dt>
<dd> <p> returns the number of bytes required to represent the code point <code>&quot;cp&quot;</code> when encoded as UTF-8. <code>&quot;cp&quot;</code> is a native (ASCII or EBCDIC) code point if less than 255; a Unicode code point otherwise. </p>
<pre><code>        STRLEN UVCHR_SKIP(UV cp)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>uvchr_to_utf8</dt>
<dd> <p> Adds the UTF-8 representation of the native code point <code>&quot;uv&quot;</code> to the end of the string <code>&quot;d&quot;</code>; <code>&quot;d&quot;</code> should have at least <code>&quot;UVCHR_SKIP(uv)+1&quot;</code> (up to <code>&quot;UTF8_MAXBYTES+1&quot;</code>) free bytes available. The return value is the pointer to the byte after the end of the new character. In other words, </p>
<pre><code>    d = uvchr_to_utf8(d, uv);
</code></pre>
<p>is the recommended wide native character-aware way of saying </p>
<pre><code>    *(d++) = uv;
</code></pre>
<p>This function accepts any code point from 0..<code>&quot;IV_MAX&quot;</code> as input. <code>&quot;IV_MAX&quot;</code> is typically 0x7FFF_FFFF in a 32-bit word. </p>
<p>It is possible to forbid or warn on non-Unicode code points, or those that may be problematic by using ``uvchr_to_utf8_flags&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        U8* uvchr_to_utf8(U8 *d, UV uv)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>uvchr_to_utf8_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Adds the UTF-8 representation of the native code point <code>&quot;uv&quot;</code> to the end of the string <code>&quot;d&quot;</code>; <code>&quot;d&quot;</code> should have at least <code>&quot;UVCHR_SKIP(uv)+1&quot;</code> (up to <code>&quot;UTF8_MAXBYTES+1&quot;</code>) free bytes available. The return value is the pointer to the byte after the end of the new character. In other words, </p>
<pre><code>    d = uvchr_to_utf8_flags(d, uv, flags);
</code></pre>
<p>or, in most cases, </p>
<pre><code>    d = uvchr_to_utf8_flags(d, uv, 0);
</code></pre>
<p>This is the Unicode-aware way of saying </p>
<pre><code>    *(d++) = uv;
</code></pre>
<p>If <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> is 0, this function accepts any code point from 0..<code>&quot;IV_MAX&quot;</code> as input. <code>&quot;IV_MAX&quot;</code> is typically 0x7FFF_FFFF in a 32-bit word. </p>
<p>Specifying <code>&quot;flags&quot;</code> can further restrict what is allowed and not warned on, as follows: </p>
<p>If <code>&quot;uv&quot;</code> is a Unicode surrogate code point and <code>&quot;UNICODE_WARN_SURROGATE&quot;</code> is set, the function will raise a warning, provided UTF8 warnings are enabled. If instead <code>&quot;UNICODE_DISALLOW_SURROGATE&quot;</code> is set, the function will fail and return NULL. If both flags are set, the function will both warn and return NULL. </p>
<p>Similarly, the <code>&quot;UNICODE_WARN_NONCHAR&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;UNICODE_DISALLOW_NONCHAR&quot;</code> flags affect how the function handles a Unicode non-character. </p>
<p>And likewise, the <code>&quot;UNICODE_WARN_SUPER&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;UNICODE_DISALLOW_SUPER&quot;</code> flags affect the handling of code points that are above the Unicode maximum of 0x10FFFF. Languages other than Perl may not be able to accept files that contain these. </p>
<p>The flag <code>&quot;UNICODE_WARN_ILLEGAL_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code> selects all three of the above WARN flags; and <code>&quot;UNICODE_DISALLOW_ILLEGAL_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code> selects all three DISALLOW flags. <code>&quot;UNICODE_DISALLOW_ILLEGAL_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code> restricts the allowed inputs to the strict UTF-8 traditionally defined by Unicode. Similarly, <code>&quot;UNICODE_WARN_ILLEGAL_C9_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;UNICODE_DISALLOW_ILLEGAL_C9_INTERCHANGE&quot;</code> are shortcuts to select the above-Unicode and surrogate flags, but not the non-character ones, as defined in Unicode Corrigendum #9 &lt;<a rel="nofollow" href="http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html">http://www.unicode.org/versions/corrigendum9.html</a>&gt;. See ``Noncharacter code points&#8217;&#8216; in perlunicode. </p>
<p>Extremely high code points were never specified in any standard, and require an extension to UTF-8 to express, which Perl does. It is likely that programs written in something other than Perl would not be able to read files that contain these; nor would Perl understand files written by something that uses a different extension. For these reasons, there is a separate set of flags that can warn and/or disallow these extremely high code points, even if other above-Unicode ones are accepted. They are the <code>&quot;UNICODE_WARN_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;UNICODE_DISALLOW_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code> flags. For more information see &quot;<code>&quot;UTF8_GOT_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code>&quot;. Of course <code>&quot;UNICODE_DISALLOW_SUPER&quot;</code> will treat all above-Unicode code points, including these, as malformations. (Note that the Unicode standard considers anything above 0x10FFFF to be illegal, but there are standards predating it that allow up to 0x7FFF_FFFF (2**31 -1)) </p>
<p>A somewhat misleadingly named synonym for <code>&quot;UNICODE_WARN_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code> is retained for backward compatibility: <code>&quot;UNICODE_WARN_ABOVE_31_BIT&quot;</code>. Similarly, <code>&quot;UNICODE_DISALLOW_ABOVE_31_BIT&quot;</code> is usable instead of the more accurately named <code>&quot;UNICODE_DISALLOW_PERL_EXTENDED&quot;</code>. The names are misleading because on EBCDIC platforms,these flags can apply to code points that actually do fit in 31 bits. The new names accurately describe the situation in all cases. </p>
<pre><code>        U8* uvchr_to_utf8_flags(U8 *d, UV uv, UV flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>uvchr_to_utf8_flags_msgs</dt>
<dd> <p> NOTE: this function is experimental and may change or be removed without notice. </p>
<p> THIS FUNCTION SHOULD BE USED IN ONLY VERY SPECIALIZED CIRCUMSTANCES. </p>
<p>Most code should use <code>&quot;&quot;uvchr_to_utf8_flags&quot;()&quot;</code> rather than call this directly. </p>
<p>This function is for code that wants any warning and/or error messages to be returned to the caller rather than be displayed. All messages that would have been displayed if all lexical warnings are enabled will be returned. </p>
<p>It is just like <code>&quot;uvchr_to_utf8_flags&quot;</code> but it takes an extra parameter placed after all the others, <code>&quot;msgs&quot;</code>. If this parameter is 0, this function behaves identically to <code>&quot;uvchr_to_utf8_flags&quot;</code>. Otherwise, <code>&quot;msgs&quot;</code> should be a pointer to an <code>&quot;HV *&quot;</code> variable, in which this function creates a new HV to contain any appropriate messages. The hash has three key-value pairs, as follows: </p>
 </dd>
<dl>
<dd>  </dd>
<dl>
<dt>&quot;text&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The text of the message as a <code>&quot;SVpv&quot;</code>. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&quot;warn_categories&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> The warning category (or categories) packed into a <code>&quot;SVuv&quot;</code>. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>&quot;flag&quot;</dt>
<dd> <p> A single flag bit associated with this message, in a <code>&quot;SVuv&quot;</code>. The bit corresponds to some bit in the <code>*errors</code> return value, such as <code>&quot;UNICODE_GOT_SURROGATE&quot;</code>. </p>
 </dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dd> <p>It&#8217;s important to note that specifying this parameter as non-null will cause any warnings this function would otherwise generate to be suppressed, and instead be placed in <code>*msgs</code>. The caller can check the lexical warnings state (or not) when choosing what to do with the returned messages. </p>
<p>The caller, of course, is responsible for freeing any returned HV. </p>
<pre><code>        U8* uvchr_to_utf8_flags_msgs(U8 *d, UV uv, UV flags,
                                         HV ** msgs)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<dt>uvoffuni_to_utf8_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> THIS FUNCTION SHOULD BE USED IN ONLY VERY SPECIALIZED CIRCUMSTANCES. Instead, <code>Almost all code should use ``uvchr_to_utf8&#8217;&#8216; or ``uvchr_to_utf8_flags&#8217;&#8216;</code>. </p>
<p>This function is like them, but the input is a strict Unicode (as opposed to native) code point. Only in very rare circumstances should code not be using the native code point. </p>
<p>For details, see the description for ``uvchr_to_utf8_flags&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<pre><code>        U8* uvoffuni_to_utf8_flags(U8 *d, UV uv,
                                       const UV flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>uvuni_to_utf8_flags</dt>
<dd> <p> Instead you almost certainly want to use ``uvchr_to_utf8&#8217;&#8216; or ``uvchr_to_utf8_flags&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>This function is a deprecated synonym for ``uvoffuni_to_utf8_flags&#8217;&#8216;, which itself, while not deprecated, should be used only in isolated circumstances. These functions were useful for code that wanted to handle both EBCDIC and ASCII platforms with Unicode properties, but starting in Perl v5.20, the distinctions between the platforms have mostly been made invisible to most code, so this function is quite unlikely to be what you want. </p>
<pre><code>        U8* uvuni_to_utf8_flags(U8 *d, UV uv, UV flags)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>valid_utf8_to_uvchr</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;utf8_to_uvchr_buf&quot;</code>, but should only be called when it is known that the next character in the input UTF-8 string <code>&quot;s&quot;</code> is well-formed (<i>e.g.</i>, it passes <code>&quot;isUTF8_CHAR&quot;</code>. Surrogates, non-character code points, and non-Unicode code points are allowed. </p>
<pre><code>        UV valid_utf8_to_uvchr(const U8 *s, STRLEN *retlen)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Variables created by xsubpp and xsubpp internal functions</h2>
<dl>
<dt>newXSproto</dt>
<dd> <p> Used by <code>&quot;xsubpp&quot;</code> to hook up XSUBs as Perl subs. Adds Perl prototypes to the subs. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>XS_APIVERSION_BOOTCHECK</dt>
<dd> <p> Macro to verify that the perl api version an XS module has been compiled against matches the api version of the perl interpreter it&#8217;s being loaded into. </p>
<pre><code>                XS_APIVERSION_BOOTCHECK;
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>XS_VERSION</dt>
<dd> <p> The version identifier for an XS module. This is usually handled automatically by <code>&quot;ExtUtils::MakeMaker&quot;</code>. See <code>&quot;XS_VERSION_BOOTCHECK&quot;</code>. </p>
 </dd>
<dt>XS_VERSION_BOOTCHECK</dt>
<dd> <p> Macro to verify that a PM module&#8217;s <code>$VERSION</code> variable matches the XS module&#8217;s <code>&quot;XS_VERSION&quot;</code> variable. This is usually handled automatically by <code>&quot;xsubpp&quot;</code>. See ``The VERSIONCHECK: Keyword&#8217;&#8216; in perlxs. </p>
<pre><code>                XS_VERSION_BOOTCHECK;
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Warning and Dieing</h2>
<dl>
<dt>ckWARN</dt>
<dd> <p> Returns a boolean as to whether or not warnings are enabled for the warning category <code>&quot;w&quot;</code>. If the category is by default enabled even if not within the scope of <code>&quot;use warnings&quot;</code>, instead use the ``ckWARN_d&#8217;&#8216; macro. </p>
<pre><code>        bool ckWARN(U32 w)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ckWARN2</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;ckWARN&quot;</code>, but takes two warnings categories as input, and returns TRUE if either is enabled. If either category is by default enabled even if not within the scope of <code>&quot;use warnings&quot;</code>, instead use the ``ckWARN2_d&#8217;&#8216; macro. The categories must be completely independent, one may not be subclassed from the other. </p>
<pre><code>        bool ckWARN2(U32 w1, U32 w2)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ckWARN3</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;ckWARN2&quot;</code>, but takes three warnings categories as input, and returns TRUE if any is enabled. If any of the categories is by default enabled even if not within the scope of <code>&quot;use warnings&quot;</code>, instead use the ``ckWARN3_d&#8217;&#8216; macro. The categories must be completely independent, one may not be subclassed from any other. </p>
<pre><code>        bool ckWARN3(U32 w1, U32 w2, U32 w3)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ckWARN4</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;ckWARN3&quot;</code>, but takes four warnings categories as input, and returns TRUE if any is enabled. If any of the categories is by default enabled even if not within the scope of <code>&quot;use warnings&quot;</code>, instead use the ``ckWARN4_d&#8217;&#8216; macro. The categories must be completely independent, one may not be subclassed from any other. </p>
<pre><code>        bool ckWARN4(U32 w1, U32 w2, U32 w3, U32 w4)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ckWARN_d</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;ckWARN&quot;</code>, but for use if and only if the warning category is by default enabled even if not within the scope of <code>&quot;use warnings&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool ckWARN_d(U32 w)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ckWARN2_d</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;ckWARN2&quot;</code>, but for use if and only if either warning category is by default enabled even if not within the scope of <code>&quot;use warnings&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool ckWARN2_d(U32 w1, U32 w2)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ckWARN3_d</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;ckWARN3&quot;</code>, but for use if and only if any of the warning categories is by default enabled even if not within the scope of <code>&quot;use warnings&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool ckWARN3_d(U32 w1, U32 w2, U32 w3)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>ckWARN4_d</dt>
<dd> <p> Like <code>&quot;ckWARN4&quot;</code>, but for use if and only if any of the warning categories is by default enabled even if not within the scope of <code>&quot;use warnings&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        bool ckWARN4_d(U32 w1, U32 w2, U32 w3, U32 w4)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>croak</dt>
<dd> <p> This is an XS interface to Perl&#8217;s <code>&quot;die&quot;</code> function. </p>
<p>Take a sprintf-style format pattern and argument list. These are used to generate a string message. If the message does not end with a newline, then it will be extended with some indication of the current location in the code, as described for ``mess_sv&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>The error message will be used as an exception, by default returning control to the nearest enclosing <code>&quot;eval&quot;</code>, but subject to modification by a <code>$SIG{__DIE__}</code> handler. In any case, the <code>&quot;croak&quot;</code> function never returns normally. </p>
<p>For historical reasons, if <code>&quot;pat&quot;</code> is null then the contents of <code>&quot;ERRSV&quot;</code> (<code>$@</code>) will be used as an error message or object instead of building an error message from arguments. If you want to throw a non-string object, or build an error message in an SV yourself, it is preferable to use the ``croak_sv&#8217;&#8216; function, which does not involve clobbering <code>&quot;ERRSV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void croak(const char *pat, ...)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>croak_no_modify</dt>
<dd> <p> Exactly equivalent to <code>&quot;Perl_croak(aTHX_ &quot;%s&quot;, PL_no_modify)&quot;</code>, but generates terser object code than using <code>&quot;Perl_croak&quot;</code>. Less code used on exception code paths reduces CPU cache pressure. </p>
<pre><code>        void croak_no_modify()
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>croak_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> This is an XS interface to Perl&#8217;s <code>&quot;die&quot;</code> function. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;baseex&quot;</code> is the error message or object. If it is a reference, it will be used as-is. Otherwise it is used as a string, and if it does not end with a newline then it will be extended with some indication of the current location in the code, as described for ``mess_sv&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>The error message or object will be used as an exception, by default returning control to the nearest enclosing <code>&quot;eval&quot;</code>, but subject to modification by a <code>$SIG{__DIE__}</code> handler. In any case, the <code>&quot;croak_sv&quot;</code> function never returns normally. </p>
<p>To die with a simple string message, the ``croak&#8217;&#8216; function may be more convenient. </p>
<pre><code>        void croak_sv(SV *baseex)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>die</dt>
<dd> <p> Behaves the same as ``croak&#8217;&#8216;, except for the return type. It should be used only where the <code>&quot;OP *&quot;</code> return type is required. The function never actually returns. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * die(const char *pat, ...)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>die_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> Behaves the same as ``croak_sv&#8217;&#8216;, except for the return type. It should be used only where the <code>&quot;OP *&quot;</code> return type is required. The function never actually returns. </p>
<pre><code>        OP * die_sv(SV *baseex)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>vcroak</dt>
<dd> <p> This is an XS interface to Perl&#8217;s <code>&quot;die&quot;</code> function. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;pat&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;args&quot;</code> are a sprintf-style format pattern and encapsulated argument list. These are used to generate a string message. If the message does not end with a newline, then it will be extended with some indication of the current location in the code, as described for ``mess_sv&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>The error message will be used as an exception, by default returning control to the nearest enclosing <code>&quot;eval&quot;</code>, but subject to modification by a <code>$SIG{__DIE__}</code> handler. In any case, the <code>&quot;croak&quot;</code> function never returns normally. </p>
<p>For historical reasons, if <code>&quot;pat&quot;</code> is null then the contents of <code>&quot;ERRSV&quot;</code> (<code>$@</code>) will be used as an error message or object instead of building an error message from arguments. If you want to throw a non-string object, or build an error message in an SV yourself, it is preferable to use the ``croak_sv&#8217;&#8216; function, which does not involve clobbering <code>&quot;ERRSV&quot;</code>. </p>
<pre><code>        void vcroak(const char *pat, va_list *args)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>vwarn</dt>
<dd> <p> This is an XS interface to Perl&#8217;s <code>&quot;warn&quot;</code> function. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;pat&quot;</code> and <code>&quot;args&quot;</code> are a sprintf-style format pattern and encapsulated argument list. These are used to generate a string message. If the message does not end with a newline, then it will be extended with some indication of the current location in the code, as described for ``mess_sv&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>The error message or object will by default be written to standard error, but this is subject to modification by a <code>$SIG{__WARN__}</code> handler. </p>
<p>Unlike with ``vcroak&#8217;&#8216;, <code>&quot;pat&quot;</code> is not permitted to be null. </p>
<pre><code>        void vwarn(const char *pat, va_list *args)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>warn</dt>
<dd> <p> This is an XS interface to Perl&#8217;s <code>&quot;warn&quot;</code> function. </p>
<p>Take a sprintf-style format pattern and argument list. These are used to generate a string message. If the message does not end with a newline, then it will be extended with some indication of the current location in the code, as described for ``mess_sv&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>The error message or object will by default be written to standard error, but this is subject to modification by a <code>$SIG{__WARN__}</code> handler. </p>
<p>Unlike with ``croak&#8217;&#8216;, <code>&quot;pat&quot;</code> is not permitted to be null. </p>
<pre><code>        void warn(const char *pat, ...)
</code></pre>
 </dd>
<dt>warn_sv</dt>
<dd> <p> This is an XS interface to Perl&#8217;s <code>&quot;warn&quot;</code> function. </p>
<p> <code>&quot;baseex&quot;</code> is the error message or object. If it is a reference, it will be used as-is. Otherwise it is used as a string, and if it does not end with a newline then it will be extended with some indication of the current location in the code, as described for ``mess_sv&#8217;&#8216;. </p>
<p>The error message or object will by default be written to standard error, but this is subject to modification by a <code>$SIG{__WARN__}</code> handler. </p>
<p>To warn with a simple string message, the ``warn&#8217;&#8216; function may be more convenient. </p>
<pre><code>        void warn_sv(SV *baseex)
</code></pre>
 </dd></dl>
<h2>Undocumented functions</h2>
<p> The following functions have been flagged as part of the public API, but are currently undocumented. Use them at your own risk, as the interfaces are subject to change. Functions that are not listed in this document are not intended for public use, and should NOT be used under any circumstances. </p>
<p>If you feel you need to use one of these functions, first send email to perl5-porters [at] perl.org &lt;mailto:perl5-porters [at] perl.org&gt;. It may be that there is a good reason for the function not being documented, and it should be removed from this list; or it may just be that no one has gotten around to documenting it. In the latter case, you will be asked to submit a patch to document the function. Once your patch is accepted, it will indicate that the interface is stable (unless it is explicitly marked otherwise) and usable by you. </p>
